9 Best Budget Olive Oils, According To Home Cooks

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Olive oil is a highly coveted ingredient due to its rich flavor, versatility, and numerous health benefits. While the price of some olive oils — such as the $50 per bottle olive oil Martha Stewart stands by — might have you convinced this golden liquid is too pricey for your wallet, the truth is that's simply not the case. Home cooks know this precious oil doesn't have to cost an arm and a leg. While they may hold no accolades or Michelin stars, household cooks carry a wealth of knowledge when it comes to which olive oils are best for daily use.

There are plenty of sensational olive oils found at affordable prices, from the mild and mellow-tasting to robust EVOOs bursting with grassy and fruity notes. Whether you're on the hunt for single-sourced EVOO or cold-pressed olive oil, there are plenty of budget-friendly options out there that don't compromise on quality. From those ideal for cooking with to those that add the perfect burst of flavor to finish a dish, it's time to reveal the olive oils that have earned heaps of praise from average home cooks like you and me.

Prices are as of the date of publication and may vary based on region.

Any culinary enthusiast looking to buy extra virgin olive oil in bulk should consider Costco's house brand for a great value cooking oil. Kirkland offers an assortment of budget-friendly olive oils in two and three liter bottles, ensuring home cooks won't be running out of this precious liquid anytime soon.

Kirkland's Organic Extra Virgin Olive Oil is particularly prized by home cooks, with supporters calling its aroma elegant and its flavor distinctive and rich. Others describe it as balanced, smooth, and mild, making it ideal for those seeking a mellower cooking oil. Aspiring chefs love its light flavor and aroma, stating it's not only great for cooking, but for whipping up appetizers, like a classic bruschetta caprese. An olive oil with a pleasing color and thickness, many consider it a great choice for everyday uses given its affordable $10.50 per liter price tag.

However, those looking for a heartier-flavored olive oil may be drawn to try Kirkland's Spanish Extra Virgin Olive Oil, which is equally praised. Those who prepare meals on the daily appreciate its more robust flavor along with the $9.50 per liter cost, making it the most cost-effective of all Kirkland's EVOO offerings. Home cooks find it to possess fruity notes with a pleasant bite. Many, in fact, prefer it over Costco's more expensive Italian EVOO.

Trader Joe's stocks a 32-ounce bottle of Premium Extra Virgin Olive Oil for just $12.49 ($0.39 per ounce), offering up a great choice for those counting their pennies. Crafted from a blend of olives cultivated in Italy, Spain, Argentina, and Greece, this cold-pressed olive oil (a method of making olive oil that includes no heat or chemicals, which many believe creates an oil with more nutrients and health benefits) is one home cooks call great quality for the price. Supporters pronounce it to be wonderful for both cooking and finishing dishes, with one EVOO enthusiast calling it the best tasting offering on the market.

Family chefs appreciate the noticeable aroma of this EVOO, finding it to have a nice finish with a good amount of pungency. Reviewers describe this reasonably priced olive oil as truly delicious, praising the way it cooks. Home cooks declare it to be extremely versatile, being a great choice whether you drizzle it, use it in a fryer, or wield it in a spray bottle. Fans of this budget-friendly oil also appreciate the handy attached spout, making for easy pouring from this hefty bottle.

A bottle that always states where the olives are sourced, you know exactly what you're getting when you purchase this wallet-approved cooking oil. An olive oil that is surprisingly well priced for the amount, Trader Joe's Premium Extra Virgin Olive Oil is one that makes a good, multipurpose olive oil, perfect for the everyday.

Are you drawn to premium olive oils, like Graza, but put off by the high price? Well Trader Joe's might have just the item for you. The internet is calling Trader Joe's Main Squeeze Extra Virgin Olive Oil a great dupe for the more expensive olive oil brand Graza. Equipped with an incredibly handy squeezable bottle, home cooks describe this olive oil as a great cooking olive oil with a nice, grassy flavor.

Undoubtedly, part of this EVOO's appeal is the squeezable container it comes in, which makes it easier to control the amount of oil released. You'll find no messy olive oil running down the side of the bottle post-use or accidentally sloshing too much into the pan, thanks to its adjustable top. Upon purchasing Trader Joe's Main Squeeze Extra Virgin Olive Oil, one home cook notes that this enjoyable olive oil will have you convinced you need to be splashing it on everything, from pizza crusts to pasta and soups. But, the reusable container this EVOO comes in is not its only draw.

This olive oil is cold-pressed, imported from the Mediterranean, and can be delightfully drizzled over everything, from roasted veggies to burrata. Available in a sizable 25.4-ounce bottle for $10.99, this Mediterranean EVOO clocks in at an affordable $0.43 per ounce. Compared to Graza's olive oils, which average around $21 for a similar product, this makes it an alluring buy indeed.

Found in affordable two packs and three packs at Costco, Terra Delyssa continues to be a budget-conscious olive oil beloved by many home cooks. A single-source olive oil crafted from Tunisian olives, one fan describes Terra Delyssa Organic Extra Virgin Olive Oil as smooth and herbaceous, never holding any bitter notes, making it perfect for pan-frying and salad dressings.

Currently offered for $34.99 for a 3-pack of 25.5-ounce bottles at Costco, this EVOO comes out to an enticing $0.46 per ounce. Home cooks love it for three simple reasons: It comes in a glass bottle, boasts a single country origin, and it tastes good. Household cooks praise this high-quality olive oil for its unique and versatile quality, stating it elevates the flavor of any dish you add it to.

The fact that it's non-GMO and USDA organic doesn't hurt either. Even more intriguing, the olives used in Terra Delyssa Organic Extra Virgin Olive Oil are pesticide-free, harvested by hand, and cold-pressed within four hours of picking. The result is an olive oil abundant in antioxidants, low in acidity, and high in polyphenol content. People also love the traceable nature of this EVOO. A quick scan of the QR code on the label lets you trace the story of your olive oil from the orchard to the bottle.

Anyone looking for a single-source olive oil that won't drain their bank account should check out Trader Giotto's Sicilian Selezione Olive Oil. A product we rated the highest in our ranking of Trader Joe's best olive oils, this EVOO is truly one that stands above the rest. Its flavor is exceptionally smooth, with delectable fruity notes and a pronounced peppery end note to round it out. Better yet, this golden elixir costs a meagre $10.99 per 16.9-ounce bottle, amounting to about $0.65 per ounce.

A bottle that is astoundingly affordable for a single-source EVOO, this noteworthy olive oil is rendered from olives that are grown, picked, and pressed on Italy's island of Sicily. The final flavor? An olive oil that has a light, peppery finish that tingles the throat — a signature of high-quality extra virgin olive oil. This tingling sensation is due to oleocanthal, a compound that causes a slight burn in the back of the throat, and a sign that the olive oil is fresh and pure.

An EVOO that Consumer Reports also rated as one of the best extra virgin olive oils at Trader Joe's, this is a great choice for those looking for a product that won't go bad before you have a chance to use it up. While you can certainly cook with this olive oil, home cooks find this to be an ideal choice for finishing dishes.

Those looking for a premium olive oil at a wallet-friendly price will be drawn to Aldi's Specially Selected Sicilian Extra Virgin Olive Oil. Weighing in at $12.35 for a 16.9-ounce bottle ($0.73 per ounce), this Aldi exclusive might be higher priced than other Aldi options, but it's actually quite the steal given its high praise.

An Aldi exclusive olive oil that has rightfully earned a place in your shopping cart, home cooks herald this olive oil for being single-sourced, with all ingredients originating in Italy. Other culinary enthusiasts applaud this EVOO for its striking hue and hearty flavor, labeling the taste as subtly grassy. Many appreciate the dark glass bottle as well — a characteristic that helps protect this precious liquid from light, which can quicken spoilage and cause the oil to lose its robust flavor. If that isn't enough, this esteemed EVOO also bears a seal from Val Di Mazara P.D.O., signifying the specific region it comes from, in addition to approval from the North American Olive Oil Association, denoting its superior caliber.

This Italian olive oil is one home cooks declare to be perfect for drizzling over just about everything from salad to pizza, as well as making a delicious olive oil cake. No wonder it took the number one spot in our ranking of Aldi olive oils.

California Olive Ranch is a fan favorite among many home cooks.A brand that provides a range of products, from single-source 100% California olive oil to global blends, you'll find an olive oil to fit every price range here. Chefs eagerly praise California Olive Ranch EVOO for its standout flavor, declaring it to be great for a number of uses, from cooking and dipping to marinades and rubs.

This cost-effective EVOO is priced at an inviting $13 to $17 for a 16.9-ounce bottle of the global blends, which are offered in mild, medium, and robust varieties. It's an olive oil home cooks find to be surprisingly affordable compared to other better-known brands. The medium global blend in particular strikes a crowd-pleasing middle ground, securing the perfect balance between peppery and mild while still holding a classic grassy characteristic.

Still, if you're looking for more of a finishing oil, the 100% California might be more appealing. Devotees of this single-source, California-exclusive olive oil declare it to be well-rounded, mild-tasting, and incredibly delicious, making it a great choice for just about everything from marinades and sautéing to baking delectable desserts. Additionally, culinary enthusiasts love the dark, glass bottle this EVOO comes in, along with the non-GMO project verification and vegan certification. Priced around $18 to $20 for a 16.9-ounce bottle, it may be more expensive, but it's an olive oil home cooks claim to last a while.

A preferred brand of chef Giada De Laurentiis, Lucini is a store-bought olive oil admired by many households for its flavor and versatility. Lucini Everyday Extra Virgin Olive Oil can be found at a reasonable $13.49 per 16.9-ounce bottle (about $0.80 per ounce), rendering it a pretty affordable option compared to higher end brands. One Amazon reviewer declared this EVOO to "be a steal" compared to what is being sold elsewhere in the U.S., calling the taste amazing and the price truly unbeatable.

EVOO fans find the cost to be fitting for the quality, declaring you can definitely taste the caliber when dipping bread into this delightful EVOO. Just add a little salt and pepper, and this palatable olive oil will do the rest. One supporter preferred it over Lucini's premium olive oil, claiming the Lucini Everyday Extra Virgin Olive Oil to possess a stronger bite, with more peppery and complex notes.

However, Lucini's Premium Select Extra Virgin Olive Oil has its backers as well. A single-source Italian oil, home cooks commend this cold-pressed EVOO for its smell, perfect for use in all applications, from cooking to salad dressings. They find the bottle perfectly sized, ensuring you use up the contents before it loses its quality. Priced at $23.99 for a 16.9-ounce bottle, it may be slightly pricier, but it's an olive oil well worth considering for its rich, buttery flavor and pungent finish.

Many are shocked at the affordable price of BONO Sicilian Val Di Mazara PDO Organic Extra Virgin Olive Oil, given its similarity to higher-end brands in taste. With a flavor one Redditor described as "pleasantly bitter and grassy, with a rich mouthfeel," many are astounded by this EVOO's phenomenal flavor profile. One avid supporter named it the best olive oil they had ever tried, finding it devoid of any bitterness at all.

Domestic cooks admire this non-GMO project verified, USDA organic certified EVOO, loving the gorgeous yellow color and authentic Italian flavors. Upon sampling, one olive oil enthusiast declared the taste to transport them back to Italy, conjuring images of dining upon mozzarella and fresh tomatoes drizzled with olive oil. Another supporter lauded the vibrant aroma and well-balanced flavor, professing it to contain the perfect ratio of peppery flavor and smoothness, which ends in a pleasantly buttery finish.

While more expensive than other household brands, its $24.99 price tag for a 16.9-ounce bottle is pretty exceptional considering the Protected Designation of Origin (PDO) certified status of this single-source olive oil, which ensures the olives that make up this delectable liquid come solely from the Val Di Mazara region of Italy. The process is supervised from growth to harvest and processing, undergoing rigorous testing to confirm the quality before being granted a one-of-a-kind serial number, denoting its authenticity and lineage.

To select the best budget olive oils recommended by home cooks, we assessed a number of online reviews, discussions, and social media posts by household cooks and amateur chefs. From here, we selected the most notable olive oils to be found at affordable prices, seeking out the best value oils that still held high-quality characteristics, such as cold-pressing, organic ingredients, or simply an exceptional taste. A range of olive oils were included in this article, from wallet-friendly options that are ideal for cooking and baking with to those that are best for raw uses and finishing dishes.

American Projects Busiest Thanksgiving Travel Period in Years

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

American Airlines expects to fly over 8 million people in the days leading up to and following Thanksgiving.

The carrier said Monday that it will operate around 85,000 flights between Nov. 19 and Dec. 1. During that time, an American flight will depart every 13 seconds, officials said.

American said it will operate 7,021 flights on the Sunday after Thanksgiving, Nov. 29, making it the airline’s largest operating day since the COVID-19 pandemic.

At 11 a.m. Central Time on Nov. 20, 29, and 30, the carrier will have 1,130 flights operating simultaneously.

“Our team is ready for one of the busiest travel periods of the year, and we’re focused on delivering a safe, reliable operation so our customers can spend less time thinking about holiday travel and more time enjoying the holiday,” American Chief Operating Officer David Seymour said in a news release.

According to TSA data, the Sunday following Thanksgiving is often the busiest day of the year for air travel. The Tuesday and Wednesday preceding Thanksgiving also rank high, as do certain weekends in June and July.

Democrats Won. When Do Hollywood Crews Get Back to Work?

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Tom Curley is happy about the election results. Whether they’ll help him get back to work is another matter. The production sound mixer, who won an Oscar for “Whiplash,” told IndieWire he has struggled to find consistent employment since his last gig, “CSI: Vegas,” was canceled in 2024.

He’s picked up some work here and there, but nothing has restored the professional footing Curley said he had just two years ago: a loss that has felt “humiliating.”

“I kind of feel like I was in a car accident almost, and I’m just here staring at the aftermath, wondering how I’m going to pick up the pieces, if I’m ever going to pick up the pieces,” he said. “It’s a very destabilizing thing.” Related Stories DC Studios’ Latest Reboot: James Gunn and Peter Safran’s Exit Is About Cost-Cutting, Not Just Creative Issues Attend, the First Marketplace Connecting Filmmakers Directly to Theater Owners, Finally Launches Today

Before last week’s midterms, Curley was cautiously optimistic about a proposed federal tax credit designed to keep more film and TV production work inside the U.S. Now, he’s worried the blue wave could inadvertently slow a bill that had been gaining momentum on both sides.

With Republicans meeting such dramatic electoral losses on November 3, Curley fears President Trump could withdraw his support for the proposed tax credit as a kind of punishment.

“All it takes is some hurt feelings to get in the way,” he said.

But the election was mostly good news for Hollywood, right? Democrats won back both chambers of Congress, and Xavier Becerra (also a Democrat) will soon succeed Gavin Newsom as governor of California — still home to the country’s largest film and TV production workforce.

Meanwhile in LA, Nithya Raman beat incumbent Mayor Karen Bass after explicitly campaigning to make it easier to shoot here. Raman’s victory was widely seen as a rebuke of the status quo in a city where production jobs have become increasingly difficult to find. It’s also part of a broader Democratic promise to make the economy work better for the people who actually depend on it, crews included.

Yet for the scads of film and TV workers IndieWire spoke to in Los Angeles, being pleased with the election results politically didn’t necessarily mean expecting more work to come their way.

Hollywood is a state of mind, sure. But it’s also a literal place where residents have rent due even when the industry isn’t thriving — and inflation, gig-economy restrictions, SAG-AFTRA contract uncertainty, permitting headaches, artificial intelligence, inaccessible healthcare, corporate consolidation, and, yes, productions increasingly leaving town are making the gaps between paychecks harder to survive.

Legislation can support crews in theory, but film and TV executives still decide what gets made, where it gets made, and who gets hired in the end. For someone like Curley, even one more gig might ease his anxiety and help with the bills. The harder question is what comes after that opportunity wraps, and whether today’s labor market can still offer the people who make movie magic enough for a real living.

The first test for this new establishment comes even before the majority takes office.

In the first quarter of 2025, roughly 46 percent of scripted American projects tracked by Luminate were filmed entirely abroad. That’s part of a broader trend toward international production that has drawn bipartisan concern in the U.S. since the late ‘90s.

Right now, Sen. Adam Schiff and Rep. Laura Friedman, both Democrats from California, are trying to pass a federal production tax credit through the outgoing Congress during the lame-duck session. The bill was introduced in September with bipartisan backing — including from South Carolina Sen. Tim Scott and Texas Rep. Nathaniel Moran, both Republicans returning to Congress next year.

The proposed Motion Picture, Television, and Entertainment Revitalization Act would lower the cost of hiring film and TV workers in the U.S. by giving eligible productions a national tax credit covering 20 percent of qualifying pay. Bonuses could bring that up to 30 percent, although qualifying projects would generally need to cost over $1 million and spend at least 75 percent of their principal photography days in the U.S.

Some domestic post-production and visual effects work would qualify for federal tax breaks, too, and paired with the right state incentives, film producers could reliably use those savings to motivate new work. In fact, Curley said the people who’d hired him before were explicitly waiting for a federal incentive along those lines. That made the proposal feel like a possible job, not just another bill.

“I’ve been in a survival state of mind for the past couple of years, and I would say my nerves are pretty frayed,” he said. “So I’m extra sensitive to potential downfalls to this whole plan that we’re hoping goes through.”

Still, legislators say there’s cause for hope. Rep. Friedman told IndieWire on November 9 that the House bill had 54 cosponsors, evenly split between Democrats and Republicans.

“I’m working to get this done as soon as possible because film and television workers need these jobs back now, not in a few months or a couple of years,” she said.

With weeks left in the current Congress, attaching the credit to a larger bill already headed for a vote might speed things up. But responding to IndieWire, Friedman didn’t identify a specific legislative package that could carry the measure to a scheduled floor date right now. If the proposal doesn’t become law before this session ends, lawmakers will have to reintroduce the initiative next year.

“It will take both parties working together to get this across the finish line, and we are pushing hard to make this much-needed incentive a reality,” Sen. Schiff told IndieWire in a statement.

Friedman noted that, as written, the credit could possibly take effect as early as January 1 — again, if the bill is signed before 2027. That could establish when production could qualify, but it doesn’t tell us when out-of-work crews might actually start getting calls. What’s more, when those job offers finally do come through? They could be far from old creative hubs like Los Angeles.

Richard Sanchez has a production job in California right now, and he knows how much that counts.

“I’m one of the very lucky folks who is working at the moment,” the visual effects editor told IndieWire by email. “But admittedly it’s lean and has been lean for quite a while.”

Tax credits have put states in fierce competition for film and TV jobs, from the first day of shooting to the final edit. And in September, Gov. Gavin Newsom signed AB 2319 — specifically creating a separate California tax credit for post-production work. The state is scheduled to start allocating those credits on July 1, 2027, and the yearly budget will ultimately determine how much is available to give out.

“It’s a start, it needs more funding but I hope that brings work in,” Sanchez told IndieWire.

California already makes up to $750 million a year available through its broader film and TV production credit. That previously expanded program began in July 2025 and generally covers 35 percent of qualifying spending, with a higher initial rate for TV series that relocate here. That can help California land a show, but it doesn’t guarantee steady employment for crew members. Studios still decide how many episodes to order and where to send the post-production work.

In the third quarter of 2026, projects in the program accounted for 40.3 percent of scripted on-location filming in Greater Los Angeles (nearly double their share a year earlier), according to FilmLA’s quarterly report. But over the same period, total on-location filming across the region fell 4.8 percent.

“Where the tax credit applies, it’s working to bring productions back to Los Angeles and put local crews to work,” FilmLA CEO Denise Gutches said in a statement to IndieWire. “Still, overall production remains far below where Los Angeles needs it to be, and crews and businesses across the region are feeling it.”

FilmLA also counts shoot days, not jobs or paychecks. So, those figures can’t tell us how many people were hired, how much they made, or whether they worked enough to qualify for benefits. One person who responded to IndieWire’s Reddit callout and identified themselves as a Bay Area camera operator said they were working on a union production but doubted they’d log enough hours for health coverage.

Gov.-elect Xavier Becerra called the current program “the floor” during his campaign and pledged to expand. Newsom’s proposal would raise the annual cap to at least $1 billion, make expenses such as actors’ pay eligible, and set aside $100 million a year for indie productions. Those changes still need legislative approval, but Becerra’s first budget will give crews a better idea of what he’s prepared to seek.

As for Curley, he got a brief stint with the U.S. main unit of “Artificial,” which shot partly in San Francisco, but most of the shoot, he said, took place in Italy. A federal credit might make a U.S. production more attractive to filmmakers facing such a choice, but then there’s the question of which state they’ll choose.

Georgia, for example, offers a 20 percent base credit plus another 10 percentage points for productions that promote the state, with no annual cap on how much productions can earn in credits. California and Georgia count different expenses, so the percentages are only part of the calculation.

And a producer weighing L.A. and Atlanta gets to run the numbers both ways — while a crew member who’s spent years building a career in one city has a different calculation to make. If the next show goes across the country, can they afford to follow it? Can they afford to stay put and wait? Either way, more American projects won’t necessarily mean more openings for the crews Hollywood has left behind.

“We can’t always be the cheapest, so we need to be the easiest.” That’s how Pamala Buzick Kim, a co-founder of the production advocacy group Stay in LA, sees the city’s job supporting the industry.

A tax credit can make a shoot look good on paper. But someone still has to get the permits, pay the fees, and figure out what happens when the next location runs across a city line. Buzick Kim said mayor-elect Nithya Raman’s office regularly talked with Stay in L.A. about what was getting in the way of filming and what the city could practically do about it. Those conversations made her optimistic.

“Everybody’s looking after their small piece of the pie, which is what their job is,” Buzick Kim said. “But we have not instituted any infrastructure to look out for our business in Los Angeles as a whole.”

Raman’s office did not immediately respond to IndieWire’s request for comment. But her campaign platform called for a staffed film office within the mayor’s office, clearer permit timelines, lower fees for smaller productions, and regular reviews of neighborhood filming restrictions. She also pledged to work with the county and neighboring jurisdictions so productions could move more easily.

“If we’re going to incentivize more people to roll here we need to cut down permit processing times, lower fees, and lift unnecessary restrictions on local filmmaking,” echoed film and TV editor Andy Young in an email to IndieWire. Young volunteered for Raman in the primary and worked as a paid freelance video editor for her campaign during the general election. (He said he was speaking for himself.)

“I’m excited to have a Mayor that sees LA as an industry town, sees how bleak things are right now, and has a real sense of urgency when it comes to filling sound stages and getting cameras rolling again,” Young wrote.

Some of Raman’s proposed changes will take cooperation across local groups. For example, FilmLA coordinates applications and charges for its services, but city officials approve permits and set some of the fees that FilmLA ultimately collects. A new administration at City Hall will have to work with the people already making those decisions to successfully enact changes.

Of course, for a smaller shoot, every cost makes a difference. FilmLA and the city recently extended their low-impact permit pilot through April 30, 2027, meaning qualifying productions can apply for just $350, compared with the standard $931 application fee. A few days on a commercial can pay the bills between longer jobs, and Buzick Kim wants city residents to see community when film crews take over their block.

“How do we get residents to be more favorable and optimistic about production?” she asked for Stay in LA. “How do we get them understanding that these are your neighbor’s jobs?”

Curley said he didn’t know enough about permitting to say how much a film office would practically help with the issues he’s been facing. After all, for years, the city’s reputation as the best place in the world for movies “was our film office,” he said. Still, taken together, Curley’s dry spell and the Stay in LA pitch assume that literal Hollywood, like anywhere else, must earn its place amongst national competition.

California voters chose new leadership in Washington, the state capital of Sacramento, and LA’s City Hall. But they didn’t get say on who would own two of Hollywood’s biggest studios.

On October 6, less than a month before the election, Paramount Skydance completed its acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery. The deal followed a separate earlier merger in August 2025, when Skydance and Paramount Global combined.

In its November 9 investor update, Skydance said it was aiming for at least $6 billion in annual savings within three years. Those plans include combining back-office functions and using more AI in some corporate workflows.

An August analysis commissioned by L.A. County estimated that the merger could put about 4,500 local film and TV jobs at risk over the next three years. That was an estimate of potential losses, not a count of people already laid off — and it also preceded a fall settlement, which required additional production spending from Skydance. Still, investors got a number and a deadline, while crews are continually being asked to wait and see how much work the combined company will actually have for them.

California and other states did get some promises in writing. A court-approved settlement disclosed in an SEC filing requires Skydance to release a minimum number of films in theaters and bars it from selling or closing the Paramount and Warner Bros. studio lots for five years. The deal also requires the new company to spend at least $1.5 billion more on U.S. filmmaking over that period than the two companies would have spent at their combined pace in 2025. But where will that business go?

Curley, for one, can actually imagine some good coming out of an industry that’s scattered beyond Los Angeles. Maybe more people will get a shot at making something of their own. Maybe there’ll be a burst of indie filmmaking like the one he associates with the ’70s. The Academy Award-winning craftsman can also see doors closing for people who’ve spent their working lives learning how to make movies.

“Maybe this is a good thing for the young people,” he said. “I’m hoping that’s the case anyway.”

For now, Curley already has an Oscar. What he wants is a call time and a chance to create again.

Ford Recalling 33,000 Explorer Police Interceptors Because of Interior Lights That Might Not Turn Off

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Our car experts choose every product we feature. We may earn money from the links on this page.

A software glitch can trigger a phantom “Global On” command, leaving the map lights illuminated even when officers try to turn them off.

Lights on a police car are extremely important. From flashing red and blue emergency lights to spotlights designed to illuminate whatever officers are investigating, they serve a variety of purposes. But sometimes, it's best if there are no lights on at all. That's why Ford Explorer Police Interceptors are equipped with a Police Dark Car feature: the last thing an officer wants during a nighttime stakeout is unwanted interior lighting. Unfortunately, more than 33,000 Explorer Police Interceptors have a problem that can leave their map lights stuck on, and it's led to a safety recall.

According to a National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) recall report, Ford is recalling 33,578 Explorer Police Interceptor vehicles from the 2026 and 2027 model years. The problem involves the overhead console, which can incorrectly interpret an electrical signal during vehicle startup as a command to turn on the map lights. Ford calls this a “phantom ‘Global On’ condition.”

Should this condition occur, not only will the map lights remain illuminated, but they can't be turned off using the individual switches. That can be annoying and distracting while driving, but it can be even more troublesome if the officers inside are trying to keep a low profile. The condition also violates Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standard (FMVSS) No. 101, Section S5.3.4(a). Under that standard, front-row interior lighting must have a means of being switched off.

Vehicles built between March 3, 2025, and October 23, 2026, are included in the recall. As of October 13, Ford received 69 warranty claims related to the defect. No crashes or injuries have been reported. Ford dealers will replace the overhead consoles in vehicles built before July 9, 2026, while newer vehicles will have their consoles inspected for an updated component. If the correct component is already installed, the console will simply be reinstalled; otherwise, it will be replaced. All repairs will be performed free of charge, as is required by federal law.

Owners are expected to receive notification letters between December 28 and December 30, 2026. In the meantime, police officers planning any nighttime stakeouts might want to use another vehicle until their Explorer has been repaired.

ZWO introduces the Seestar S30 Plus

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

ZWO has added a new entry point to its smart telescope lineup. The Seestar S30 Plus went on sale Nov. 10 for $499 — $474 for a limited time — undercutting the S30 Pro by $200 and making it the cheapest Seestar available.

“Many people are interested in astrophotography but hesitate when they see the equipment and setup traditionally involved,” ZWO founder Sam Wen said in a press release. “With S30 Plus, we wanted to give beginners more room to explore and create without taking away the simplicity that makes Seestar easy to start with.”

Since the original S50 debuted in April 2023, the Seestar line has continued to grow, most recently with this year’s 4K S30 Pro and S50 Pro. The S30 Plus heads in the opposite direction from the Pro models — and with the original S30 reportedly discontinued, it now holds down the entry-level slot.

Effectively, the new Plus model is an S30 Pro with a few features stripped out to meet a lower price. Like the Pro, it carries two cameras: a telephoto for galaxies, nebulae, and other deep-sky targets; and a wide-angle for broad sweeps of the night sky. The telephoto camera keeps the Pro’s 1.2-inch (30 millimeters) aperture and four-element apochromatic lens, including an element of extra-low-dispersion (ED) glass.

The biggest change is to the sensors. Both of the Plus’ cameras shoot 1440×2560 images (3.7 megapixels, or roughly 2K), versus the S30 Pro’s 8.3-megapixel 4K output — still a step up from the original S30’s 2.1 megapixels. But the difference in sensor size doesn’t mean less detail — only a difference in field of view. The Plus has the same pixel size as the Pro, so zooming in on your photos reveals the same level of detail.d

RELATED: Review: ZWO Seestar S50 smart telescope

On the telephoto side, the Plus swaps the Pro’s larger Sony sensor for a 1/1.8-inch SmartSens SC485SL chip. Both have the same 2.9-micrometer pixels behind the same 160 mm lens, so the Pro’s bigger chip just spans more sky: about 4.6°, compared with the Plus’ 3°. That’s still enough to comfortably frame targets like the Pleiades star cluster and the Orion and Rosette nebulae, along with the Moon’s full disk — or the Sun’s, with the included solar filter in place.

The wide-angle camera uses the same 1/2-inch Sony IMX586 sensor as the Pro, behind a lens with identical specs (6 mm, f/1.75). But where the Pro takes in a 63° view, the Plus crops it down to the same 2K format as its telephoto sensor, covering a smaller 43.5° field — still wide enough for the Milky Way, whole constellations, star trails, and time-lapses.

The Plus does give up a few of the S30 Pro’s handier features. The Pro can run both cameras at once, stacking a nebula through the telephoto while the wide-angle captures the Milky Way and landscape around it; the Plus only shoots with one at a time. Its wide-angle camera also can’t stitch Milky Way panoramas, though a telephoto mosaic mode for larger deep-sky targets remains. And onboard storage is halved to 64 GB.

In the box, buyers get the scope, a tabletop tripod, a carrying case, a USB-C cable, and a magnetic solar filter. The 3.6-pound (1.65 kilograms) unit runs up to 6 hours on a charge and has UV/IR-cut and duo-band light-pollution filters built in.

Like every Seestar, the S30 Plus is driven entirely from a phone through the Seestar app. Users simply pair the device to their phone, let it calibrate, and then pick a target — or let the app suggest one. The S30 Plus automatically slews to the target, centers it, and begins tracking and live stacking, with the image building in detail on screen as frames accumulate.

The app can also identify and label constellations in the wide-angle view, and it can queue up automated imaging plans to run through the night. Finished shots can be tweaked and shared straight from the phone. For those who’d rather process elsewhere, the scope saves stills as JPEG and FITS files and video as MP4 and AVI.

The Seestar S30 Plus is available now for $499 through Seestar’s and ZWO’s online stores, Amazon in the U.S. and Europe, and authorized dealers worldwide. It’s now the cheapest way into the Seestar ecosystem — and owners who outgrow it can easily step up to a Pro model.

More details are available at ZWO’s product page.

Spike in ADHD, Autism Diagnoses Driven by Broader Recognition, Understanding

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Two new studies suggest autism and ADHD are becoming better recognized, not actually more prevalent, and that executive function and social challenges seen in both conditions actually stem from different underlying brain mechanisms.

ADHD and autism spectrum disorder (ASD) share overlapping symptoms, and prior studies suggest that nearly half of autistic children also have ADHD.1 Now, two new studies on ADHD and ASD profiles suggest that rising diagnosis rates are a product of improved symptom recognition and diagnostic practices, and that executive function and social impairments seen in both ADHD and ASD may be traced to different cognitive pathways.

The first study, published in JAMA Psychiatry,2 evaluated the health records of 71,000 children and adolescents diagnosed with ADHD or ASD and 713,000 controls; it found that the annual number of individuals in the nationwide Danish health registry with newly diagnosed ADHD and/or ASD increased from 4,944 in 2012 to 9,179 in 2022. The proportion of girls diagnosed with ADHD or ASD and the median age at diagnosis also grew during the 10-year study period from 2012 to 2022.

However, traditional risk factors strongly linked to an ADHD or ASD diagnosis in 2012, such as low birth weight or lower household income, have become progressively less predictive with time. For example, children born with a low birth weight were 54% more likely to receive a subsequent diagnosis of ADHD or ASD in 2012; by 2022, that percentage dropped to 17%. Overall, the study found that children and adolescents diagnosed with ADHD or ASD today more closely resemble their neurotypical peers compared to those diagnosed 10 years ago.

These findings do not mean that ADHD or ASD has become less impairing; the spectrum of cases has shifted as clinicians get better at identifying less obvious cases. Greater awareness of ADHD and ASD, and broader access to diagnostic assessment tools, are also factors contributing to growing diagnoses among children.

“The study results do not show that these conditions are being overdiagnosed, or that they have become less severe,” says Oleguer Plana-Ripoll, Ph.D., a senior author of the study. “Rather, they indicate that the population receiving these diagnoses has changed over time, and this needs to be taken into account when interpreting current trends.”

The second, smaller study, published in Frontiers in Child and Adolescent Psychiatry,3 found that executive functioning (EF) and social cognition (SC) impairments in children diagnosed with ADHD or ASD may look similar, but the impairments stem from different underlying mechanisms.

The cross-sectional, four-year study analyzed performance-based measures of 123 children, aged 6 to 11 years, across three matched groups: mild ASD, combined-type ADHD, and neurotypical peers.

Findings indicated that both ADHD and ASD groups performed worse than their neurotypical peers on measures of EF and SC. However, children with ADHD demonstrated weak social cognition due to difficulties with response inhibition and attention. Social cognition weaknesses in children with ASD, on the other hand, appeared to result from difficulties in understanding and interpreting social information.

More specifically, children in the ASD group performed significantly worse than children with ADHD on measures of:

Children with ASD also displayed more difficulties than did children with ADHD on executive function tasks that measured the ability to shift between rules and adapt strategies.

Children in the ADHD group performed significantly worse than those in the ASD group on measures of:

How executive functions related to social cognition across disorders was the strongest indication of different cognitive processes between the two clinical groups. In the ADHD group, the theory-of-mind model explained 72% of the variance, compared to just 55% in the ASD group.

The researchers surmise that a person with ADHD relies heavily on executive functions, such as inhibition, auditory attention, and design fluency, for social cognition. In essence, their difficulties in social situations are not because they don’t understand social concepts but because distractions and impulsivity interfere.

In the ASD group, social difficulties were more likely to stem from affect recognition (understanding facial cues) and response set (cognitive flexibility, shifting perspectives), rather than from attention deficit or impulse control.

“ADHD and autism are two separate and distinct conditions that happen to be found together much more frequently than would be expected by chance alone,” says ADHD expert William Dodson, M.D., LF-APA. “For people with co-existing ADHD and ASD, treating the ADHD is a means to an end. The world is a classroom for people with ASD, and they have to be ready to observe and practice what they’ve learned.”

Results from the study suggest individuals would benefit more from targeted ADHD and ASD interventions matched to each disorder-specific profile rather than a single shared approach.

1Lydia Furman, MD, Children with Both Autism Spectrum Disorder and Attention Deficit Disorder- New Insights (March, 2018). publications.aap.org/journal-blogs/blog/pdfDownload/2377

2Tarp, M.E., Lousdal, M.L., Rask, C.U., et al. (2026). Changes in characteristics associated with ADHD and ASD diagnoses over time. JAMA Psychiatry. https://doi.org/10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2026.2722

3Pastorino, G.M.G., Olivieri, M., Aquino, A., Mungo, N., Suriano, R., and Operto, F.F. (2026) Neurocognitive endophenotypes in neurodevelopmental disorders: social cognition and executive functions in children with autism spectrum disorder level 1 and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder. Napoli, l’ass.Cosenza: “Stadio Maradona? Alte probabilità per Euro 2032. Il cantiere aprirà nel 2028”

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

A Radio Napoli Centrale, nel corso di Un Calcio alla Radio è intervenuto l’assessore alle infrastrutture Edoardo Cosenza: “L’allarme bomba inviato alle scuole italiane? Difficile capire se l’email inviate agli istituti siano effettive. Però per principi di sicurezza, capisco che si sia reagito in questo modo. Non sono scherzi da ragazzi. Bisogna capire cosa ci sia dietro. Napoli città ospitante per EURO 2032? La data limite di fine ottobre per scoprire se la città avrebbe ospitato gli Europei era stata data dall’UEFA, ma da parecchio tempo avevo intuito che si sarebbe andato oltre. Di Napoli parlano benissimo, come parlano benissimo di altre città. Gli incontri con UEFA e Abodi sono andati benissimo. La probabilità che la città ospiti gli Europei è alta. Le grandi città come Milano, Roma e Napoli hanno un forte vantaggio competitivo. Così come Torino. Ci sono fibrillazioni in altre città come Milano per l’abbattimento di San Siro, ma qui non ci sono problemi. Sono due anni che stiamo lavorando per questo. Quando un’opera supera i 50 milioni di euro, deve avere l’ok del Consiglio Superiore dei lavori pubblici. Questo non comporta l’allungamento dei tempi, la procedura si risolverà entro fine gennaio. La delibera Cipess dovrà essere deliberata dalla Corte dei Conti. A quel punto, il nostro progetto sarà ancora più affidabile. Sul terzo anello dello stadio Maradona si va avanti, ma anche per la riqualificazione dei parcheggi. Quando lo stadio Maradona diventerà un cantiere? Se la gara la faremo partire nel primo trimestre del 2027, con conclusione attorno alla metà del 2027, allora poi servirà il progetto esecutivo. Dall’inizio del 2028 saremo pronti per l’inizio dei lavori. Il nostro primo obiettivo è mettere a posto il terzo anello, per non ridurre la capienza dello stadio. Quando faremo i lavori per il primo anello, garantiremo la possibilità di andare al terzo anello. Non si andrà mai meno dei 54 mila posti attuali. Le prime prove le avremo nel breve. In funzione dei lavori al primo anello, apriremo poi il terzo anello. Il terzo anello lo apriremo man mano che ci servirà, ma i lavori inizieranno certamente prima dei lavori al primo anello dello stadio Maradona”.

The Dane Steadman Interview: Hard Going and High Stakes in the Karakoram

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

There is much more to an expedition than summit or failure. In this exclusive interview, American climber Dane Steadman explains why his recent new route on Karakoram’s Sosbun Brakk (6,414m) “was much more difficult than the one on Yashkuk Sar, both on and off the mountain.” Strong words, considering that he and his partners were awarded a Piolet d’Or for Yashkuk Sar.

The one factor on the team’s side was the previous experience of Steadman and Cody Winckler. “It was our third trip, so we knew a lot more about climbing there,” Steadman said. Indeed, after Yashkuk Sar, Winckler and Steadman returned to Pakistan in 2025 and climbed two new routes with Blake Berghoff.

Grant Stewart of Canada, the third member of the Sosbun Brakk team, is a skilled climber but was less familiar with the Karakoram. His most remarkable climbs had taken place in Alaska and at home in the Canadian Rockies.

Comparing the two epic climbs, Steadman highlighted the differences.

“The trip to Yashkuk Sar went incredibly smoothly. Getting there and acclimatizing was great. We had an amazing weather window for the climb. Nothing on it was exceptionally hard, and we did it on the first try, and the descent went without a hitch,” he said.

He explained that the hardest parts of that 2024 first ascent were their lack of experience and the uncertainty that came with it. An unexpected mishap also occurred during the climb itself: a snow mushroom collapsed above them, forcing them to change their ascent route halfway up.

“On the other hand,” Steadman went on, “almost everything about the Sosbun Brakk trip was hard: lost bags, sickness acclimatizing, big storms beforehand and while on the mountain, hard climbing and route-finding, and a very difficult descent through terrible weather.”

The unclimbed north face of Sosbun Brakk. Photo: Dane Steadman

“[Overall,] on the Yashkuk trip, I think we felt for most of the time that we would be able to do it with only some moments of doubt here and there,” Steadman admitted. “On Sosbun, we thought much of the time that we wouldn’t be able to do it for all the reasons above. We were quite surprised [to succeed].”

One unusual aspect of the Sosbun Brakk expedition was its dates. They went to Pakistan and climbed in early fall, two months later than the Karakoram’s typical July-August climbing season. Yet, Steadman says this was planned to account for the changing climate in the high ranges.

Approaching the face. Photo: Dane Steadman

“First, while the Karakoram doesn’t receive as much of the Indian monsoon as the main Himalaya, it is still a monsoonal climate, and I think (and have heard from many locals now) that the weather tends to be much better in the fall than in the summer,” he explained.

“Second, we were concerned about rockfall and generally unsafe conditions on the face, being relatively low and lacking in buttresses. We thought the colder temperatures would make things safer and increase the odds of finding good ice.

“Third, Grant was able to get time off in October more easily than earlier in September. In a perfect world, we probably would have gone one to two weeks earlier, but we worked with what we had.”

Asked about other expeditions planning to visit the Pakistani mountains in the future, Steadman suggests that October could be a good time for certain climbs, but that September is also great. “This year, the weather was much less stable in September-October than I’ve experienced on my previous trips. Things got much snowier than normal for that time of year, [according to locals], but I think the weather was still better than through most of the summer.”

The team planned a single alpine-style push in the purest style. However, when they started on the route, they encountered an impassable hanging serac. They had to retreat and decide whether to try a totally different line or go home.

The team decided to try a route to the right of the originally planned one. But how could they be sure they wouldn’t reach another dead end?

Steadman admitted it was difficult. “The line we ended up climbing was entirely hidden from view at our base camp and where we acclimatized,” he explained. “[It was] an east-facing gash on a north face, which we only saw from the northwest until we tried it. From all the views we had before the first attempt, the central line we first attempted appeared to be the only route that wouldn’t involve climbing huge walls of monolithic gneiss, as it followed ice streaks and sheets for a good amount of it, and only looked to have relatively short cruxes.

At the base, they discovered that a tiny but menacing serac hung over the center of the wall, threatening the lower half. (The upper half appeared protected by an overhanging rock wall above it.)

“So we shifted to a different line to the left, which looked much harder but possible, and planned to rejoin our intended line at mid-height, under the protection of the overhangs.

That attempt didn’t so much dead-end as things compounded, forcing a retreat. “We were tired even on the approach from non-stop efforts to get there and acclimatize. We had some equipment issues, were moving slowly, and most of all, the climbing was damn hard,” he said.

In the end, they chose to rappel down after a two-hour-plus lead climb on a pitch that hadn’t looked hard from below. Dusk was approaching; there was no bivy spot in sight, and they were only 300m up the 1,800m wall.

“However, from our position on that attempt, we were able to see the right-trending, east-facing gash on the right side of the face, which was totally hidden by monolithic rock walls from all our previous vantage points,” he said. “So, after bailing, we felt like the central line would require fresh bodies, minds, and a huge weather window, but the right-hand line would be much more feasible.”

The climbers rested for three days in base camp and then set off again. “We certainly felt that we could get shut down at any number of points, especially at the steep mushroom-choked chimney on the headwall below the junction with the northwest spur,” Steadman admitted. “But it seemed like there was a good chance we could do it, and, most importantly, we felt like we could bail from anywhere [if necessary].”

The summit ridge of Sosbun Brakk after many tough decisions. Photo: Dane Steadman

However, bailing would not be easy. “The lower part would involve rappelling off V-threads, he explained, and if they had to turn back below the headwall, they would need to rappel out right to an icefield.

Route topo: line of ascent in red, line of descent in yellow. Photo: Steadman/Stewart

The trio summited on their fifth day on the wall, at 4 pm. Days were now short, and they still had a long, uncertain descent ahead. The descent began on the line of ascent, but eventually deviated to new terrain.

“We descended the upper half of our route, almost entirely rappelling down the northwest spur, with a little bit of downclimbing/traversing),” Steadman said.

They then reached a point west of their ascent line, where the face was slightly smaller and more covered in permanent ice. “Then we rappelled straight down, diagonaling back left partway down to avoid some serac hazard.”

Descending in worsening weather. Photo: Dane Steadman

Dane Steadman and Cody Winckler of the U.S., together with Grant Stewart of Canada, completed the first ascent of the north face of Sosbun Brakk (6,414m) in Pakistan’s Karakoram last month. The peak had been climbed only once before, by a Japanese team in 1981, from a different, milder side. The new 1,800m route includes difficulties of M7, WI5.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

Taula eclipse

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The Aug. 12, 2026, total solar eclipse sets over Talatí de Dalt, a late Bronze Age settlement on the Spanish island of Menorca. The Sun’s partial phases march down to the horizon, where totality lasted about 73 seconds — the site’s first total eclipse since 1539. The photographer composited frames shot with a Sony mirrorless camera and a 35mm lens; the foreground is a 1/30-second exposure at f/2 and ISO 160.

Todd Monken: I intend for Deshaun Watson being our starting QB

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Browns head coach Todd Monken opened his Wednesday press conference by confirming that Deshaun Watson will be back as the team’s starting quarterback for their Week 10 game against the Texans.

Watson did not play last week after missing a pair of practices while in the concussion protocol and the team’s plans for this Sunday remained a topic of conversation because Monken declined to say who would start during a Monday press conference. Monken explained that response by saying that he wanted to talk to the team’s coaching staff before adding that he also didn’t feel a need to make a public proclamation.

“Ultimately, the full body of work, the previous eight games we had played,” Monken said. “Deshaun played pretty well. That was my intention, I just wanted to make sure to run it by my staff before I said it publicly. I don’t know why I have to say it publicly.”

Monken was asked if he will be making a quarterback determination on a weekly basis moving forward.

“I don’t look at it week-to-week and I don’t look at it as long term,” Monken said. “We’re judged each week. I am, he is, our team is. . . . I anticipate him being our starter. That’s what he’s been, he’s been our starter. . . . I have no intention of Deshaun not being our starter.”

The Texans are 5-4 heading into their game with the Texans and they have a bye in Week 11. If they are unable to stay on the right side of .500 after that break, Monken will likely field more questions about the plan for Watson moving forward.

American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

Two Hikers Were Killed by a Sow Grizzly with Cubs in ‘Very Rare’ Predatory Attack

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 11, 2026 5:31 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Two women and four grizzlies are dead near Bella Coola, British Columbia after an unusual and horrific predatory attack. A sow with three cubs killed two hikers, a mother-daughter pair who had been missing since November 1. DNA tests confirm the women were killed by the bears.

“It’s surreal. It doesn’t seem real,” Josh Balcarras, a family member, told CBC News. “The way it starts is a few hours of not hearing from them. That turns into a day, and then a night. Then a second night comes and it becomes a real thing.”

The remains of Jennifer Balcarras, 48, were found November 2. The remains of her daughter, 20-year-old Selah Reed, were found November 3. The two were not far from a trail  in the steep and heavily-wooded Snooka Trail System. Both were buried in caches, something bears often do to hide their food from other animals. Investigators consider the attack to be motivated by predation.

“This attack involved predatory behavior, which I can tell you is very rare,” Jeff Tyre, British Columbia Conservation Officer Service (BCCOS) inspector said in a recorded statement issued November 10. “That is to say, we don’t know how it started but we know how it ended. Out of respect for the family, I’m going to decline to answer what predatory behavior looks like specifically in this case.”

The mother grizzly bear was in her late teens. Her three cubs were born this year. BCCOS announced November 10 that all four were captured and euthanized for their predatory behavior. A week after finding the remains of the second woman, the case is considered closed and trapping equipment has been removed. Nuxalk Nation cultural leaders will decide how the bear remains will be used.

“They all had to be destroyed because bears that are involved in attacks on people cannot be rehabilitated or relocated,” Tyre said. “This type of behavior presents an extreme risk to the public and such bears are dispatched to protect the public and prevent similar attacks in the future.”

This most recent attack happened nearly a year after a grizzly attacked a group of tribal school children who were having lunch in the same region last November. In that case covered by OL, three students and one adult were hospitalized after a grizzly raided their picnic.

Despite the two high-profile incidents, investigators say grizzly bear attacks are not increasing near Bella Coola. They also cross-checked DNA from bears involved in both cases. None of the grizzlies euthanized this year were involved in the group attack last year. That bear was never located.

The tribe renewed its safety protocol during this year’s investigation. It has since lifted curfew but continues nightly patrols and offers escorts to take kids to school.

“This attack has been deeply re-triggering for our community members as we continue to recover from last year’s attack,” stated Samuel Schooner, Nuxalk Nation elected chief. “Our focus continues to be on taking care of our community members.”

Bella Coola is in the coastal region of British Columbia. Coastal bears and interior bears are genetically the same grizzly species, but their size and diet are different. Coastal bears have access to salmon, a nutrient-rich food source.

“Individual bears with increased access to salmon tend to be larger, have bigger litters, have lower stress hormone levels, and exist at higher densities at a population level,” reads an October 2025 study by Lauren Henson and Kris Christensen published in the scientific journal G3: Genes, Genomes and Genetics. “Interior grizzlies [have] smaller body mass and skull size than coastal individuals.”

Read Next: These Alaskans Stopped a Charging Grizzly at 5 Yards with Their 10mm Pistols

BC’s combined coastal and interior grizzly bear population has increased from 6,600 in the 1970s to 15,000 in 2018. In 2017, BC implemented a controversial grizzly bear hunting ban. Those in favor of the ban believe that a lack of grizzly hunting does not lead to more human fatalities, but organizations opposing the ban refer to recent attacks as reason to re-open the hunt.

“We have to do something,” Jesse Zeman, B.C. Wildlife Federation executive director told CBC News. “What we’re currently doing is very clearly not working.”

Kris Millgate is a multimedia journalist based in eastern Idaho. She’s a fly fisher, a bird hunter and an avid seeker of awkward moments. Read and watch more of her work at tightlinemedia.com

RILEGGI IL LIVE – Eurocup, Niners Chemnitz-Napoli Basketball 90-112

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

I tedeschi non mollano, Napoli sul +9 (59-68)

Williams dall’arco prova a tenere Chemnitz agganciata (51-62)

Smith segna due tripla consecutivi (30-42)

Ramsey da tre risponde alla tripla dei tedeschi! (10-13)

DC Studios’ Latest Reboot: James Gunn and Peter Safran’s Exit Is About Cost-Cutting, Not Just Creative Issues

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

When they were hired to run DC Studios, James Gunn and Peter Safran were tasked with turning the struggling comic book company into a true rival to Marvel. Four years later, the two have been handed their walking papers before they were able to accomplish their task.

Skydance, which recently bought Warner Bros. Discovery and with it the rights to heroes like Superman, Batman, and the other DC staples that Gunn and Safran were trying to reinvigorate, opted to go in another, cheaper direction. They brought in Jeremy Latcham, a low-profile executive who, like Gunn, had spent years working for Marvel, to oversee DC’s latest reboot. Related Stories ‘Paper Tiger’ Review: Adam Driver Gives a Career-Best Performance in James Gray’s Devastating Tragedy ‘Iron Boy’ Trailer: Beloved Animated Feature Oscar Contender Throws a Whole Watercolor World Out of Whack

There have been mutterings that the change was because of the commercial performance of DC’s films, and the track record has been a mixed bag. “Superman” did what it needed to do, earning $618.7 million worldwide on a $225 million budget, but it wasn’t a blockbuster on the level of Christopher Nolan’s “Dark Knight” films or “Aquaman.”

But “Supergirl” was an unmitigated disaster, earning $126.5 million against a $170 million budget, resulting in losses of roughly $100 million. And “Clayface,” a body horror film produced for $44 million, has been a modest success, earning approximately $110 million. So no, Gunn and Safran had yet to produce a “Guardians of the Galaxy”-style smash that taps the zeitgeist. Though, to be fair, Marvel hasn’t made as many of those lately either. Tastes have changed, and comic book movies, at least ones not starring Spider-Man, aren’t as popular as they once were.

But look closer, and Gunn and Safran’s ouster is about something more than their films’ commercial performances. It’s about cost-cutting too, something that Skydance, with its $80 billion in net debt, needs to start chipping away at if it’s going to realize the $6 billion in savings it’s promised to find.

The duo’s deal, which was set to expire in 2027, was unusually generous. Safran not only got paid to run the comic book label, he was also allowed to run The Safran Company, the wildly successful production company behind “The Conjuring” franchise (former Netflix film chief Dan Lin, in contrast, divested from his company Rideback when he went over to the streamer). Then there was Gunn, who commanded fees for producing films like “Supergirl” and “Clayface,” as well as fees for those DC films that he wrote and directed, such as “Superman” and “Man of Tomorrow.” Gunn’s total haul for a DC film could be as high as $10 million for all his various duties, according to insiders.

When Warner Bros. Discovery chief David Zaslav offered the pair such a sweet deal, comic book movies looked more durable and he needed to make a splash after DC produced flops like “Aquaman and the Lost Kingdom” and “The Flash.” To sweeten the offer, Zaslav also allowed Gunn and Safran to be relatively autonomous, reporting directly to him, whereas Skydance wanted the DC leadership to be under Skydance Motion Picture Group chiefs Dana Goldberg and Josh Greenstein.

Skydance has also been stunned by the financial picture of Warner Bros.’ film unit since taking over. The studio has suffered a devastating wave of flops this year, losing hundreds of millions on a slate that included bombs such as “Digger,” “The Bride!,” and last weekend’s “The Cat in the Hat.” One insider put the estimated losses at nearing $1 billion. Of course, most of those films were greenlit under Michael De Luca and Pam Abdy, who were let go as soon as David Ellison got the keys to the Warner lot, and have nothing to do with Gunn or Safran. But it does put pressure on Skydance to turn things around and improve the film division’s margins, fast. On an earnings call this week, Ellison acknowledged the film division faces “headwinds” and “has underperformed this year.” He went on to pledge to “apply the same playbook we did at Paramount: greenlighting more selectively.”

Safran remains widely respected on the lot for his stewardship of DC, and is praised for trying to keep budgets in line and for cleaning up the company’s byzantine production practices so that its films, shows, and comic books had a unifying creative voice. Gunn, though a gifted filmmaker, had a harder time adjusting to life in the c-suite, something he admitted to this week in a lengthy post on X. “What only the closest people to me have known over the past four years is my time at DC Studios has been trying, to say the least,” Gunn wrote. “I dislike the at-times corporate nature of the job (thanks to Peter for taking the brunt of that). I’m always a very hard worker, but as a filmmaker I would at least get to take short breaks to unwind, and I’ve stolen even that time from both myself and my loved ones over the past four years.” He closed by saying that he was excited to return to working purely as a creator.

Gunn’s habit of engaging with fans directly on social media raised eyebrows at Warner Bros. and, later, at Skydance. But it cut both ways, with Gunn often using his vast following to promote upcoming projects and to engage more directly with fans in a way that inspired loyalty. Insiders also credit Safran and Gunn with not only reinvigorating Superman on screen, but also backing critically acclaimed shows such as “Lanterns” and “Peacemaker.” And the pair handled a delicate situation with the production of “The Batman: Part II,” which was delayed because of personal issues involving its writer and director Matt Reeves, a situation that ultimately required Steven Soderbergh to step in to finish the sequel under Reeves’ guidance. It is expected to wrap production in the next few weeks.

Insiders also note that Gunn and Safran didn’t have a chance to fully realize their vision of making superhero films featuring a wider variety of characters across a greater diversity of genres made for a broader range of budgets. Gunn was already plotting his next move even as he edited “Man of Tomorrow,” which features a team-up of Lex Luthor and Superman. He had outlined a third “Superman” film and was working on a “Justice League” and a “Batman” film, either one of which was expected to be his next project.

In Hollywood, however, timing is everything. And Gunn and Safran had the misfortune of trying to build their own MCU just as comic book movies were declining in popularity. Will Latcham have more success? He’s widely liked and boasts ties to the Christian community that could broaden DC’s core audience. But he also doesn’t come with the kind of pedigree and profile that Gunn did.

As for DC’s fans, they are bracing for yet another reboot having seen the comic book brand they love go through three different regimes over the last decade. Will Latcham provide the kind of consistency the company so desperately needs, or will he be just the latest in a long line of executives asked to pull off a miracle without being granted much of a runway?

Attend, the First Marketplace Connecting Filmmakers Directly to Theater Owners, Finally Launches Today

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Way back in October 2024, The Fithian Group, led by former NATO chief John Fithian and founding partner Patrick Corcoran, announced plans for an ambitious new marketplace, one that would help connect filmmakers and their projects directly to theater owners looking to book their movies and effectively cut out the distribution middleman. Today, that vision is now a reality.

The Fithian Group on Wednesday at the American Film Market announced the formal launch of Attend Theatrical Marketplace, the group’s online platform and marketplace for filmmakers and exhibitors that is now live and ready for business.

Attend launches with today just a curated selection of four films, all of whose filmmakers will now be doing the legwork to connect with theaters and find venues at which to exhibit their work. But the platform is already loaded with exhibition partners who will be able to browse available films, with more to be added soon, and Attend will eventually be a fully open platform to any filmmaker who wishes to do outreach directly to theater owners. Related Stories DC Studios’ Latest Reboot: James Gunn and Peter Safran’s Exit Is About Cost-Cutting, Not Just Creative Issues ‘Paper Tiger’ Review: Adam Driver Gives a Career-Best Performance in James Gray’s Devastating Tragedy

IndieWire got an early demo of Attend and how it aims to simplify the work that distributors do every day in putting movies into theaters, as well as the work exhibitors have to do in booking movies, paying invoices, and more. And IndieWire will on Wednesday lead a panel discussion at AFM with Corcoran, Charles Beale of the site’s designer Titlepool, and filmmaker Lilian T. Mehrel, whose Tribeca award-winning film “Honeyjoon” will be one of the four inaugural films shopping for theaters.

Beyond “Honeyjoon,” the other three films available on Attend at launch are Kenny Riches’ crime drama “Mouse”; “Spoke,” which is a documentary about firefighters and the trauma they deal with after the fires; and “Censurada,” an LGBTQ+ drama from Marco Garza about forbidden love in 1969 Spain.

The vision of Attend was outlined to IndieWire over a year ago with an indie rom-com called “You, Me, and Her” serving as an early guinea pig for the idea. The thinking was, if you had a film that could demonstrate it already had an audience and you had a clear sense of where your audience was and how to target them, all you needed were the screens to showcase your movie and the means to get your movie into the hands of those theaters.

That was and remains the tricky part. Few people have the connections with theater owners to be able to reach them and get a film out in any meaningful capacity. This year has produced a number of examples in which enterprising filmmakers have done without distributors and found the audience they already knew was there to release their movies. YouTuber Markiplier went directly to theaters and made $50 million with his horror movie “Iron Lung.” The documentary “NAZA” skipped a distributor in order to get its story about the IDF out with urgency. Even an AI movie called “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” is next month releasing without the aid of a distributor.

Attend seeks to use technology to simplify that process, to get your film out to an even wider audience and a more targeted one. For filmmakers, the platform is designed to eliminate the guesswork of who you need to talk to, how you reach them, and what attributes a given exhibitor is looking for in a film. For exhibitors, it filters all your titles and screens into one place, and it makes the receiving of deliverables or the payment of invoices more automated than the often tedious work it is for most exhibitors today. It’s exciting to see the whole vision come into fruition.

On the filmmaker end, the way Attend works is you as an individual can upload details and assets about your film, from simple details like its runtime, logline, and genre, to much more granular details about the aspect ratio, the available formats, the awards it has won and festivals it has played, the social networking reach of talent, and many more data points. There may be assets you don’t realize will be significant to a given exhibitor but will jump out as they browse your profile. You can then identify themes that speak to the film’s target audience and narrow the demographics for which your film is intended.

From there, you can filter and browse theaters to identify which theaters would be the best fit for your movie. For instance, Attend has data that can show a theater is within walking distance of a firehouse or that the theater has ties to a local advocacy group that aligns with the themes of the film’s story, and Attend’s predictive AI engine can recommend those theaters that would be available and the best fit for your film. Then, instead of reaching out to each of those theaters individually, filmmakers can easily select all the relevant theaters they want to message and have the option to manually add or subtract more beyond the recommended targets. You can even modify proposed theatrical terms for individual theaters or personalize messages, and as theaters come in, Attend can dynamically estimate P&A spend for those locations.

When theater owners are contacted, they’ll receive push notifications about films that are available for them to review, and they can further browse any available films in the marketplace and do their own outreach. Exhibitors can create tabs and info on any number of their circuit’s screens or locations and can upload in spreadsheets specs like screen size, audio, and seat count that filmmakers can view themselves to better get a sense of which theaters are the best fit. Exhibitors can specify rental prices for individual screens, you can mark your theater as being willing to offer simple four-walling options or that it’s capable of hosting big premieres, whether you want to offer “split screens” or “clean screens,” or you can set terms for how long you’re able to hold a movie. And not only does it also itemize marketing costs, Attend clearly indicates what the share of a film’s box office would be if they were to accept it and program it.

This helps theater owners in a number of ways. It takes the busy work out of things like paying numerous individual vendors or distribution partners, and it lets you download and easily generate a W9 form based on that film’s booking, being able to do so across multiple locations. But for theater owners that generally don’t know what they’re playing until the Monday before, this gives theaters far more advanced leeway and awareness to then be able to do the necessary marketing, put tickets on sale, and actually get butts in seats. All that is especially important for the smaller indies that need that word of mouth.

For now, theaters haven’t linked their loyalty data or other point of sales or scheduling tools to Attend, but that could come with time, and as people actually begin to use the platform, the predictive functionality will improve in the long run.

Corcoran and Fithian see Attend being useful for smaller distributors themselves in the long run, not just individual filmmakers who want to cut out the middleman. Distributors can use it to plan the campaigns of multiple titles and use its automation features to save time and costs. And while today Attend just supports new releases, there’s no reason why older library titles couldn’t be marketed here to exhibitors as well.

Time will tell if filmmakers find Attend an effective form of reaching theaters or if exhibitors adopt the platform widely to make it a useful tool for all parties. But it’s an ambitious vision that if implemented properly could have enormous impacts for the independent film world. And after two years of effort and hype, that day could finally be here.

Physicists propose new quantum device to measure how Earth twists space-time

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →
