A proposed tabletop quantum sensor could match the precision of the best satellite measurements of frame dragging, a subtle effect predicted by Einstein's general relativity.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Scientists have proposed a new tabletop device to detect the subtle way Earth's daily spin on its axis twists the space-time around it ‪—‬ an effect predicted by Einstein's general relativity.

If built, the sensor could measure the quantum effect known as frame dragging with the same precision as the best satellite experiments, but in minutes instead of years, the researchers proposed in a new study.

The research, published Sept. 17 in the journal Physical Review Letters, aimed to address a classic mismatch in physics. Picture a ring of clocks bolted to a spinning merry-go-round. You synchronize each clock with its neighbor around the ring. Then, you compare the last clock with the first. They don't match.

"It is impossible to synchronize that collection of clocks when you're in a rotating frame," Kai-Isaak Ellers, a physics graduate student at the University of California, Berkeley (UC Berkeley) and first author of the study, told Live Science.

A photo of scientists working on NASA's Gravity Probe B in 2003. This device measured Earth's frame-dragging from space, confirming Einstein's predictions with an 18% margin of error.

Einstein's theory of general relativity predicts that a massive, spinning object drags the fabric of space-time along with it, just like a spoon twirling in honey. The effect is clearly visible around supermassive objects, like the Milky Way's central black hole. But around Earth, the effect is very faint.

So far, scientists have measured Earth's frame dragging only from orbit. They tracked how the twisting of space-time slowly shifts spinning gyroscopes or the paths of satellites. In 2011, NASA's Gravity Probe B satellite, which carried four gyroscopes, confirmed Einstein's prediction with a margin of error of about 18%. Satellites tracked with lasers have since done even better. In July, researchers reported in the journal Nature that the LARES-2 satellite had shrunk that margin of error to just 0.2%.

However, that took about three years of laser tracking. Researchers also had to account for the fact that Earth bulges at the equator and its mass is spread unevenly, which gives the planet’s gravity small irregularities that tug on satellites far more strongly than frame dragging does. To subtract those tugs, the team relied on detailed maps of Earth's gravity made by other satellites.

Measuring frame dragging more precisely is a way to stress-test general relativity. Several alternative theories of gravity predict a slightly different amount of twisting, and a lab-based measurement would also be free of the modeling assumptions that satellite experiments depend on, Ellers said.

The new device is a loop of superfluid helium-4 — helium chilled until it flows without friction and behaves like a single, giant quantum object. The loop is interrupted by a thin barrier riddled with tiny holes, called a Josephson junction. Superfluid helium can slip through the holes, but ordinary liquid helium cannot.

"That's where the quantum behavior comes from," said K. Birgitta Whaley, a quantum physicist at UC Berkeley and a co-author of the study.

As Earth spins and drags space-time, it nudges the quantum state of the helium flowing through the junction. That nudge shifts the frequency at which the helium sloshes back and forth in the loop, which the researchers could detect.

A visualization of Earth's uneven gravity field taken by NASA's Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment in 2009. The lumpiness of our planet's gravitational field makes it harder to precisely measure relativistic effects.

Superfluid devices, many of them built at UC Berkeley, have detected Earth's rotation since the late 1990s. But the helium-4 versions built around a Josephson junction, like the new design, have worked within about a hundredth of a degree of 2.17 K (minus 455.76 Fahrenheit, or minus 270.98 Celsius), the temperature at which helium-4 becomes a superfluid. At that temperature, a lingering fraction of "normal" helium mixed in with the superfluid adds noise.

Instead, the team calculated how the device would behave at a few thousandths of a degree above absolute zero. Below 0.7 K, the noise changed character, and the team found it drops sharply as the helium gets colder. One key finding in the new paper is the result of the team's thermal noise analysis, which showed how sensitive these devices could be at millikelvin temperatures, Elles said.

The catch is the barrier. Earlier helium-4 devices used holes about 90 nanometers wide. Working at these frigid temperatures demands much smaller holes, about 1 nanometer across ‪—‬ roughly the size of a helium atom. Keith Schwab, a physicist at Caltech and co-author of the study, and his collaborators are developing such materials, but they haven't been built into a working sensor yet.

If they can be, the researchers calculate that the device could measure frame dragging to 0.2% precision in about 35 minutes. If its vibrating membrane were made from the lowest-loss crystals, such as quartz or sapphire, that could drop to about one second, they said.

"This seems to be a very serendipitous discovery that at millikelvin, we can get this high sensitivity," Whaley told Live Science.

The signal corresponds to a time difference of about 10-35 seconds between helium flowing one way around the loop and the other — the same mismatch as the clocks on the metaphorical merry-go-round.

"The interesting thing about it is just how small it is," Whaley said. "It's not so far off from the Planck time," about 10-44 seconds, the shortest slice of time before the known laws of physics are expected to break down. This experiment is still a billion times too large to reach that limit, Whaley said. But future helium experiments could help explore whether time flows smoothly, like a ramp, or ticks forward in tiny, indivisible jumps, like the frames of a movie.

Ellers, K.-I., Christodoulou, M., Schwab, K. C., & Whaley, K. B. (2026). Quantum sensing of gravitational frame dragging with a superfluid ⁴He gyrometer. Physical Review Letters, 137(12), 121404. https://doi.org/10.1103/q3v9-rjm9

Larissa G. Capella is a science writer based in Washington state. She obtained a B.S. in physics and a B.A. in English creative writing in 2024, which enabled her to pursue a career that integrates both disciplines. She reports mainly on environmental, Earth and physical sciences, but is always willing to write about any science that sparks her curiosity. Her work has appeared in Eos, Science News, Space.com, among others.

For More Flavorful Fried Eggs, Add A Teaspoon Of This Spicy Ingredient To The Pan

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Know the right fried egg cooking hacks, and your breakfast can easily take on a gourmet twist. For one such easy enhancement, try a hit of spice — just a teaspoon of harissa will effectively flavor eggs. Hailing from Tunisia, this smoky-savory condiment consists of chilies, garlic, and a wide assortment of spices (often cumin and coriander), all blended into a smooth dark-red consistency. It's a bold but not overwhelming blend of flavors that shines alongside eggs in a pan.

For the greatest ease amidst bleary-eyed mornings, you can buy the spice online in the form of paste in a jar or tube. Don't confuse the condiment with powdered harissa seasoning, a dry blend with a similar flavor profile, but not the right composition for this application — though it might do very well mixed into scrambled eggs.

Instead, you'll want a whole teaspoon of the paste itself. Heat up the pan, and throw it into a hot neutral oil, creating an aromatic red bath of chilis and spices. Follow this up by cracking in a fried egg or two, and sizzle up the lot in the fragrant oil. You'll get eggs with a dark red color and a warm dose of spice, making a dish with a lot of flavor yet not much fuss.

Fried eggs make for a wondrously versatile food, fit as both a dish garnish and a meal in themselves. Upgraded with an ingredient like harissa, such versatile applications continue — only now with a bold hit of spice. You can integrate the seasoned eggs into a wide array of dishes.

Start by taking inspiration from çilbir, a Turkish egg dish consisting of poached eggs, pepper-spiced butter, and yogurt. Consider serving the harissa fried eggs atop a thick and tangy dairy canvas, like Greek-style yogurt or salty labneh. Scooped up with bread or pita, it's a satisfying meal that doesn't require much effort. You can also make a quick and easy shakshuka-style fried egg by creating a base of tomato paste and harissa for your eggs to simmer in.

Once lunch or dinner rolls around, add a little heft to the dish. Harissa-spiced eggs make for a great topping atop extra crunchy chickpeas or a creamy batch of white beans. A pork chop topped with a fried egg is a tried-and-true classic, and the harissa element will lend the construction an extra touch of spice. Not to mention, fried eggs belong atop sauteed greens, too; it's a terrific enhancement to a bowl of kale, for example. Harissa-spiced eggs deliver an aromatic punch, making them a culinary combo worth keeping in mind.

Asteroid Mining Will Crash the Platinum Market, But That’s Good News for Earth

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Science fiction authors have been dreaming of robotic spacecraft harvesting fortunes from asteroids for decades. Humanity is slowly getting closer to that reality. But if you ask asteroid mining skeptics, they will usually ask one specific question - what happens to the prices of all those rare materials once someone actually brings them back? A pair of system dynamicists from MIT directly tackled that question with a new paper titled Will AstroForge Collapse the PGM Market?

Platinum Group Metals, or PGMs, are absolutely critical to modern industry. Two of the most commonly used are platinum and palladium, which are critical components in everything from automotive catalytic converters (hence when thereâs a raft of people stealing them) to hydrogen fuel cells. However, these materials are exceptionally hard to come by - on Earth at least. More than 80% of the known planetary reserves are locked in deep-shaft mines located in South Africa and Russia.

Their absurd prices (tens of millions of dollars a ton) are based at least in part on how difficult they are to extract. Even at the Russian and South African mines, they are only able to glean a few grams of material per ton of rock. In the process, they require ventilation, cooling, and heavy machinery, as well as actual laborers down deep mine shafts. That sounds exactly like the kind of industry that is ripe for disruption - at least if there is another source of the raw material.

Fraser talks about whether we will mine asteroids.

Luckily, there is. Metallic (M-type) asteroids are the leftover metallic cores of protoplanets, and carry concentrations of PGMs dozens of times higher than even the richest mines on Earth. A single kilometer-wide space rock of this type holds more PGMs than humans have extracted throughout all recorded history. No wonder these particular materials have been the focus on asteroid mining efforts for decades.

AstroForge, a start-up based in California, is one of a second-round of asteroid mining start-ups that are focusing specifically on returning PGMs to Earthâs market. Instead of multi-billion dollar asteroid mining craft, they specialize in small, agile probes that cost only a few million dollars each to launch. The value of what they return, though, is much higher.

Their architecture involves latching on to the metallic asteroid using magnets. High power lasers then vaporize the asteroidâs crust, and an onboard refinery separates the PGM gas from the less valuable nickel and iron that makes up the majority of the asteroidâs bulk. After the PGMs have been refined, they are blasted back to Earth in a heat-shielded return capsule, ready to be brought to market on Earth itself. That way, the company only ships high value material rather than processing minerals that donât have as much value back down on Earth.

Isaac Arthur is much more bullish on our eventual ability to mine asteroids, as he discusses in this video. Credit - Isaac Arthur YouTube Channel

Understanding how this would affect the terrestrial market is the focus of the paper, and researchers Robert Nachtrieb and Steven Smith built a dynamic computer simulation tracking what they believe will happen to the PGM market over a 20 year period following the first successful return of a chunk of material from an asteroid. According to their model, there will be a dramatic, two-phase transition.

The first phase can be thought of as a âspace gold rushâ. The prices for PGMs have been held artificially high by lack of supply - a classic problem in traditional economics. Once the supplied amount changes - such as when new sources come online, the price begins to fall as the supply line of the classic âXâ graph moves to the right. But it doesnât happen overnight. At the start, space-based metals will be able to be sold at normal prices, allowing the asteroid mining companies to enjoy astronomical profit margins.

Those profit margins will, at least in theory, drive plenty of additional private investment, which in turn will be used to expand the production capacity of the space-based options. According to the paper, this will drive a cycle where industry profits will skyrocket to eight times what they currently are during this first phase. Thatâs enough to make whoever successfully send material back first fabulously wealthy.

But eventually, the second act will begin - what the authors describe as equilibrium collapse. Global supply of PGMs will far exceed original terrestrial amounts, and the market prices will start falling dramatically. High cost mines, such as those operating in North America, will be the first casualties. The Russian and South African behemoths, though, will not be far behind, as they will be unable to maintain a profit with the lower price caused by the increased supply. Over the course of fifteen years, terrestrial mining supplies will be completely replaced by space-mined ones, and market prices will drop by over 75%.

That might sound like terrible news for the terrestrial mining industry - and to put it bluntly, it is. But itâs great news for everyone else. Cheaper PGMs mean less expensive hydrogen electrolyzers, and therefore a less expensive transition to the âgreen economyâ for the rest of the world. In addition, space-based mining does not have all the acid mine drainage, arsenic pollution, and hazardous labor in deep tunnels that are required to get PGMs out of the ground on Earth.

Which all sounds like a much better future for humanity more generally. The problem is the engineering challenges to get to that point. Astroforge has had a few high-profile failures during its development cycle so far. But thatâs the case with any fast-moving ânew spaceâ company. It is undeniably getting closer to making this future world of green tech provided by space rocks a reality. And, if they do manage to do so, everyone involved should become extremely wealthy in a span of less than two decades.

R. T. Nachtriev & S. J. Smith - Will AstroForge Collapse the PGM Market?

UT - How Much Are Asteroids Really Worth?

UT - The Legal Void of the Asteroid Gold Rush

Andy has been interested in space exploration ever since reading Pale Blue Dot in middle school. An engineer by training, he likes to focus on the practical challenges of space exploration, whether that's getting rid of perchlorates on Mars or making ultra-smooth mirrors to capture ever clearer data. When not writing or engineering things he can be found entertaining his four children, four cats, and two dogs, or running in circles and throwing plastic discs at people to stay in shape.

This Seated Twist Stretches Your Spine, Engages Your Core, and Enhances Your Awareness

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

In Yoga Journal’s Archives series, we share a curated collection of articles originally published in past issues beginning in 1975. This article about a seated twist first appeared in the March-April 1994 issue of Yoga Journal. Find more of our Archives here.

To encourage new perceptual skills in my students, I like to ask them from time to time to practice in a new spot in the yoga studio. If you’re like most people, after a few classes you find yourself regularly gravitating to the same location, often within a few inches of accuracy.

The mere suggestion to place your mat in the front of the room instead of the cozy corner may make you decidedly un-comfortable. Changing positions enables you to see yourself and the world from a different vantage point, much as a photographer might circle a subject to get pictures from different angles.

It’s not surprising, considering the propensity of our species to form habits, that we tend to perceive our bodies from one perspective, including some aspects of ourselves while leaving others out.

Thus our perception of our body may become like a two-dimensional sketch, deprived of the volume, form, and texture that would extend our awareness into the three-dimensional realm. While Eastern cultures have investigated and honored the inner layers of the body as the key to our physical, mental, and spiritual health, most Western cultures have concerned themselves primarily with the body’s superficial layers. Because of this bias, most of us have come to view our movements as entirely dictated by the muscles and bones, unconsciously deleting other systems from our awareness. Keeping the mind on the outer layers, we become unable to feel or act from our deeper selves.

One of the body systems that brings us most succinctly into a deeper sense of self are the organs, the contents of the musculoskeletal container. Awareness of the organs gives our movements volume, form, and a sense of ripe fullness. Through the health and vitality of the organs, the outer layers gain their strength and form.

Just as we might bring our attention to the sensation of a muscle stretching, we can bring our attention to the sensation of an internal organ. When we change the vantage point of our awareness to bring the inner body into the foreground (an inner adjustment analogous to moving to a different place in the yoga studio), the way we experience ourselves and the quality of our movement will be quite different.

For most of recorded history, yogis have been engaged in just this kind of research, exploring the effects that the asanas (as well as pranayama and meditation) have on the deeper, subtler bodily systems. The ancient practitioners noted which movements and practices stimulated, purified, sedated, or neutralized the energy currents moving deep within the body, correlating these phenomena to health, longevity, and the elevation of consciousness. By contrast, they were not terribly interested in the state of their hamstring muscles, and traditional texts make few references to such structures.

Movements that twist the body compress, elongate, and massage the inner organs. Unfortunately, twists are often practiced exclusively from the perspective of the musculoskeletal system; we use the strength of the muscles and the leverage of the bones to force the body into rotation without ever inviting the soft inner contents along for the ride. Focusing on the internal organs not only reduces the possibility of injury, but stimulates and tones these deep structures by bringing our attention to them, just as electrical activity is created in our muscles simply by visualizing them flexing.

In this article, we’re going to look at how changing the vantage point of our awareness affects the practice of Pose Dedicated to the Sage Marichi (Marichyasana III), a seated twist.

Bringing attention to the inner body is a way to physically organize our presence on this center. Knowing that there is nowhere to “get to,” we can proceed with the full knowledge that we are on our way.

The following exercise was adapted from Sensing, Feeling, and Action by Bonnie Bainbridge Cohen. Sit on a cushion or blanket with your legs crossed. Close your eyes and let yourself settle. Bring your awareness to the soft inner contents of the torso surrounded by the musculoskeletal container. Imagine your body as two concentric cylinders with the soft internal organs on the inside and the harder musculoskeletal system surrounding them. Feel how the rise and fall of the breath swells from the core and communicates itself to the outer layer.

Now gently bring your awareness to each of your major organs. In the chest is the heart, slightly to the left of center, with the lungs on either side. In the upper left quadrant of the abdomen is the stomach; in the upper right quadrant of the belly is the liver, which extends up underneath the rib cage. The small intestines occupy most of the central and lower belly. In the lower right quadrant of the belly, the small intestine becomes the large intestine, which makes a hairpin turn to run up the right side of the belly (the ascending colon), across the top (the transverse colon), and down the left side (the descending colon), finishing as the sigmoid colon and rectum.

You may want to refer to an anatomy text for a more in-depth discussion of the internal organs. For now, sense their general position and volume, using your breath to palpate them. The kidneys lie on either side of the spine above the waist. The uterus is deep in the abdomen above the pubic bone.

Now bring your attention to your skeletal structure, as if you were seeing the body with X-ray vision. Extend your right arm over your head and bend to the left, focusing your attention on the bones moving. Repeat this to the other side.

Returning to the first side, initiate the side bend from the skeleton and then Shift your attention to the underlying organs, focusing on elongating the organs on the right side of the body and compressing the organs on the left. Did you go further? How did the quality of the movement change as your focus shifted?

Now initiate the side bend as a movement that arises from the organs. Breathe into and elongate the organs on the right side of the body, letting the skeleton follow. How did this experience differ from the one in which you had your mind on the skeletal system alone?

The organs are the seat of our deepest emotions; we physically experience strong feelings as sensations in the viscera (for example, the butterflies of fear or the tight knot of anger). Therefore, many people find that when they initiate movement from the organ system, the movement seems to arise out of their deepest self—that is, there is not a dualistic sense that the mind is “doing something” to the body. By contrast, when you perceive the body as nothing but a skeleton, it may feel as though your mind is manipulating your body like a puppet on a string.

Now integrate this new awareness of your organs into a simple twisting posture.

Some preliminary suggestions may make your task easier. One of the obstacles to twisting freely is the inability to sit upright with the normal curves of the spinal column intact. When the legs are extended, this problem may be further exaggerated because the hamstrings may pull on the base of the pelvis, causing the back to round (Figure 1: Incorrect). In this position, you are actually sitting on the base of the spine rather than on the sitting bones, and the collapse of the outer body makes it difficult to breathe freely.

To make sitting—and therefore twisting—a little easier, this first variation calls for both legs to be folded. By deactivating the hamstrings, we allow the pelvis to move more freely into an upright position.

Sit cross-legged with the buttocks elevated on a blanket or cushion. Place the right leg so the shin is upright, with the sole of the right foot directly in front of the left ankle. Inhaling fully, allow the abdomen to expand freely. On your exhalation roll the abdomen to the right, guiding the abdomen back against the spine to encourage the twist. Bring your left elbow onto the outside of the right knee (Figure 2).

With each breath, allow the soft inner body to move circularly in the direction of the twist, very much as if you were doing a belly dance. Once the lower torso is turning freely to the right, direct the breath more fully into the right side of the chest. Expanding the right lung with the inhalation, encourage the thoracic spine to move in and up. As you exhale, release the left lung from the center of the chest out into the shoulder, allowing the muscles along the back of the body to follow the changing shape of the lung.

As this occurs, the shoulder blade and then the arm will move further around.

Replace the elbow against the knee as necessary. When you feel you have reached your limit, stay for a few more breaths, simply letting the inhalation and exhalation oscillate the body slightly to and fro. Release on an exhalation and then repeat on the other side.

Now extend both legs in front of you, placing a blanket underneath your buttocks if necessary to create an erect sitting position. Sit with your back one arm’s length from a wall. Bend your right knee, placing the heel of the right foot in line with the sitting bone of the right side of the pelvis. Extend downward through the length of the left leg, simultaneously pressing the right foot down into the floor. Feel how this groundedness through the bones rebounds up through the body as the energy flows through the soft organs and the bones to support the erect carriage of the torso. When you release the downward movement of the legs, notice how the spine sags and the whole torso wilts.

Sense how the movements of the muscles and bones affect the shape of the internal organs, and vice versa.

As you inhale, direct the breath into the mid and upper chest. With each exhalation, successively turn from the lowest part of the belly to the head. Bring your elbow onto the outside of the right knee and place your hand against the wall, making sure that you are far enough from the wall that the shoulder is not elevated toward the ears (Figure 3). Use your arm to lift and support the torso, but do not overpower the soft movements of the inner body by using the arm to forcefully turn the outer muscles. Instead of using the arm as a “dictator,” let your arm become a “guide.” In the same way, wait for the inner body to yield to the movement, then encourage it with the guiding arm.

Take a few more full breaths before releasing and repeating the movement on the second side.

In this exercise you will focus on the contents of the spine—the spinal cord—to bring deeper awareness to your movement. Begin by sitting in Staff Pose (Dandasana) with both legs extended. Bend the left knee and place the heel of the left foot in line with the sitting bone. Now place the left hand immediately behind the left buttock and use it to support the trunk.

Before beginning the twist, take a moment to bring a few central images to mind. Start by bringing your awareness to the contents of the skull. Sense whether the brain is balanced equally between the front and back of the skull. Exiting the brain is the brain stem; then the spinal cord runs through each vertebrae like thread running through a string of beads.

Think of your spinal cord as the tail of your brain and the vertebrae as individual beads, each with its own size and shape.

As you bring your awareness to the brain stem, notice how the spinal cord curves inward so that the cervical vertebrae stack up underneath the head. As you continue your mental journey down the back, the soft spinal cord curves outward, following the convex curve of the thoracic spine. As you move your attention down toward the base of the rib cage, the spinal cord loops back in and the five lumbar vertebrae stack directly under the center of the chest. Continue on down to the massive sacral bone which curves outward and articulates with the two wings of the pelvis.

Having established this anatomical map, begin to sense how the bones of the spine are positioned in relationship to the soft spinal cord. Are there any areas of the spinal cord that feel compressed, perhaps even red and sore from being pressed and rubbed by the bodies of the vertebrae? Are there vertebrae that feel as if they were glued together, or areas where the bones feel rotated or collapsed on one side? Use your breath to gently move your vertebrae as if you were rearranging very precious antique furniture in a room. Adjust the vertebrae so they glide over the spinal cord easily.

As you enter the twist, continue to gauge your movement to avoid any irritation to the spinal cord. (While the brain is insensitive to pain, the outer sheath of the spinal cord is richly supplied with sensory nerve fibers.) Imagine that you are initiating the movement from the level of the spinal cord. If you overpower the spine with the strength of the arm, the result will often be irritation in the spine during the twist and an ache in the back long after your practice is finished.

Once the torso has rotated so the chest is facing the left knee, you can take the “hand lock” by bringing the right arm around the outside of the left shin. Link the fingers or, if possible, hold the left wrist with the right hand. Exhale and draw the hands away from the shoulders, allowing the spine to lengthen strongly upward.

If taking the hand lock compresses the front of the abdomen and collapses the spine, you are moving your skeleton further than the organs can support. Be conscious of using your breath to shape the internal organs. Keep the front of the abdomen open and draw the base of the left rib cage in toward the waist to encourage full spinal extension. Turn the head to look back at the foot of the extended leg (Figure 4). Take a few more breaths and then release. Repeat on the other side.

To clarify the distinction between initiating movement from the inner body and dominating with the outer muscles, you might try the previous exercises again, this time exerting force with the outer body by using the arm as a strong lever. Notice the tone in the muscle tissue when you exert force from the outside, as opposed to the quality when you allow the inner body to give to the movement in its own time. Also notice how the two different ways of initiating movement create different psychological states.

Within each of us is a longing for wholeness. Like the spiraling shape of the twist, our spiritual path must constantly turn back toward the still center within us. The longing to “get somewhere” through yoga is a distortion of our true urge to return back to the undifferentiated, unchanging core from which all life expands.

Two Everyday Drink Swaps Are Linked To Less Weight Gain

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Swapping just three drinks a week was linked to less weight gain over four years. What mattered was which drinks people gave up.

Swapping just three drinks a week was linked to less weight gain over four years. What mattered was which drinks people gave up.

Swapping sugary drinks for water is linked to less weight gain over time, according to a study of more than 140,000 adults.

Replacing them with diet drinks shows much the same pattern.

Switching from diet drinks to water was linked to a much smaller difference in weight.

Both swaps could make a difference by cutting the calories people get from their usual soft drinks.

A diet version can also preserve the familiar sweet taste, which may make the change easier for some people to stick with.

The findings suggest that the drink someone gives up may matter more for their weight than which of these two alternatives they choose.

The researchers drew on three long-running US studies that followed nurses and other health professionals for a typical period of 28 years.

Participants regularly reported what they drank and how much they weighed, allowing researchers to see how their drinking habits and weight shifted over four-year periods.

The researchers estimated about 1.4 kg less weight gain over four years when three sugary drinks a week were replaced with either water or diet drinks.

The estimated difference for replacing three diet drinks a week with water was only about a tenth of a kilogram over the same period.

People who cut back on sugary drinks tended to gain less weight whether or not they increased their intake of diet drinks.

The researchers estimated the swaps from changes in people’s reported habits, so the study can show associations but cannot establish how much weight any individual would gain or lose after making the switch.

Replacing a regular soft drink with its diet version changes the calories while keeping much of the experience of having a sweet drink.

Switching to water also removes the sweet taste, which may change how much sweetness people prefer over time.

In a separate year-long randomised trial, people assigned to replace sugary drinks with unsweetened ones showed a greater drop in their preferred level of sweetness than those assigned to artificially sweetened drinks (Ebbeling et al., 2020).

That change in taste preference offers a possible reason why an unsweetened choice might become more appealing with time.

The study was published in the American Journal of Clinical Nutrition (Espinosa et al., 2026).

Psychologist, Jeremy Dean, PhD is the founder and author of PsyBlog. He holds a doctorate in psychology from University College London and two other advanced degrees in psychology. He has been writing about scientific research on PsyBlog since 2004. View all posts by Dr Jeremy Dean

This year’s top-searched holiday toys will make your Millennial heart sing

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Roller skates, wooden trains and a giant floor piano all spiked on Search in 2026. Here’s what parents are shopping for this season.

By Sara Goldstein Updated Nov. 11, 2026

Each November, Google gives us a peek behind the curtain, synthesising billions of shopping-related searches into their Holiday 100 Gift Guide. The list is built from a full year of Search trends across every category from tech to style. And this year, the toys and games section looks suspiciously like the inside of an elder millennial’s childhood toy chest.

Here are the trending toys parents will want to know about, plus what the list says about how we’re shopping this year.

Searches for “classic toy train set” climbed 240% this year, one of the biggest jumps Google shared. Wooden trains survive toddler teeth, being thrown down the stairs and never fail to get passed down more than once. (Expect the figure-eight to claim your living room floor for the next few years.)

“A ton of play,” Google wrote, and we respect the pun. Searches for stuffed elephant plushies rose 240%, with the Pets Alive Baby Elephant Surprise as the featured pick. Plush is a good pick for a 1-year-old and a 10-year-old alike, and the right one becomes a friend for life.

Searches for the Fujifilm instax holiday set jumped 190%. For tweens, a camera that spits out a real photo is a nice break from the phone, and the prints end up taped to bedroom walls instead of buried in a camera roll. They’ll fly through the film, so tuck a couple of extra packs in the box.

Searches for the FAO Schwarz Piano Mat “struck a chord,” rising 180% in 2026. Anyone who watched Tom Hanks stomp out a duet on the giant keyboard in “Big” wanted one of these, and the floor-sized version now fits in a playroom. It’s a sneaky music-and-movement toy for preschoolers. It’s also loud, so choose its permanent residence carefully.

Searches for “adjustable roller skates” cruised up 180%. Adjustable skates expand across several sizes, so they can outlast a growth spurt or two. Just remember to add a helmet and pads to the cart.

Some of this may come down to who’s doing the shopping. Millennials make up a big share of today’s parents, and plenty of us spent our childhoods at the roller rink, building wooden train tracks across the living room rug and waiting for Polaroids to develop. (By impatiently wagging them back and forth, of course.) It’s not hard to imagine a few of these searches coming from parents trying to hand down something that felt magic to them. The best part? There’s a good chance they’ll all still be in rotation when next year’s list comes out.

KTM X-Bow Arrow Straps an Audi Powertrain Into a Nearly-$100,000 Side-by-Side

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The Arrow is the company’s first foray into the side-by-side business, and it’s not holding back.

Here’s one we didn’t see coming: the team over at KTM X-Bow is entering the world of off-roading with a ballistic side-by-side. It’s called the Arrow, and it’s a seriously expensive shot across the bow at segment stalwarts from Polaris and Can-Am.

And we mean it when we say this side-by-side is pricey. The standard model starts at $59,800, while the Black Limited Edition goes for a ludicrous $98,600. For reference, even the quickest side-by-sides from Polaris and Can-Am land in the $50,000 range. KTM X-Bow has its reasons for charging as much as it does, though.

Instead of a revvy engine and CVT, the KTM is fitted with an Audi powertrain. The 2.0-liter turbocharged EA888 inline-four is sourced for motivation, while a seven-speed DSG transmission does the shifting. KTM says the standard model has 280 horsepower, while the Black Limited Edition is pumped up to 330 horsepower. Both models have front and rear limited-slip differentials, and the Limited Edition adds a hydraulic fly-off handbrake to the equation. You also get a fully enclosed cabin and air conditioning in the more expensive model, but Sabelt seats with a harness system come standard in both versions.

KTM says it built a chromoly steel chassis with double wishbone suspension front and rear. It uses shocks developed with KW Reiger, which will offer up to 23.6 inches of travel. Development work was heavily influenced by those involved with KTM X-Bow’s racing expertise; Dakar Rally and Rally-Raid drivers Guillaume de Mévius and Puck Klaassen helped dial the Arrow in during testing.

“As a driver, what immediately stands out about the Arrow is how connected it feels to the terrain,” said Klaassen. “You have the performance and responsiveness you expect from a competition vehicle, but with a level of comfort and refinement that makes it just as exciting to drive outside of a race environment. It feels like a completely different approach to what a side-by-side can be.”

The Black Limited Edition will naturally be restricted in production. KTM says it’s planning to sell 500 numbered vehicles worldwide through a reservation system, but the standard model won’t be limited. Initial deliveries are planned for late 2027, and reservations will open on November 17.

The Chandra X-Ray Observatory Unmasks a Supernova Impostor

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

When is an apparent supernova not really a supernova at all? That's a tough question to answer, especially since no supernovae have been observed in the Milky Way in the telescope era. The ones that astronomers see are mostly many millions of light years away in distant galaxies.

SN 1987A was the closest modern supernova, but it exploded in the Large Magellanic Cloud, which is still more than 160,000 light years away. And that was before the Hubble launched, and studying its aftermath was difficult. Now we have automated supernova surveys that scan the sky for exploding stars. One such survey detected what seemed to be a supernovae in 2016, in a distant galaxy.

Sometimes, a star exhibits variations in brightness before it explodes as supernovae, and that can create confusion around what's actually happening. That describes the object detected in 2016.

That object was a massive star in a distant galaxy that's showing behaviour associated with a typical supernova explosion. Supernovae explosions are the final act of massive stars that reach the end of their lives, and their prodigious energy output can light up the sky. That's what this star does, sort of.

But unlike a supernova with a single explosion, this star has a volatile outburst every 113 days, something that no supernova can repeat. In an effort to understand what's behind these repeated outbursts, astronomers turned to one of NASA's workhorse space telescopes, the Chandra X-ray Observatory. Chandra's exceptional angular resolution sets it apart from other x-ray observatories, and it can pick out faint sources in detail at great distances.

The volatile star, named AT 2016blu, has about 33 solar masses and is in the spiral galaxy NGC 4559, around 30 million light years away. AT stands for astronomical transient, and blu is a sequential suffix that tells us the star was the 1,685th transient reported in the year 2016. AT 2016blu is also called a luminous blue variable (LBV), and was first discovered in 2012 by an automated supernova search.

LBVs are rare, and as noted above, exhibit powerful variations in both their brightness and their spectra. Their variability is usually considered to be unpredictable, but in this case, it's periodic.

New research in The Astrophysical Journal has an explanation for AT 2016blu. It's titled "AT 2016blu: Accretion-powered Outbursts in a Luminous Blue Variable and Compact Object Binary," and the lead author is Mojgan Aghakhanloo, a Virginia Institute of Theoretical Astronomy Origins Fellow at the University of Virginia in Charlottesville.

"We present the first X-ray detection of the supernova (SN) impostor AT 2016blu in NGC 4559," the authors write.

Astronomers are calling AT 2016blu a 'supernova impostor' because LBVs are often detected in supernova searches. "Supernova (SN) impostors are transient events associated with massive stars that are often initially discovered in SN surveys," the authors explain. "However, because their outbursts are less energetic and less luminous than true SNe, and because the star is expected to survive, they are subsequently classified as SN impostors."

Unlike a supernove, AT 2016blu appears to have a nearby companion that triggers the outbursts.

"AT 2016blu exhibited 27 detected quasiperiodic (â¼113 day) outbursts between 2012 and 2026 June, presumably triggered by periastron passages in an eccentric binary system in which the primary is a â³33 Mâ luminous blue variable (LBV)," the authors explain.

Chandra had observed AT 2016blu by accident in 2001-2002, but that was before its first documented outburst. Those observations were actually aimed at another nearby ultraluminous X-ray source (ULX), NGC 4559 X7. XMM-Newton, an ESA x-ray observatory, also observed AT 2016blu four times while looking at NGC 4559 X7. So there's a collection of happenstance observations of the region.

âFor 15 years, astronomers have been watching this star get really bright and then dim again on a regular basis,â said lead author Aghakhanloo. âUntil we were able to get Chandra data at the right time, we couldnât figure out what was making this happen.â

Overall, astronomers observed 27 outbursts of optical light from the supernova impostor between 2012 and 2026. That's one burst about every 113 days, something no supernova can do, since they're destroyed in their explosive outbursts.

âThis starâs behavior was confusing at first because it kept acting like a supernova, but clearly itâs not because it keeps surviving these outbursts about three times a year,â said co-author NÃºria Torres-AlbÃ , also of the University of Virginia. âTo understand the cause of these eruptions, we realized we needed X-ray data that was timed so that weâd observe the star when it was in an outburst.â

Once it was known to exhibit outbursts, the researchers watched it in optical light leading up to its next predicted outburst. Then they used Chandra to observe it in x-rays, since it's the only x-ray telescope powerful enough to discern AT 2016blu from the other nearby x-ray source, ULX NGC 4559 X7, which is much brighter than the supernova impostor. "We monitored the sourceâs visual-wavelength variability around its predicted outburst time and triggered a Chandra Target of Opportunity (ToO) program in 2026 March, obtaining observations with detections over five closely spaced epochs," the authors write.

The left panel shows a stacked Chandra image of all five target-of-opportunity observations taken during the 2026 March optical outburst of AT 2016blu, in a span of 4 days. The right panel is an XMM-Newton image of the same sky region. XMM-Newton is not as powerful as Chandra, and cannot resolve the two sources. The image is dominated by ULX NGC 4559 X7, much brighter than AT 2016blu. Image Credit: Mojgan Aghakhanloo et al 2026 ApJ 1011 252

âWe suspected that the bright repeating flashes at optical wavelengths might actually be powered by periodic blasts of high-energy X-rays,â said co-author Nathan Smith of the University of Arizona in Tucson. âThe dense gas surrounding the system is heated by the X-rays and then glows in the optical. So, we needed to plan the Chandra X-ray observations carefully to be simultaneous with an optical outburst.â

Chandra's x-ray data from its 2026 outburst shows that AT 2016blu is 100 times brighter in x-rays than in its data from 2001 to 2002. This is best explained by a binary companion. "These outbursts are most likely triggered by periastron encounters in an eccentric binary system in which the primary is an LBV," the authors write. The secondary is almost certainly a neutron star or a black hole. As they orbit each other, the LBV ejects material that finds its way to the secondary. The material orbits the secondary at very high speeds in an accretion ring, and that heats it up until it emits x-rays.

Based on the x-ray luminosity, the researchers estimate that about 2.7% of Earth's mass is transferred during each outburst.

As is often the case, the researchers had to rule out a potential background source, and for that they relied on Chandra, too. "The alternative to a genuine counterpart to AT 2016blu would be a background quasar with a coincident position," the researchers explain. "This quasar, furthermore, would have brightened at a time coincident with our observations, and thus the AT 2016blu outburst."

These panels are all Chandra images. The first five are from Chandra's observations of AT 2016blu during the ToO campaign, and it's clearly visible during its outbursts. The sixth panel is a stacked image of the region outside of an outburst, clearly showing no background quasar. "No source is detected in the stacked archival images prior to the first observed optical outburst of AT 2016blu, when it was a stable system," the researchers explain. Image Credit: Mojgan Aghakhanloo et al 2026 ApJ 1011 252

"AT 2016blu represents the first observationally known LBV system with a compact companion," the authors write. "Previously studied LBVs either lack X-ray detections or show luminosities consistent with windâwind collisions in massive-star binaries."

Its discovery could shed light on other, similar transient phenomena. Many stars show changes in brightness before they explode as supernovae, and maybe some of them are similar to AT 2016bu. "The discovery of an LBVâcompact-object binary undergoing accretion-powered outbursts also connects AT 2016blu to a growing class of models in which massive starâcompact object binaries explain a variety of pre-SN transient phenomena," the authors explain.

The next step, as is often the case in observations of new types of astrophysical objects, is to find more of them.

"Expanding the sample of such systems is therefore essential to better understand the evolutionary pathways and physical mechanisms connecting SN impostors, LBVs, and interacting SNe," the researchers conclude.

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

United Airlines CEO Talks Starlink In Veterans Day Message

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

United Airlines CEO Scott Kirby used his Veterans Day message to thank the 8,500 veterans who work at the airline. But before turning to their service, he spent a substantial part of the message discussing Starlink, winning customers from competitors, and his belief that United is the best airline in the world. The opening offers another glimpse into what Kirby sees as the opportunity ahead in 2027.

I typically share Kirby’s internal Veterans Day message each year (he also posted publicly on LinkedIN). His appreciation for military service and his affection for the United States are familiar themes. This year, though, he began with a thank-you for the summer operation and a look ahead at how better Wi-Fi might persuade passengers to switch airlines.

Happy Veterans Day everyone. Before I start on my Veterans Day message, I want to thank everyone for a really incredible summer. All the hard work you do. There was a lot of weather this summer, but the United team just did a great job so thank you. I also want to tell you how excited I am for 2027. In 2027 we’re going have a unique advantage in Starlink. And what that means is we’re gonna have a lot of customers who are loyal to other airlines today try us because of Starlink and I can already can hear this from customers. What’s going to happen is I hope they’re going to realize that United really is the best airline in the world when they come try United. We’re gonna be able to get those customers to come to United because of Starlink but to stay with United because everything else United is better. So thank you in advance for what you’re gonna do to win new brand loyal customers in 2027 for us at United.

But what I really wanted to do today is thank the 8,500 veterans that work here at United Airlines. I think all of you know that I am deeply patriotic. I love this country. I feel fortunate to have been born in this country and think it’s the greatest country in the history of the planet, but that wouldn’t be the case if it wasn’t for all the veterans who work at United but all the other veterans and the people that serve today. So to all the veterans not just at United, but around the world who have served to put their lives on the line to keep this country free and great, thank you for your service. Thank you for everything that you’ve done. And today when you’re out working and you see a veteran, and I’m sure you will because there’s so many United, give them a special thank you for their service. So to everyone at United, thank you for everything that you’ve done. And the veterans, a special thank you on Veteran’s Day and I look forward to seeing you in the friendly skies.

The commercial opening is an unusual preface to a Veterans Day tribute, though Kirby explicitly separates it from the message that follows. That makes sense considering he is addressing all employees and using the opportunity to discuss more than one subject. Still, his emphasis is revealing.

Kirby sees Starlink as a reason for passengers loyal to other airlines to give United a try, which is likely true. The more ambitious part is his expectation that the rest of the experience will persuade them to stay.

Reliable, fast internet can absolutely influence my booking decision. If I need to work during a flight, connectivity is more important than modest differences in the seat or meal and I’m hardly alone.

The second proposition is true, though certainly easier said than done. A passenger attracted by Starlink still has to navigate the airport, interact with the crew, and arrive at the destination. A great internet connection may get someone to book once. How United handles that journey, especially when something goes wrong, will help determine whether the passenger returns.

I remain unconvinced that there is one universally “best” airline. But Kirby is giving his employees a clear assignment: use the opportunity created by Starlink to earn repeat business. That is indeed a useful objective and to Untied’s credit, it improving operational performance and overall onboard product are giving front-line staff the means to deliver.

Turning to Veterans Day, Kirby’s message is direct. He credits those who have served with protecting the country he loves and asks employees to thank the veterans they encounter at work. I appreciate that practical request and will do the same today in my own interactions (I’m not flying, though).

Gratitude does not require agreement with every military deployment or government decision. We can (and should) scrutinize those decisions while appreciating the people asked to carry them out and the sacrifices their service entails.

Kirby’s Veterans Day message combines a familiar expression of patriotism with an enthusiastic commercial outlook for 2027. His expectation that Starlink will bring new passengers through the door does indeed create a golden opportunity for United. Whether United earns their loyalty will depend on what happens beyond the Wi-Fi connection.

But today’s central message deserves its own acknowledgment. To the veterans at United Airlines and elsewhere, thank you for your service.

Ferrari F40 Driver in Hong Kong Arrested After Nearly Colliding With Double-Decker Bus

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The F40’s operator was booked on suspicion of dangerous driving, an offense punishable by up to three years in prison.

It’s almost unquestionable that the Ferrari F40 is one of the most desirable supercars ever made. A big part of its appeal is the absence of features that interfere with the connection between car and driver: there's no traction control, no ABS, not even carpeting. One thing the F40 does have, however, is windows. Apparently, however, a driver in Hong Kong forgot to look through them before pulling out in front of a double-decker bus. His F40 escaped an expensive crash, but he might not be as lucky when it comes to avoiding jail time.

According to Dim Sum Daily, Hong Kong police arrested a 38-year-old man on November 9 on suspicion of dangerous driving. The incident occurred two days earlier on Repulse Bay Road; videos of the near crash began to circulate online, and prompted police to investigate. The video shows the red F40 emerging from a side road, apparently ignoring signs and pavement markings requiring traffic to yield. The driver of the double-decker bus reacted quickly and slammed on the brakes to avoid hitting the multi-million-dollar Ferrari broadside.

Hong Kong Island Regional Traffic Investigation Unit reviewed the footage, and identified the vehicle and suspected driver. Officers then arrested the suspect in the Aberdeen area; police said they intend to pursue prosecution for dangerous driving.

In Hong Kong, dangerous driving is a serious offense. A conviction can result in a maximum fine of HK$25,000—approximately $3200 at current exchange rates—and up to three years in prison. Driving a 478-horsepower Ferrari with a 200-mph top speed and not an airbag in sight demands your complete concentration in any country, however. Thankfully, the bus driver in this situation was paying attention to the road; now the Ferrari driver may have to explain to a court exactly what he was doing instead of watching where his F40 was going.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/hospitals/bjc-health-st-lukes-reveal-plans-merge" hreflang="en">BJC Health, St. Luke's reveal plans to merge</a>

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →
BJC Health, St. Luke's reveal plans to merge" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Missouri-based BJC Health and St. Luke's Hospital are planning a merger.

The providers announced on Wednesday that they have signed a letter of intent to come together, bringing the community hospital into the fold with the Show Me State's largest health system. The pair expects the deal to improve access and improve local care in the St. Louis region.

St. Luke's said in the announcement that the letter "follows a deliberate and thorough evaluation process," conducted by its board of directors and leaders "to identify the best path forward for St. Luke’s and the communities it serves."

The providers said they intend to spend the next several months building a definitive agreement to merge and complete the needed regulatory approval process. In the interim, St. Luke's patients can continue to see their regular physicians.

BJC said in the announcement that it is committed to supporting local, community care while also allowing St. Luke's to tap into the resources available across its ecosystem. It intends to provide key support for the hospital's clinical technology and equipment as well as backing infrastructure and facility upgrades and provider recruitment and retention efforts.

St. Luke’s is a well-respected hospital, known for clinical quality, patient experience and the personal attention given to everyone in our care,” said Andy Bagnall, CEO of St. Luke’s, in the announcement. “At the same time, the demands of health care delivery and our communities’ needs are changing rapidly."

We believe that together with BJC, we can strengthen patient care in St. Louis in ways we could not easily do on our own," Bagnall said.

Nick Barto, CEO of BJC Health, called St. Luke's "a trusted leader in our region with a reputation for excellence."

"Together, we will build on that foundation through our aligned clinical strengths and complementary services," Barto said in the announcement. "Our shared goal is to expand access to differentiated, high-quality care while ensuring St. Luke’s can continue serving the growing communities of West St. Louis for generations to come.”

Volunteer to play the ‘bad guy’ for the TSA’s bomb-sniffing dogs

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 11, 2026 4:36 PM EST

Want to play with dogs at the airport and have it be for a good cause? Well, boy, do we have the volunteer gig for you. It’s actually less playtime and more very serious Transportation Security Administration (TSA) work, but there’s still dogs, so we consider that a win: The TSA is currently looking for volunteers to be explosive detection K9 decoys, and honestly there may not be a cuter way to spend a weekend. Let these very smart dogs show us all just how smart they really are!

In their call for volunteers, the TSA says that a volunteer shift with these crafty canines “may feel like a high-stakes game of hide and seek.” They also emphasize that “this training is a serious and important part of keeping our canine teams ready for duty.”

You’re basically pretending to be an airport bad guy or, as the TSA puts it, “a covert role player.” You might be tasked with carrying a “training item” and wander around an airport acting like you’re just an everyday passenger in the ticketing lobbies, going through security, or just generally waiting around for their boarding time. Then, the real game begins, and the canine team will try to sniff you out.

The entire set-up is meant to mimic what these dogs might face in the real world.

The entire volunteer shift takes about two to three hours.

There aren’t a ton of restrictions on who can volunteer with these very smart doggies. You have to be at least 18 years old to volunteer. Although minors can volunteer alongside an adult companion, they can only participate in limited ways.

You also have to be comfortable sitting, walking, and moving around an airport. However, wheelchair and scooter users can participate in the program.

You can check out their annual calendar, which will be available in November or December. A German shorthaired pointer named Bolt won the TSA’S Cutest Canine Contest for 2026 and will be one of the working dogs featured on the 2027 calendar.

Martin Solar cuts out zip ties with new mounting rail

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Martin Solar‘s newly manufactured Dual-Channel Solar Rail (DCSR) was recently installed on a 70-kWDC solar project, marking its first real-world application.

The Dual-Channel Solar Rail from Martin Solar.

“Management of PV wires and AC trunk cables is one of the most important aspects of rooftop PV installation. Anyone who has ever experienced running hundreds, thousands of feet of conductors on a rooftop system knows what a hassle it is having to apply countless zip ties that are not really suitable for long-term wire management,” said Preston Nelson, director of Martin Solar and co-inventor of DCSR. “What’s worse than the initial application of zip ties is having to pull up finished solar panels to apply even more zip ties after an inspector inevitably cites an installation for having wires that droop down to the roof surface.”

DCSR is designed with a wire trough large enough to fit three-phase trunk cables, like those manufactured by Enphase, Hoymiles, and AP Systems. It can also fit up to twenty standard PV cables, without the use of zip ties. Instead of using specialized panel clamps, DCSR’s panel attachment channel is off to the side of the wire trough, where it separately accepts panel hardware.

“The dual-channel design with separately dedicated features allowed us to increase the cross-sectional area of the wire trough to a size never seen before as a rail feature. In the past, we would install a separate wire tray to manage numerous long trunk cable runs, which was even more expensive and time-consuming. Now, this present project installation involves over 600 total feet of trunk, without a single cable tie. The separate wire trough can even allow access to wire runs without lifting panels, especially convenient when a worker simply needs to track a PV string,” Nelson said.

DCSR is the first product accessory to Martin Solar’s Hidden-Fastener Solar Mount, which mounts to the roof surface without putting holes in shingles. While Martin’s proprietary solar rail and mount can be paired, both are compatible with other solar racking and mounts.

Martin Solar will show DCSR and its solar mounting system at RE+ from November 17 to 19 at booth C5071.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

34 Unique Christmas Tree Themes That'll Inspire You This Holiday Season

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

From bejeweled ornaments to popcorn garlands, these ideas bring major holiday cheer.

When it comes to Christmas tree decor, some folks keep it classic, using the same ornaments every year, while others choose new themes. Both approaches capture the charm and spirit of the season. However, if you find yourself in the latter cohort, you know how hard it can be to find yearly inspiration that will wow your guests and blend with your existing decor. So, if you’re in need of a muse, we’ve curated a list of tree themes so stunning, you’ll want to repeat them next year.

While many people stick to the traditional charm of classic hues, this year offers bold alternatives. From glittering jeweled ornaments to vintage-inspired touches like tinsel, popcorn garlands, and flocked branches, social feeds are buzzing with fresh, trendy ideas and new possibilities. There's a lot of Christmas creativity to spread the cheer this year.

To spark your imagination, we've compiled 34 merry and bright Christmas tree themes that are sure to bring jolly spirits. Whether you're drawn to the rustic farmhouse aesthetic or punchy plum color trends, these seasonal styles will infuse your home with magic and joy—and as a bonus, they look fantastic on both real and faux firs.

A Christmas tree can quickly become crowded, so take inspiration from Anthropologie and Terrain's holiday house and swap your standard ornaments for oversized baubles. These larger, multicolored orbs make a true statement, especially when paired with vibrant lights or flocked branches.

Enjoying a warm drink after a long day of skiing was the inspiration for this miniature tree. Featuring gloves, boots, and a Santa with his own pair of skis, take inspiration and decorate a tree dedicated to your favorite seasonal hobby.

For those who like their ornaments to be matchy-matchy, it might be time to start collecting. Anthropologie displayed an adorable miniature tree decked out with festive mice. It's accompanied by a village fit for their Christmas-ready friends.

Ideal for the girly-girl in your life, or maybe even a coastal holiday setting, think outside of the box and abandon the evergreen altogether this year. Instead, take inspiration from design firm BEFoundCollective's white Christmas tree, decorated with cheerful pastel ornaments.

There are few things cozier than a flocked Christmas tree. This one by Pottery Barn is adorned with cozy and metallic ornaments like stars, birds, and even pinecones. This Christmas tree theme is great for a mountain or woodsy cabin, but you can even bring that countryside Christmas energy into your city apartment.

A soothing yet vibrant shade, you can't go wrong with a Christmas tree decked out in peach and orange tones. The silver tinsel contrasts perfectly while adding some glitz and glam. This tree, by the design firm BEFoundCollective in Virginia, showcases vintage ornaments among the lush foliage.

Interior designer Beata Heuman ties dozens of bows on the branches of her showroom's Christmas tree for easy and low-cost DIY decorations. Use ribbons in the traditional Christmas color scheme, match your interior's decor, or, like Heuman, choose hues from the entire rainbow.

Perfect for warmer climates (or those wishing spring would circle back around already), forgo traditional ornaments and adorn your tree with faux florals. Take inspiration from the Winterthur Museum, Garden, and Library in Delaware and use more voluminous blooms like hydrangeas as filler between smaller flowers like mums or daffodils.

A classic color scheme beloved by modern, vintage, and even Art Deco lovers, a black and gold palette is timeless. Make your tree stand tall with a bold trunk cover and top with a snowflake-inspired star to tie your entire decor scheme together.

Topped with a cheerful snowman, this Christmas tree theme is all about the seasonal icons we've come to love. Gnomes and mini Santas stand out against the lush foliage of the tree while striped baubles and twinkling stars glam up the traditional red-and-white color palette.

If your little one is still running around singing "Part of Your World" from The Little Mermaid, pay tribute to their favorite princess with a sophisticated Christmas tree theme. Chock-full of glittering mermaids, chic metal whales, and glossy sea turtles, this tree designed by Pottery Barn is the perfect way to bring the film to life for the holidays.

While winter whites always get the bulk of the attention, blue is the other color of the season we're suggesting you embrace. Opt for chinoiserie-style ornaments and a decadent velvet ribbon garland to make it feel just as cozy as red and green.

Perfect for the diva of the family, this Anthropologie-designed Christmas tree is all about glamorous, textured accents like peacock feathers, Depression-style glass, and velvet accents. Christmas is allowed to be over-the-top!

The summer of Barbie may be over, but that joyous love of all things pink can (and should) continue all year long. If you're feeling as bold as Margot Robbie's iconic character, go all in on a bright pink tree with gold and white accents. Like Barbie, your Christmas tree can be anything you want it to.

If you're always looking at moon phases and tarot cards, bring that love of all things astrological to your Christmas decorations this year. Golden crescent moons, flying doves, and iron-star-studded wreaths are incorporated in this flocked tree. Add warm-hued string lights, and the decorations take on an ethereal glow.

Classics are classics for a reason—and nothing is more instantly festive than a hallmark red-and-white Christmas tree. This bountiful beauty was decked by Inspired by Charm, who included lodge-inspired elements like buffalo check wrapping paper and wooden skis to make it feel fresh and fanciful.

If a streamlined and sleek aesthetic is more your style, use this Christmas tree by For the Home as inspiration. The intentionally sparse look nods to traditional Scandinavian spruces, which feature less dense branches and are left mostly bare save for a few basic ball ornaments and twinkling white lights.

Why choose just two colors when you can have all of them? This Technicolor tree created by Serretta Style leans into a more-is-more aesthetic with tiers of ornaments in every color of the rainbow. While decorations in the same shapes and designs are used here (just in different shades), you could get the same effect by hanging your existing ornaments by color from top to bottom.

The weather outside may be frightful, but it certainly inspires a delightful Christmas tree design! Nod to all things snow and ice with a winter-wonderland-themed evergreen. This scene designed by Lolly Jane combines a flocked tree with an assortment of white and silver ornaments—like plush "snowballs" and frosty pinecones—for a snowy and chic scene. Plenty of warm white lights and a pair of ice skates complete the charming look.

Anthropic’s highly anticipated IPO, ticker ANT, is set to price in a $70–$78 range and begin trading on Nasdaq around mid-to-late November 2026, with a targeted valuation as high as $1.4 trillion. However, in a wild turn of events, Anthropic’s own Claude AI predicts that its own share price could tank upon launch.

Yet in an over-the-top bearish scenario, the stock could suffer one of the most dramatic post-IPO collapses in modern market history by January 1, 2027. The landmark catalyst that unlocks this downside is a highly unlikely but not impossible convergence of events: a high-profile safety incident or regulatory action involving Claude models coincides with the deepening fallout from the Nvidia CEO scandal and a broader political backlash against frontier AI.

In this hypothetical, revelations of serious alignment failures or misuse cases trigger emergency congressional hearings, restrictive new rules from multiple governments, and a sudden freeze in enterprise adoption. Combined with already fragile sentiment around massive AI infrastructure spending, the narrative flips from “indispensable technology” to “overhyped liability,” prompting a rapid and severe derating of the entire sector.

(SOURCE: Claude AI Predicts a Disastrous Anthropic IPO)

Because ANT has not yet begun trading, pure technical analysis is necessarily forward-looking. In the bearish path, the stock would open with an initial pop or modest premium but quickly form a series of lower highs as institutional sellers and lock-up-related supply emerge.

A failure to hold the IPO price range as support, followed by a break below key psychological levels such as $60 and then $50, would confirm a distribution pattern. Volume spikes on down days, deteriorating relative strength versus the Nasdaq, and a rapid slide into oversold RSI territory would support the case for further downside, especially if the broader AI complex continues to de-rate.

An extreme safety or regulatory shock layered on top of that environment could produce the kind of violent repricing rarely seen in large-cap technology debuts.

A wobbly exoplanet vanished—but astronomers aren’t worried

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 11, 2026 4:01 PM EST

For the first time, astronomers witnessed a gigantic exoplanet dramatically vanish from view. But the “hot Jupiter” located about 1,000 light-years from Earth didn’t evaporate into the vacuum of space. Instead, the gravitational pull from an uncomfortably close neighbor is causing TOI-2535 b to wobble in its orbit thanks to the same dynamics dictating solar and lunar eclipses. The unusual discovery is detailed in a study recently published in Astrophysical Journal Letters, and relies on observations gathered by NASA’s Transiting Exoplanet Survey Satellite (TESS) orbital telescope.

“We think of the universe as resembling clockwork, where everything is regular, but that’s not true,” Samuel Yee, a University of California Los Angeles (UCLA) astronomer and study co-author, explained in a recent profile. “If there are multiple planets in a system, they can affect each other’s orbits.”

Astronomers first began examining TOI-2535 b in April 2019 using TESS. The eagle-eyed telescope identifies new planets by flagging subtle changes in stellar brightness. These changes are often caused by a previously unknown planet’s transit in front of its star as seen from Earth. In some ways, TOI-2535 b resembles our solar system’s largest planet, but it is even closer to its own star than Mercury is to our sun. While Mercury takes 88 days to complete the trip, TOI-2535 b circles back around once every 8.25 days.

While using TESS to get glimpses of TOI-2535 b over the next seven years, researchers realized the hot Jupiter’s transits didn’t occur on a regular schedule like most other planets. Instead, the exoplanet’s light fluctuations could arrive upwards of six hours earlier or later than predicted. Over time, astronomers also noticed these transits become shallower and shorter. Then, in March, TOI-2535 b simply didn’t show up during the space telescope’s scan of the system.

Researchers including Yee now believe their hot Jupiter isn’t alone—it actually has an incredibly close gas giant neighbor with a comparatively tilted orbit. The two planets’ relationship causes them to essentially wobble like a pair of tops, eventually throwing one another out of alignment so that they disappear from Earth’s viewpoint.

“This is the most extreme example we’ve seen because the two planets are interacting so strongly,” said Yee. “The planet’s orbit is tilting so quickly that its transit disappeared while we were watching.”

The forces at play are the same as those that ensure eclipses don’t follow a regularly repeating cycle. UCLA astrophysicist and study co-author Erik Petigura explained that this is due to the moon’s tilted orbit, which comes from its cataclysmic early formation following a collision with Earth.

“If the moon’s orbit around the Earth was perfectly aligned with the Earth’s orbit around the sun, we would see an eclipse every month,” Petigura explained.

Yee, Petigura, and their colleagues are lucky to have spotted TOI-2535’s disappearing act when they did. Based on their calculations, its next transit in front of its star won’t occur for another 30 to 50 years—although they may see its partner planet sooner once it emerges from behind its stellar anchor.

The warning came after Tuesday’s October inflation report ran hotter than expected for a third month in a row. In Bitcoin news, the coin trades at $73,888 on Binance, down 0.6% in 24 hours. It dropped to $72,727 on Tuesday after the report, its lowest price since August 20.

The bank still expects the Fed to raise rates by 0.25 percentage points, or 25 basis points (bps), at its December 8–9 meeting. But it says the case for a bigger move is growing, and it calls the Fed’s decision to hold rates in October a possible policy mistake.

Traders are starting to listen. On Polymarket, the odds of a hike of 50 bps or more in December rose from 0.6% on Monday to 11.4% on Wednesday.

BANK OF AMERICA WARNS FED COULD DELIVER 50BPS HIKE IN DECEMBER

Bank of America says October's hot CPI report virtually guarantees a December Fed rate hike — and opens the door to a larger 50bps move.

Headline CPI rose 0.4%, while core CPI climbed 0.3%, marking a third…

— *Walter Bloomberg (@DeItaone) November 11, 2026

The bank’s call rests on three months of hot data. Consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, and core prices, which leave out food and energy because they swing the most, rose 0.3%. Both beat forecasts, the third upside surprise in a row in the Bureau of Labor Statistics report.

Bank of America now estimates that core PCE inflation, the Fed’s preferred measure, is running at 3.3% a year. The Fed’s target is 2%. The bank says price rises are spreading from goods to services, with electronics, airfares and hotels leading the increases.

Its base case is still a 25 bps hike in December. The change is in the risks: a 50 bps move is now possible, and the bank sees a growing chance of another hike in January instead of March.

The bank is not alone. Fed Governor Christopher Waller said in a speech on Tuesday morning that he leans toward a 25 bps hike in December. If core inflation came in around 0.3% again, he said, a 50 bps hike “should be considered.” It did.

Higher rates make cash and bonds pay more. That tends to pull money out of risky assets like BTC, and a faster pace of hikes pulls harder.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best New Crypto to Hodl

The size of the hike has mattered for BTC. We measured Bitcoin’s price on Binance from the 2 p.m. ET announcement of each Fed rate hike since 2022 to one week later. That covers 12 hikes: six of 50 bps or more and six of 25 bps.

After the six big hikes, BTC was lower a week later five times. The median move was a 6% drop. The worst came after the 50 bps hike in May 2022, when BTC fell 23% in a week.

After the six 25 bps hikes, the picture was milder. BTC was lower a week later four times, but the median move was a drop of just 0.6%.

The record comes with a caveat. All six big hikes happened in 2022, a bear market that also saw the collapses of Terra in May and FTX in November. Those crashes made the falls after the May and November hikes worse.

The most recent hike was a 25 bps move on September 16. BTC rose 11.3% in the week after it, as the uncertainty that had built up before the meeting cleared. That makes the size of December’s hike, more than the hike itself, the Bitcoin news to watch.

DISCOVER: Bitcoin CPI Reaction: 10 of 13 Cool Inflation Reports Triggered an Hourly Gain – Can Inflation Data Reverse the Downtrend?

BTC trades at $73,888, down 0.6% in 24 hours and 5.8% over the past week. It sits below both of its exponential moving averages (EMAs), averages that give recent prices more weight, on the daily chart. The 50-day EMA stands at $78,897 and the 200-day EMA at $76,449.

For Bitcoin news, the next test is the Fed’s own message. Traders already expect a 25 bps hike. If more officials join Waller and Bank of America in backing 50 bps, BTC may have to defend $72,727 before the December 9 decision.

Florida's 'dark magic' mushrooms have a deadly combination of chemicals never seen before

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →
