The Miami Heat Dropped Brand-New Jerseys Just in Time For Grand Theft Auto VI

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Growing up in Oakland, Miami Heat forward Keshad Johnson and his brother Kenny used to sneak away from their parents to play Grand Theft Auto. “It caused some fights between me and him on turns,” Johnson says. “We used to play that if you crash the car, you gotta pass the sticks.”

For many of us who came of age in the early aughts, sneaking away from our parents to play GTA was a rite of passage. And since the first edition of the game dropped in 1997, at least part of the thrill of this fictional world was often tied to the clandestine affair. But next week, Johnson won’t be hiding from anybody. Instead, he’ll be part of a once-in-a-lifetime Grand Theft Auto experience when he and his Heat teammates take the court in special-edition Vice City jerseys inspired by the game.

Grand Theft Auto VI, Vice City, launches on November 19th. But the night before, when the Heat and superstar Giannis Antetokounmpo take on his former team, the Milwaukee Bucks, they'll be ringing in what is by most accounts the biggest video game launch of all time with a special event that only Rockstar Games and the Heat could pull off. “My whole family is trying to get a Vice City jersey,” Johnson said when we spoke last weekend.

“We're bringing sports and entertainment together in a way that's never been done,” adds Miami Heat Chief Creative Officer Jennifer Alvarez. “And we're going to deliver an experience that no one has actually had at an NBA game.”

More on that in a second. But let’s start with the kits: all black, with bright pink accents that pop like neon signs in the South Beach sky. The word “Heat” is splashed across the front. “It's kind of like the outlaw type vibe,” Johnson said. “That's what we want to give when we're on the court.”

Hidden inside the letters and jersey numbers is a map of Miami streets. They were designed by Nike and Travis Scott’s Cactus Jack, whose logos are featured on the jersey and shorts. As the whole campaign makes clear, details matter.

“I feel like Vice City has its own fashion culture,” says Heat star Bam Adebayo.

The jerseys will be available for purchase on game night at the arena, where fans will be transported into the video game itself upon entering the Kaseya Center, whose plaza will be made over with a light-blue Ferrari and turquoise Chevrolet Monte Carlo, just two of the sports cars one can drive in GTA.

A sign hoisted atop the arena will read “Welcome to Vice City.” In fact, Mitchell was late to practice one day because his usual route was blocked by a helicopter that’d been deployed to help install it. “It shows how much time and effort they're trying to put into this new game,” Mitchell says. “This is probably gonna be the greatest game of all time.”

Though everyone involved has kept tight-lipped about what happens once inside the arena, fans attending Heat games have already been able to order burgers from Burger Shot and chicken from Cluckin' Bell, two fictional fast-food chains in the Grand Theft Auto universe. “It's a full 360 experience. I can't wait for people to really understand what that means,” Alvarez said, coyly. “And that's all I can say about it.”

One can understand the secrecy, since the Heat’s Vice City takeover has been two years in the making. It all started when Rockstar Games dropped the trailer for Vice City in December 2023. A digital Kaseya Center can be seen in the first 30 seconds.

“When we saw that we were in the trailer, we thought we should figure something out,” Alvarez recalls. “Because we've been synonymous with the word Vice since 2017."

The Heat launched the Miami Vice campaign in 2017 with its acclaimed Original Vice jerseys that set the standard for NBA teams launching City Edition jerseys. Those campaigns went beyond the uniforms and included local activations like custom license plates and a fashion line created in collaboration with local artists. Over the years, the Heat launched five separate Vice jerseys, each campaign expanding on previous ones. Ask those with the Heat, and they’d agree that this latest installment is the biggest one yet. “It feels like all of it built up to this,” Alvarez said.

To be clear, the Heat don’t look at this as an extension of their own Vice line. Rather, this is meant to be as exclusive as any streetwear brand launch. Also, it’s not easy to transport fans into a video game every single night. Go big, then go home.

Meanwhile, Rockstar has infiltrated Miami, turning the city that inspired Vice City into Vice City itself. Burger Shot came to life through a collaboration with Skinny Louie, a local smash-burger joint that has rebranded their brick-and-mortar (and arena) locations for the launch. Rockstar Games has activations throughout the city, including in Downtown, Little Haiti and the famous Wynwood Walls. It’s an Inception-style, video game outside the video game-type thing that crescendos with the Heat game on the eve of the Grand Theft Auto VI launch.

Players got their first look at the jerseys and the campaign during a team meeting in early October. After hours of presentations about business operations, media responsibilities and other team matters, the energy in the room sparked when Alvarez took the microphone and showed the first Vice City slide. The players locked in. A few even stood up and cheered.

Mitchell, for his part, was taken back to his childhood, when he would come home from his grandma’s house and play Grand Theft Auto until it was time for bed. For a while, Mitchell became obsessed, playing GTA while other popular games like Call of Duty and NBA 2K collected dust.

“I just played that all the time, doing missions, but also just running around,” he remembers. “It's kind of like your own world in a way.”

And next week, he’ll get to enter it IRL.

Ocarina of Time is even better if you use a smartphone app

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I never liked hunting for Golden Skulltulas in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. The game has 100 of them tucked all over Hyrule, and even over my many hours with the N64 classic growing up, I can’t remember ever actually finding all of them, or even wanting to. Jumping into the Ocarina of Time remake, I expected to ignore the Golden Skulltulas once again. But while poking around Nintendo’s Zelda Notes companion app, I discovered that its maps are a huge help to tracking down all of the elusive golden spiders — and pretty much every other collectible in the game — and now I find myself constantly opening up the app to make sure I haven’t missed a secret.

Inside Zelda Notes, which is available in the free Nintendo Switch app (you don’t need a Switch Online subscription to use it), you can peruse a big map of Hyrule and tap on individual areas for a closer look. The app offers just enough direction to nudge me toward an elusive pickup; when zoomed in, the app drops icons on the map showing you the locations of different collectibles, and if you tap on an icon, the app will show a popup noting any items or special requirements you need to fulfill to be able to obtain it. There are also a bunch of filters you can apply, like swapping between the maps for child and adult Link and toggling categories of icons on and off.

For Skulltula hunting, the map has already saved my sanity many times. While exploring, I’ll often come across the telltale Golden Skulltula sound but have no idea how to get to the tricky spider, even with the help of additional chimes from the new Echo Gem Skulltula tracking item. But with a peek at the Zelda Notes app, I can pinpoint the exact location of where the Skulltula is supposed to be and if I need to use a certain item or change the time of day to be able to get to it.

I recently delved into the Shadow Temple, for example, and I couldn’t figure out how to get up to a Skulltula that I saw while coming down an elevator. But when I checked the icon on the Zelda Notes map, it said that I needed the Scarecrow Song to create a hookshot point, which I hadn’t gotten yet. As soon as I finished the dungeon, getting the Scarecrow Song was my first priority.

Zelda Notes has been useful for tracking down Pieces of Heart, too, which give Link more health once you collect enough. While running up and down Zora’s River, I kept seeing two Pieces of Heart on faraway ledges that I couldn’t figure out how to get to. (My memories of my extensive time with Ocarina of Time as a kid also failed me.) But tapping one of the icons on the Zelda Notes map revealed that I needed a Cucco to get to it — and suddenly, I understood why there was one of the chirping white birds near the entrance of the zone.

Yes, it can be annoying to have to open my phone and dig through an app to track down all of the Skulltulas and other objects of curiosity scattered around Hyrule. I play video games to try and get away from my phone, not look at it more. And sure, I could just look up an online guide, but that often means wading through walkthroughs of varying quality, trusting an AI summary, or scrubbing through a YouTube video. But with Zelda Notes, I’m usually in and out relatively quickly, putting down my phone after seeing a clue or an icon that pushes me in the direction of the next golden spider. I’ve already found more than 70 Golden Skulltulas, and to my surprise, I now want to hunt for the full 100.

The appointment follows the launch of Phase’s Layer 1 blockchain mainnet and AI agent services, backed by $45 million in founder funding.

SAN FRANCISCO, November 11, 2026 — Phase, a technology company building the operating layer for the AI agent economy, today announced that Yana Prikhodchenko, former CEO of Cointelegraph, has joined the company as co-founder and CEO. She will lead strategy, global expansion and commercial development, working alongside founder Kirill Eves, as Phase scales its decentralized infrastructure for autonomous AI agents.

Prikhodchenko joins Phase after nearly five years at Cointelegraph, where she served as CEO, leading a global team of more than 200 people and transforming the company from a crypto news platform into a diversified group spanning media, marketing, research, acceleration and industry events. Before joining Cointelegraph, she spent more than a decade in senior leadership roles at major banking and insurance companies, including Allianz.

Phase is building the operating layer for the AI agent economy, giving AI agents everything they need to operate autonomously, from accessing the open web and orchestrating AI models to performing computing tasks and making payments. These services run on Phase’s Layer 1 blockchain, powered by a decentralized network of everyday devices whose owners earn rewards for validating transactions and contributing connectivity and computing power.

“AI agents are moving from answering questions to performing real tasks on our behalf, but the infrastructure they depend on was not built for autonomous systems. Phase is building an open, decentralized network that anyone can participate in. My focus is to bring this technology to developers and businesses worldwide and build the ecosystem needed to support the next generation of AI agents,” said Yana Prikhodchenko, co-founder and CEO of Phase.

“Yana brings extensive leadership experience across media, technology and financial services, along with the operational expertise and industry relationships needed to take Phase into its next stage of growth. As we bring our technology to market, she will lead the company’s commercial development and global expansion,” said Kirill Eves, founder of Phase.

Phase is a technology company building the operating layer for the AI agent economy, with four integrated services that give AI agents everything they need to operate autonomously, from accessing the open web and orchestrating AI models to performing computing tasks and making payments. The platform runs on its own Layer 1 blockchain, powered by a decentralized network of everyday devices. Phase has received $45 million in funding from its founder, Kirill Eves.

Phase Proxy enables AI agents acting on behalf of users to access websites that restrict automated traffic through mobile and residential internet connections. Phase Router intelligently directs requests across leading AI models to optimize quality, performance and cost. Phase Compute harnesses the processing power of devices across the network to run open-weight AI models, while on-chain settlement enables autonomous payments using stablecoins and PHASE, the network’s native token. Phase is building its own AI model to serve as the platform’s central intelligence, capable of advanced reasoning, solving complex tasks independently and orchestrating other AI models.

Unlike most blockchain networks that require dedicated servers or token staking, Phase allows anyone to become a validator simply by downloading the app, without specialized hardware or upfront investment. Its reputation-based consensus and randomized validator selection secure the network and prevent control from concentrating in the hands of a few powerful operators. Phase rewards device owners with PHASE tokens for validating blocks and contributing connectivity and computing power, enabling millions of people to help build the AI economy and share in the value it creates.

Google Fi now switches between the best mobile networks automatically – how it works

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Networking > Mobile Carriers

Pixel users also get a bonus Wi-Fi upgrade. Here's what to know.

From left to right: Samsung Galaxy Z Flip 8, Google Pixel 11, Google Pixel 11 Pro XL.

Google Fi wireless customers with Pixel phones just got a serious upgrade to their mobile and Wi-Fi connections for free.

Google has announced two new “super network” connection features for Fi Wireless: one that automatically switches between two of the largest 5G cellular networks, and another that automatically connects to premium Wi-Fi in millions of locations without you needing to log in.

The features are rolling out today for Pixel 9 phones and newer models on every Google Fi plan. If you’re not a current Fi customer, you can sign up for a free seven-day trial to test it alongside your current carrier.

Here’s how the new mobile connection feature works: if your main connection slows or becomes unstable, Fi will automatically move to a secondary connection. This not only means you’ll have the fastest option when your connection is good, but you’ll have fewer dead zones when service is limited.

Also: I compared Verizon, T-Mobile, and AT&T 5G coverage on a road trip – and the winner surprised me

The feature will run on MEP (Multiple Enabled Profiles) technology, which allows a single eSIM chip to host and run multiple SIM profiles simultaneously. Footnotes in Google’s release note that you’ll need to have “any non-Fi SIMs disabled.”

Google Fi originally offered automatic switching between two networks when the service debuted a decade ago, but it was between Sprint and T-Mobile, which merged in 2020. Google didn’t reveal what the “two of America’s best 5G cellular networks” were, but ZDNET has reached out to ask. The service usually runs on T-Mobile. Along with T-Mobile, the three biggest carriers are AT&T and Verizon, so it’s likely the second carrier is one of those.

In addition to a mobile connection upgrade, users are getting better Wi-Fi, too. Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ will automatically connect you to faster Wi-Fi in millions of locations like airports and malls (Google defines “faster” as two times better reliability and 30% improved latency compared to normal Google Fi cellular networks) without you having to log in or head to your settings. These connections will be automatically encrypted, Google said, keeping your online activity safe.

Also: 6 default Pixel 11 settings I changed that made a big difference – and why

This feature is also coming to Samsung phones running One UI 9 or higher and select modern Motorola phones.

Both of these features, Google explained, are available internationally and are included on Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans at no extra cost.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/carelon-agrees-pay-10m-settle-ghost-network-coverage-denial-allegations-new-york" hreflang="en">Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York</a>

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →
Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Carelon will pay more than $10 million across two settlements in New York over mental health networks and coverage denials.

The company, a division of Elevance Health, will pay $2.35 million in penalties, fees and costs as well as up to $8 million in restitution for members who paid out-of-pocket for out-of-network mental healthcare, to reimburse those whose claims were improperly denied or to overhaul its networks.

New York Attorney General Letitia James said in an announcement that the settlements resolve two investigations from her office that determined the company misled members by directing them to seek care from providers that were out-of-network—through so-called "ghost networks"—and improperly denied coverage for treatment for substance abuse disorder.

James noted that Carelon administers behavioral health benefits for multiple plans, including Anthem Blue Cross, EmblemHealth, Independent Health and the Empire Plan, which covers state and local government workers.

Across these insurers, Carelon manages behavioral health benefits for more than 4 million people in the state, James said.

The alleged behavior also goes against a 2015 agreement with the AG's office, where the company agreed that it would maintain accurate provider networks and "use approved criteria" when denying substance abuse treatments.

"When someone is ready to seek help for a mental health condition or addiction, every day matters," said James. “Carelon left New Yorkers calling provider after provider who could not see them and made it harder for people to get the treatment to which they were entitled."

"These settlements will help people get the care they need and put money back in New Yorkers’ pockets," she said.

Elevance Health said in a statement to Fierce Healthcare that it is committed to ensuring patients have access to the most up-to-date provider information.

"Carelon Behavioral Health is committed to helping people find and access high-quality mental health and substance use disorder care. Accurate, up-to-date provider information is an important part of that effort," the company said. "We continue to invest in tools and processes that support accurate provider information and work closely with providers and other partners to help connect consumers with the care they need."

Carelon was hit with a lawsuit in April over the alleged ghost networks and their impact on the Empire Plan in particular. In the case, plaintiff's attorneys conducted secret shopper calls to analyze Carelon's network, calling 300 providers. Just 17% of those contacted took insurance and were open to new patients.

These "ghost networks" have also drawn concern in other markets, such as for maternal care services in Medicaid and in Medicare Advantage. A study released last month found that commercial plans also routinely have inaccurate provider directories.

Want To Befriend a Crow? Here's How To Attract These Clever Birds To Your Yard and Earn Their Trust

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

5 Easy Fall Wins to Help Your Backyard Birds Survive Winter – How to Beat the Big Chill and Ensure More Visits

8 Things I Learned About Gardening From an Amish Farmer – and Why They're Worth Trying in Your Own Yard

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

If you're keen to make nice with the Einstein of the bird world, here's how to go about it...

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Is there anything more whimsical, more magical than the idea of being able to befriend a crow?

With the ability to recognize individual people, remember faces and learn from experience, these glossy-feathered birds are among the cleverest birds we're likely to encounter in our gardens. They're also steeped in symbolism, appearing in various traditions as spiritual messengers or guides associated with wisdom, transformation and the mysteries of life.

For those who enjoy looking for a little meaning in the natural world, then, spotting one could feel like more than just another garden bird sighting. Especially if you're the sort of person who obsesses over your birth month bird, or spends ages wondering what the first bird you see each morning says about your day ahead.

Look, we all love finding little stories and connections in nature. Indeed, some of us even believe that the bird we see most in our garden says everything about our gardening style – and a regular crow visitor might suggest there's something rather welcoming about your outdoor space. So, yes, it makes sense many of us are secretly wondering how to befriend a crow.

If you've been on social media lately, you may have stumbled across the (admittedly niche) trend of CrowTok, which sees people share videos of their feathered visitors, the food they leave out and, occasionally, the curious little objects their avian friends appear to bring in return. And while we might like to imagine these offerings are thank-you gifts, there's some fascinating science behind the behaviour.

Yes, the National Audubon Society has explored how crows sometimes leave objects for people who feed them, with corvid biologist John Marzluff explaining that a clever bird could learn that leaving an object results in a tasty reward.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

It's a lovely reminder that our relationship with nature can be full of surprises. Crows are social and remarkably observant birds, and while they're wild animals rather than pets, they can learn to recognise familiar faces and become more comfortable around people who treat them consistently and respectfully.

So, if you'd love to befriend a crow and see a few more of these clever characters dropping by your backyard, there are some simple ways to make your outdoor space more appealing. From offering their favorite snacks to creating a safe spot where they can stop for a drink, here's how to attract crows to your yard – and perhaps even earn their trust in the process.

If there's one way to catch a crow's attention, it's with a tasty snack. Peanuts are a popular choice, and unsalted peanuts are a good option to offer. You could also try a little variety with other suitable foods, such as cooked eggs (plain, obviously, so hold the seasoning!) or mealworms.

It's not just what you offer that matters, however; it's how regularly you do it. Environment for the Americas recommends putting food and water out at the same time and in the same place each day, as crows are intelligent enough to recognise routines, and may soon learn when to expect a visit.

Start by putting out a small amount, then, and clear away anything uneaten to avoid attracting unwanted visitors. With time, your regular visitors may begin to recognize you as the person responsible for their daily delivery.

Crows are naturally cautious birds, so where you put their food can make a big difference if you're trying to make friends with one. Rather than hiding snacks away in a traditional hanging bird feeder, then, try placing a small amount on a low feeding platform or another suitable open surface (like this nifty Deck Bird Feeder from Amazon).

A spot near a fence, shed roof or sturdy tree branch can give them somewhere to perch while they tuck in. And, ideally, you should choose somewhere with clear sightlines, as this allows crows to keep an eye on their surroundings and spot potential danger.

Try to give them plenty of space, too. If you're watching from indoors, they'll have the opportunity to get comfortable with your presence without feeling as though they're being observed too closely.

Food might get their attention, but a reliable source of fresh water can give crows another reason to visit. Try placing a shallow bird bath or sturdy water bowl somewhere accessible, with a gently sloping edge that makes it easy for birds to drink and bathe.

Keep the water fresh, changing it regularly and cleaning the dish to prevent a build-up of dirt and bacteria. Position it somewhere relatively open so visiting crows can watch for potential predators while they stop for a drink... and then get ready for the most tedious part of our How To Befriend A Crow 101 Guide: be patient.

Oh yes, rather than rushing outside to get closer, try sitting quietly at a distance while the birds eat and drink. Some crow enthusiasts on Reddit also suggest making a gentle whistling sound as you put food out, as this helps the birds associate that sound with a reliable food source, but that one is well and truly up to you!

It takes time to befriend a crow. Give it a little while, though, and you may find that your visitors become less wary when you're around. Just let them set the pace – earning the trust of a wild crow is a relationship worth taking slowly.

Sure, you might not end up with a crow delivering shiny little presents to your doorstep, but creating a garden where these intelligent birds feel safe enough to visit? Well, that's a rather lovely reward in itself, I say!

Kayleigh is an enthusiastic (sometimes too enthusiastic!) gardener and has worked in media for over a decade. She previously served as digital editor at Stylist magazine, and has written extensively for Ideal Home, Woman & Home, Homes & Gardens, and a handful of other titles. Kayleigh is passionate about wildlife-friendly gardening, and recently cancelled her weekend plans to build a mini pond when her toddler found a frog living in their water barrel. As such, her garden – designed around the stunning magnolia tree at its centre – is filled to the brim with pollinator-friendly blooms, homemade bird feeders, and old logs for insects to nest in.

Eleven new relists in search of four votes

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Relist Watch column examines cert petitions that the Supreme Court has “relisted” for its upcoming conference. A short explanation of relists is available here.

First, some old business. The justices disposed of two repeat visitors last week, producing one winner and one loser. After three relists, Goldman Sachs Bank USA v. Brown finally got the nod. The court will decide when bankruptcy courts can refuse to enforce arbitration agreements in suits alleging violations of the automatic stay. But two-time relist Clemente Properties, Inc. v. Pierluisi-Urrutia struck out: The court denied without comment the petition brought by Roberto Clemente’s family over Puerto Rico’s use of the baseball legend’s image, which raised questions about takings of intangible property and Puerto Rico’s sovereign immunity.

After a quiet week, the relist pipeline is suddenly gushing: The justices have served up 11 new relists for Friday’s conference, up from four last week. Several come in pairs or trios, and the federal government – the court’s most successful repeat litigant – advocates review in three of them.

The government seeks asylum – from the D.C. Circuit

The most consequential new relist this week may be Mullin v. Refugee and Immigrant Center for Education and Legal Services. On his first day back in office, President Donald Trump issued Proclamation 10888, which suspended the entry of aliens “engaged in the invasion across the southern border” and barred those who violate the suspension from invoking provisions of the immigration laws that would let them stay, including asylum.

Three nonprofits and 13 individuals sued. The district court certified a class of people subject to the proclamation who are or will be present in the United States. It held unlawful the proclamation’s restrictions on asylum and other statutory protections, as well as its authorization of removal procedures outside those established by Congress, and issued a classwide injunction. A divided panel of the U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit narrowed the class definition, but affirmed. It reasoned that although asylum is discretionary, the executive cannot categorically deny covered migrants access to asylum by proclamation and informal guidance rather than through the procedures Congress prescribed. Judge Justin Walker dissented in relevant part.

The solicitor general’s petition argues that asylum is discretionary and that the president may categorically deny it to those covered by the proclamation. Requiring the government to accept applications it has already decided to reject, the government contends, serves no purpose. It also challenges the classwide injunction and the breadth of the certified class. The government argues that the ruling threatens to revive chaos at the border. Iowa and 21 other states support the petition, arguing that the decision undermines the federal duty to protect the states against invasion.

The respondents’ brief in opposition argues that Congress expressly gave noncitizens physically present in the United States the right to apply for asylum, regardless of how they entered, and that the president cannot eliminate that right by proclamation. They emphasize the absence of a circuit split and dispute the government’s claims that the policy was responsible for reducing border crossings. They call the remedial issues fact-bound and say the government overstates the border crisis, noting that apprehensions had already fallen more than 90% from their peak before the proclamation.

The D.C. Circuit’s mandate is stayed, leaving the asylum restrictions in place for now. The absence of a split weighs against review, but the invalidation of a major administration policy could well persuade the justices to take another trip to the border. Will the court resolve this case quickly or let it sit for a while as it decides what to do, as it has in other high-profile cases?

Our first group of cases asks whether Washington may regulate private companies that run federal immigration detention centers. In GEO Group, Inc. v. Nwauzor, a class of detainees and the state sued GEO, which operates the Northwest ICE Processing Center in Tacoma under a federal contract, for not paying Washington’s minimum wage to detainees in a voluntary work program for which ICE reimburses $1 a day. GEO argues it’s not a conventional labor relationship, but just an effort to prevent the detainees from becoming restless from idleness. The U.S. Court of Appeals for the 9th Circuit certified the question to the Washington Supreme Court, which held that the state minimum-wage law applied to the detained workers. A divided panel of the 9th Circuit then rejected GEO’s supremacy clause defenses, leaving GEO with a judgment it puts at about $37 million. Seven judges voted to rehear the case en banc, and six joined dissents, arguing that (among other things) the state law “interferes with federal immigration-detention policy” and thus directly regulates the federal government.

GEO argues that Washington cannot regulate a federal detention program through a contractor while exempting its own detainees from the wage law. Washington and the detainees respond that the law applies equally to every private detention contractor, whether it works for a city, the state or the federal government, and that the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 3rd Circuit applies the same test as the 9th. The detainees also note that ICE has since revised its detention standards, which they say limits the decision’s future impact.

In a court-invited amicus brief, the U.S. solicitor general recommends granting review. The government argues that the law discriminates against the federal government, because Washington exempts its own facilities while barring itself from contracting with private ones, and that federal statutes leave detainee pay to the Secretary of Homeland Security. It also says the decision conflicts with a decision of the 3rd Circuit ruling on a New Jersey law targeting private detention contractors.

GEO Group, Inc. v. Ferguson challenges a 2023 Washington statute regulating private civil detention facilities, which GEO says reaches its Tacoma facility alone. The district court preliminarily enjoined it, but the 9th Circuit vacated that ruling, rejecting GEO’s direct regulation and preemption claims and remanding on its discrimination claim against private contractors. GEO says Washington is micromanaging federal detention by regulating a contractor; the state says its requirements resemble those governing other civil facilities. It also points to further proceedings on remand as a reason not to intervene yet.

A grant in Nwauzor seems likely; Ferguson presents a closer question, but at minimum it seems likely to be held if Nwauzor is granted.

Two companies that went public while business was booming present a question of required disclosures in federal securities filings: How much bad news must a company reveal? Two related relists ask the justices to clarify when withholding information makes an IPO registration statement misleading under Section 11 of the Securities Act of 1933.

In Robinhood Markets, Inc. v. Sodha, the online brokerage rode the GameStop meme-stock frenzy and Dogecoin boom into its July 2021 IPO. Robinhood warned that trading might decline and provided an estimate of second-quarter revenue, but investors say it withheld information showing that trading and other business metrics were already declining. A district court dismissed their claims, but a divided panel of the 9th Circuit revived them. Robinhood’s petition argues that merely omitting material information cannot create liability unless the omission makes an affirmative statement misleading or violates a specific disclosure requirement. The investors respond that the company’s disclosures presented a misleading picture of its performance. The U.S. solicitor general, invited to weigh in, recommends granting review on the Section 11 misleading-omissions question, but not on a separate question concerning disclosures of known trends under Item 303 of Regulation S-K (which the agency is reviewing).

In ON24, Inc. v. Leadersel Innotech ESG, a webinar-platform provider prospered as businesses moved online during the pandemic. Investors allege that the company knew before the IPO that many pandemic-era customers did not intend to renew. An unpublished 9th Circuit memorandum revived the suit. ON24 asks whether describing an unmaterialized risk as hypothetical is misleading, alleging a split with the U.S. Courts of Appeals for the 1st, 3rd and 10th Circuits, and raises two questions that overlap with Robinhood’s. ON24 argues that the court improperly required it to predict future customer cancellations; the investors respond that “the warned-of risk was already materializing.” ON24 also asks the justices either to grant its petition or hold it for Robinhood. Leadersel Innotech answers that the 9th Circuit decision is non-precedential and that the panel found the risk had already begun to materialize.

With the solicitor general supporting review in Robinhood, a grant there looks plausible. Whether ON24 gets invited along is less certain. It seems a likelier hold.

It’s not every week that the Taliban is a respondent in the Supreme Court. That said, it has not responded, leaving an Afghan diplomat filing an amicus brief to oppose review – not on the Taliban’s behalf, but on behalf of the Afghan people.

When the Taliban retook Kabul in August 2021, the Treasury Department blocked billions of dollars that Da Afghanistan Bank held at the Federal Reserve Bank of New York. Victims holding judgments against the Taliban sought to execute on the funds under Section 201(a) of the Terrorism Risk Insurance Act, which makes the blocked assets of a terrorist party and its “agency or instrumentality” subject to execution.

A divided panel of the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 2nd Circuit affirmed the denial of turnover. It held that an entity’s status as a terrorist organization’s instrumentality must be determined when its assets were blocked, before the Taliban installed its officials at the bank. Separately, the panel rejected other plaintiffs’ attempt at prejudgment attachment, holding that Da Afghanistan Bank enjoys sovereign immunity as the central bank of Afghanistan, which the executive continues to recognize as a foreign state. Judge Richard Sullivan dissented.

In Havlish v. The Taliban, judgment holders challenge the blocking-date rule, alleging a conflict between the 2nd Circuit and the U.S. Courts of Appeals for the 9th and 11th Circuits. They are backed by Iowa and 30 other states.

In Owens v. The Taliban, plaintiffs who lacked judgments when the district court ruled say the 2nd Circuit is the first court of appeals to require courts to defer to the executive on “foreign state” status, and thus “breaks with th[e] consensus” positions of the U.S. Courts of Appeals for the 1st, 3rd, and 6th Circuits. Ashton v. The Taliban joins Owens in arguing that courts should not defer to the executive branch in determining whether an entity is a “foreign state” entitled to sovereign immunity, and seeks a GVR in light of this year’s decision in Exxon Mobil Corp. v. Corporación CIMEX, S.A.

As noted, no respondent has appeared. The only opposition is an amicus brief jointly filed in the three cases by Naseer Faiq, chargé d’affaires of Afghanistan’s mission to the United Nations, who says no one is currently authorized to appear for Afghanistan in U.S. courts. He argues that the facts are unlikely to recur, that there is no circuit split, and that the immunity issue affects only the petitioners’ priority among creditors. The Havlish petitioners have sought leave to respond, arguing that the legal question is likely to recur in terrorism litigation and does indeed implicate a circuit split.

At the very least, the court will need more time to figure out what to do when the respondent seems unwilling to appear to defend the cases.

How many places can a state declare off-limits to firearms before the Second Amendment right to carry in public becomes largely theoretical? That’s the question in Novotny v. Moore, a challenge to Maryland’s extensive restrictions on carrying firearms in “sensitive places.”

Maryland prohibits licensed gun owners from carrying firearms in numerous locations, including public transportation, parks and forests, healthcare facilities, museums, stadiums, casinos, public demonstrations, and establishments serving alcohol. I thought this was America! A divided panel of the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 4th Circuit upheld those restrictions, finding historical support for them under the Supreme Court’s decision in New York State Rifle & Pistol Association v. Bruen. Judge G. Steven Agee dissented as to many of the restrictions, warning that the majority had stretched the sensitive-places exception too far. The panel separately struck down Maryland’s presumptive ban on carrying firearms on private property open to the public.(After this year’s decision in Wolford v. Lopez, which struck down Hawaii’s similar private-property rule, Maryland dropped its own cert petition seeking review of that ruling.)

The gun owners’ petition argues that historical restrictions on carrying firearms in sensitive places were generally limited to locations where the government provides security, not ordinary places where people gather. Maryland responds that historical regulations support restrictions at crowded places and locations serving vulnerable populations, and contends that review is unnecessary because the lower courts largely agree on the governing principles.

Invoking Wolford, the petitioners ask the justices to grant review or at least send the case back for reconsideration. Whether the court is ready to take on the sensitive-places doctrine so soon after Wolford remains to be seen. But reconsideration under Wolford seems like a real possibility.

In Sprint Corp. v. Federal Communications Commission, the Commission fined Sprint $12.2 million and T-Mobile $80.1 million for failing to adequately safeguard customers’ location information shared with third-party aggregators, including by continuing to disclose the information after learning of significant security vulnerabilities. The U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit upheld the penalties, concluding that information about a cellphone’s location – even when no call is underway – qualifies as confidential “customer proprietary network information” under the Communications Act.

The carriers’ petition argues that the statute protects only call-location information, not all device-location data. They also contend that the FCC circumvented a statutory penalty cap by treating separate relationships with third-party companies as multiple “continuing violations,” and that the D.C. Circuit improperly deferred to the agency’s interpretation despite Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo. But the carriers’ principal request is narrower: They ask the justices to send the case back in light of this year’s decision in FCC v. AT&T, which held that an FCC forfeiture order does not itself compel payment. That opinion, the carriers say, rested on a key government concession: A company can refuse to pay and contest the penalty in a subsequent enforcement proceeding with a jury. Sprint and T-Mobile say they paid under protest because the FCC’s orders demanded payment and existing precedent left them little choice. The government responds that the carriers always had the option not to pay, that they failed to preserve their claim of being misled, and that AT&T provides no reason for reconsideration. The carriers disagree.

A remand remains possible, although the government notes that the court rejected a comparable request from Verizon. For now, Sprint and T-Mobile are hoping the justices will take their call.

How long can a “hot pursuit” stay hot after police lose sight of the suspect? That’s the question in City of North Las Vegas v. Jones, a case arising from a search for a suspected domestic abuser that ended with police entering a stranger’s backyard and shooting two dogs. The suspect was never found.

After officers saw a man flee over a backyard wall, the officers stated that they established a neighborhood perimeter rather than pursuing him on foot. A K-9 officer arrived about 18 minutes later, and during the ensuing search, his police dog alerted them toward a walled backyard. An officer climbed in without a warrant. Three pit bulls emerged, two of which attacked the police dog and were shot. The dogs’ owners, Genoa Jones and Cornell Tinsley, sued. The district court granted the officers qualified immunity, but the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 9th Circuit reversed on the warrantless-entry claim, holding that the officers had not been in immediate and continuous pursuit and that circuit precedent clearly established the search’s illegality. It left intact qualified immunity for shooting the dogs. Eight judges dissented from the denial of rehearing en banc.

The city’s petition argues that the officers never abandoned the pursuit: They say they established a perimeter and used a police dog to locate a potentially dangerous suspect. Even if the search was unlawful, the city argues, no sufficiently specific precedent clearly established that fact. It also asks the justices to hold that only Supreme Court decisions – not lower-court precedent – can clearly establish law for qualified-immunity purposes. The homeowners respond that the officers never actually established a perimeter or chased the suspect and instead just conducted a neighborhood-wide search without knowing his whereabouts.

The eight dissenting judges may help keep the petition warm. But the court closely reviews many petitions in cases where police are denied qualified immunity, and unless the error was clear enough to warrant summary reversal, the justices may let this pursuit go cold – perhaps with a dissent from denial of cert.

The court has enough to chew on that it seems likely to take a while to finish processing this week’s new offerings. As for me, I’ll be watching Monday’s orders to find out how much of this I get to reuse.

Issue: Whether the supremacy clause allows a state to reclassify federal immigration detainees participating in a federal work program as employees and thereby impose its state minimum-wage law just because a private contractor provides detention services at the federal facility where the detainees are housed.

Issues: (1) Whether an issuer violates Section 11(a) of the Securities Act of 1933’s misleading-omissions prong by failing to disclose interim financial data that investors may consider material, without an inquiry into whether the omission rendered any affirmative statement misleading; and (2) whether an issuer violates Item 303 – and thus Section 11(a)’s required-statements prong – by failing to disclose and quantify interim financial data before quarterly reporting deadlines.

Issue: Whether Maryland’s numerous prohibitions on carrying firearms in many places frequented by the general public violate the Second and 14th Amendments.

City of North Las Vegas, Nevada v. Jones, 25-1355

Issues: (1) Whether officers’ warrantless search of a backyard in close proximity to the domestic violence victim’s residence was a lawful “hot pursuit” when the suspect’s trail was temporarily lost within the perimeter for approximately 18 minutes but recovered through immediate and continuous police efforts; (2) whether, even if the search did not qualify as a “hot pursuit,” the officers were entitled to qualified immunity because no “clearly established law” was violated; and (3) whether, in the interest of avoiding the perpetual flood of federal court qualified immunity litigation, the court should decide that clearly established law means only the decisions of the Supreme Court, and not lower court decisions, which fail to provide police departments with meaningful guidance because they are highly fact-determinative and subject to numerous differing and often contradictory judicial interpretations.

ON24, Inc. v. Leadersel Innotech ESG, 25-1376

Issues: (1) Whether an issuer violates Section 11(a) of the Securities Act of 1933’s misleading-omissions prong by describing unmaterialized risks as hypothetical; (2) whether an issuer violates Section 11(a)’s misleading-omissions prong whenever it omits information related to a disclosed fact regardless of whether the omission renders an affirmative statement misleading; and (3) whether an issuer violates Item 303 of Regulation S-K – and thus the prong of Section 11(a) prohibiting the omission of required statements – by failing to disclose immaterial facts as “known trends or uncertainties” that are reasonably likely to have a material impact on financial results.

Sprint Corp. v. Federal Communications Commission, 25-1422

Issues: (1) Whether this court should grant, vacate, and remand in light of FCC v. AT&T, Inc. and the government’s concessions in that case; (2) whether “location” customer proprietary network information under 47 U.S.C. § 222 refers only to call-location information, not other formation about the location of a mobile device; and (3) whether the number of “continuing violations” arising from a single failure to act under 47 U.S.C. § 503(b) is a legal question for courts to decide, without deferring to the Federal Communications Commission’s conclusion.

Issue: Whether the supremacy clause permits a state to enact a law micromanaging the details of how a federal contractor must provide services at a federal detention facility, while exempting the state’s own detention facilities from all those requirements.

Mullin v. Refugee and Immigrant Center for Education and Legal Services, 26-239

Issues: (1) Whether Presidential Proclamation No. 10888 and its implementing guidance – which suspended the entry of certain aliens and precluded their access to statutory provisions that would enable their continued presence in the country, including asylum – are lawful; (2) whether 8 U.S.C. § 1252(f)(1) bars the classwide relief entered by the district court; and (3) whether the class definition comports with Article III and Rule 23 of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

Issue: Whether, under the Terrorism Risk Insurance Act, a plaintiff’s right to execute on blocked assets depends on whether the assets’ owner was a terrorist party or its agency or instrumentality at the time the assets were blocked.

Issue: Whether, in light of the court’s intervening decision in Exxon Mobil Corp. v. Corporación CIMEX, S.A., which confirms that a court – not the Executive Branch – must resolve through ordinary statutory construction whether an entity is a “foreign state” entitled to sovereign immunity, the judgment below should be vacated and remanded so that the Second Circuit must reconsider, under the correct framework, whether the Taliban and its instrumentalities are entitled to immunity under the Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act.

Issue: Whether the Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act requires courts to independently interpret its provisions, or whether a court must defer to State Department websites and Department of Justice litigation filings in determining whether an entity is entitled to sovereign immunity under the FSIA.

Issue: Whether private instruction in courses required for state licensure constitutes a government speech.

(Relisted after the Sept. 28, Oct. 9, Oct. 16, and Nov. 6 conferences.)

Issues: (1) Whether, in a use-of-force case which involves multiple instances of force, Barnes v. Felix requires that each use-of-force be analyzed progressively from before the first instance of force used through the last instance of force used, considering the information known to the officer, the suspect’s prior actions (including any compliance, non-compliance, resistance, and reaching or grabbing for a weapon), and the suspect’s reactions to those prior use(s)-of-force; and (2) whether, when analyzing a defendant law enforcement officer’s motion brought under Rule 12(b)(6) or 12(c), in a use-of-force case where body camera video clearly depicts the entire lead-up to and application of force by a law enforcement officer, whether the lower courts may analyze the clearly established prong of qualified immunity at a more general, plaintiff-friendly, level than at the motion for summary judgment stage of litigation.

(Relisted after the Sept. 28, Oct. 9, Oct. 16, and Nov. 6 conferences.)

Issues: (1) Whether a plaintiff can plead single-incident municipal liability without alleging a violation of a clearly established constitutional right; and (2) whether a plaintiff can plead single-incident municipal liability without alleging a complete lack of training on the constitutional right in question.

(Relisted after the Sept. 28, Oct. 9, Oct. 16, and Nov. 6 conferences.)

Issue: Whether the principles of Hope v. Pelzer are limited to Eighth Amendment claims or extend to free speech and free exercise clause claims such that petitioner’s constitutional claims should not be barred by qualified immunity.

(Relisted after the Sept. 28, Oct. 9, Oct. 16, and Nov. 6 conferences.)

StandWithUs Center for Legal Justice v. Massachusetts Institute of Technology, 25-1404

Issues: (1) Whether the harassers’ subjective discriminatory intent is an element of a hostile environment claim under Title VI; and (2) whether the First Amendment bars a plaintiff from pleading a Title VI hostile environment claim based on “protected speech.”

(Relisted after the Sept. 28, Oct. 9, Oct. 16, and Nov. 6 conferences.)

Issue: Whether the law clearly established that the officer’s use of a less-lethal projectile, under the totality of the circumstances, constituted an unreasonable use of force in violation of the Fourth Amendment.

(Relisted after the Oct. 9, Oct. 16, and Nov. 6 conferences.)

Issue: Whether an automobile is per se an instrumentality of interstate commerce, which would make a federal offense of every kidnapping involving an automobile under 18 U.S.C. § 1201(a)(1).

(Relisted after the Oct. 16 and Nov. 6 conferences.)

Issues:(1) Whether the district court erred by not requiring plaintiffs to offer an alternative map that performed just as well as the state’s map with respect to the state’s districting criteria; (2) whether the district court erred by finding racial-bloc voting without accounting for politics; (3) whether the district court erred by finding that the totality of circumstances suggests an objective likelihood of discrimination; and (4) whether the state violated the 14th Amendment by declining to draw a race-based plan.

(Relisted after the Oct. 16 and Nov. 6 conferences.)

Aeromexico Adds New U.S. Routes, Flights

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Aeromexico is again expanding its footprint in the U.S. with assistance from longtime partner Delta.

The carrier announced Monday that it will connect Mexico City with Nashville, Tennessee, New Orleans, and San Juan, Puerto Rico. None of the three cities currently has nonstop service to the Mexican capital.

All three connections will operate four times weekly. Service to Nashville will launch first, on June 13, 2027, followed by New Orleans on June 17 and San Juan on June 24.

Flights to Nashville and New Orleans will use Embraer E190 aircraft with 99 seats. For the route to San Juan, the airline will operate Boeing 737 MAX 8s with 166 seats.

Aeromexico will also launch a second daily service between Mexico City and Austin, Texas, on June 3.

“The addition of Nashville, New Orleans, and San Juan to our network will allow us to provide more connectivity options, bringing our customers closer to destinations known for their unique identity and remarkable attractions,” Giancarlo Mulinelli, senior vice president of global sales at Aeromexico, said in a news release. “Together with Delta, we continue to open markets and create more opportunities that strengthen tourism and ties between Mexico and the United States.”

Last month, the partners announced new routes from Mexico City to Orange County, California, Fort Lauderdale, Florida, and Portland, Oregon.

Napoli su Varela, ma il Monza lo valuta 40 milioni: ci sono anche altre squadre

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

William Scuotto per IlNapolista ha raccolto delle informazioni sulla trattativa tra il Napoli e Varela:

“Gustavo Varela al Napoli al momento è poco più di una speranza. Si è aperta un’asta attorno al centravanti che il Monza ha portato in Brianza per soli 2,5 milioni. Adesso il giovane portoghese (ha 21 anni) ne vale più di 40. È la cifra raccolta dal Napolista che ha parlato con fonti accreditate. Il lusitano è stato un ottimo investimento da parte della società lombarda. Varela ha segnato 9 gol in 9 partite ed è in testa alla classifica marcatori. Da solo.

È fisiologico che il Monza voglia monetizzare. Ci sono tanti club interessati al calciatore. C’è anche il Napoli ma la cifra è quella. E al momento non è ancora chiaro se il Monza voglia la somma cash o se sia disposto ad altre modalità di pagamento.

Chiaramente Su Varela ci sono anche club stranieri, c’è anche il Benfica ma la somma è alta. In un calcio povero di centravanti che segnano, averne uno è un tesori. Sia per la squadra sia per il mercato.

Il Monza deciderà i tempi e le modalità. Fino a gennaio ci sono ancora altre partite: se Varela dovesse proseguire a questo ritmo, la quotazione potrebbe persino salire.”.

Top 10 Resorts in the East for Terrain Variety (2027)

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Terrain variety may not be the first thing you think of when planning a ski trip, but it matters, especially when your group includes different ability levels. A resort’s range of runs can make or break a group ski day. It can be the difference between beginners and intermediates riding lifts together or only meeting up at lunch.

Below are SKI readers’ 10 top-ranked resorts for terrain variety. Each has something for everyone, from hard-charging experts to those who still need help buckling their boots.

Score: 8.45 Other Strengths: Lodging, Nightlife Weaknesses: Parking, Value

“Great terrain, topnotch grooming, good services overall.”

“Families will find plenty to like if they have the budget for it, and adventure seekers will never run out of terrain to explore if they know where to look.”

A White House ballroom petition and border wall backlash

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court Building is closed today for Veterans Day. In recognition of that, this is an abridged edition of SCOTUStoday.

The Trump administration has brought the battle over construction of a new White House ballroom back to the Supreme Court. In a petition for review filed last week but made public on Monday, U.S. Solicitor General D. John Sauer told the justices that the project “should be permitted to move forward outside the shadow of judicial superintendence.” For more on the petition, see the On Site section below.

On Tuesday, the justices heard argument in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards, LLC, on whether the Constitution allows the executive branch – as opposed to the courts – to adjudicate an employer’s obligation to comply with employment agreements concerning temporary immigrant workers.

On Friday, the justices will meet in a private conference to discuss cases and vote on petitions for review. Orders from Friday’s conference are expected on Monday at 9:30 a.m. EST.

Stone Age children left fingerprints on ancient art projects

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

Clay figurines hint at hands-on learning in the Levant

Stone Age hunter-gatherer children from the Levant may have helped make small clay sculptures of birds, gazelle, humans and more (some shown).

This is a human-written story voiced by AI. Got feedback? Take our survey . (See our AI policy here .)

Children have been making art with help from grown-ups for millennia.

Fragmented clay figurines dating as far back as 15,000 years ago, discovered in what is now Israel, preserve the fingerprints of both adults and young children. The finding suggests the grown-ups taught the kids how to shape and decorate clay, researchers report November 11 in PLOS One.

Children’s contributions to prehistoric art and culture aren’t well understood. Concrete evidence of their involvement has been hard to find, says archaeologist Laurent Davin of the Hebrew University of Jerusalem.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

In recent decades, researchers have found a handful of objects made by children — including 200,000-year-old rock art bearing child-sized handprints in Tibet and 30,000-year-old clay sculptures pockmarked with child-sized fingerprints in Czech Republic.

Davin and colleagues hadn’t set out to find Stone Age children’s artwork. They were investigating whether the Natufian culture — a group of hunter-gatherers who formed some of the first permanent settlements in the Levant about 14,000 years ago — made clay art before the advent of agriculture in the region.

The archaeologists came across the first set of fingerprints while sorting through 40 kilograms of clay collected at three Natufian sites, which range in age from 15,000 to 11,600 years old. Among tens of thousands of clay lumps, the team found some 150 fragments of sculptures, including 15 recognizable bird, gazelle and human figures. Around 60 of these pieces preserved fingerprints from where people had pinched or prodded the clay.

The distances between the fingerprints’ ridges, which are influenced by age and sex, suggest that most of the figurines were made by women and children — some younger than 10. Only a few sets of fingerprints belonged to men. And the torso of one tiny human statue had both adult and child fingerprints, hinting that an elder and child had passed it back and forth. Davin suspects that the adult actively taught a child how to work the clay.

This evidence of intergenerational learning so far back in time is fascinating, says archaeologist Steven Dorland of Grand Valley State University in Allendale, Mich. It demonstrates how much researchers can learn from broadening their focus beyond adult males, he says. Dorland has used pottery to understand social learning in Indigenous societies of the Great Lakes region. He has even studied children’s fingerprints on some of those artifacts.

The purpose of the Natufian figurines is unclear. Most of the statues are less than 2 centimeters long and don’t display signs of intense use, the team found, suggesting they weren’t treated as precious heirlooms.

Davin suspects the figurines may have represented stories, like how kids draw Disney characters today, he says. “Maybe the idea is that the role of children here is not necessarily to learn to make figurines, but more to learn the story behind them.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

L. Davin et al. Modeling symbols among the first-sedentary communities: Emergence of clay figurines in Epipaleolithic Southwest Asia. PLOS One. 2026. doi: 10.1371/journal.pone.0360388

Freda Kreier was a fall 2021 intern at Science News. She holds a bachelor’s degree in molecular biology from Colorado College and a master’s in science communication from the University of California, Santa Cruz.

Sony’s latest Pulse wireless headset packs planar magnetic drivers at a good price

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A comfortable, minimalist option that supports concurrent low-latency audio and Bluetooth.

A comfortable, minimalist option that supports concurrent low-latency audio and Bluetooth.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s tempting to seek out the do-everything wireless gaming headset with noise cancellation, phenomenal sound quality, multiplatform connectivity, swappable batteries — you name it. But if you just want the basics done well, Sony’s new $99.99 PlayStation Pulse headset is worth considering. It has very good sound and just enough extras, even if you use more gadgets than just your PlayStation 5.

Inside the Pulse’s nondescript ear cup shells are custom planar magnetic drivers, which deliver clean, detailed sound whether you’re gaming, listening to music, or both. They aren’t bass-heavy or too shrill; their default equalizer is pleasing and punchy, though you can also tweak it in the PS5’s interface, like other Pulse headsets. They’re not Sony’s first audio gaming product to feature this type of high-end driver (the first was in 2023), but they are the most affordable at $99.

The Pulse comes with either a white or black headband, with a soft-touch underside and a decent amount of give near the top of your head where it rests its weight. Thankfully, the headset is lightweight and offers size adjustments that go beyond what my large head requires for comfort. These are some of the only headphones I’ve used with ear cups that can rotate 180 degrees in either direction.

Functionally, the Pulse stands out as a semi-affordable wireless headset that can stream audio from PlayStation Link (Sony’s low-latency, lossless wireless connection) and Bluetooth simultaneously. It includes a USB-C Link dongle that lets you pair it quickly with whatever you plug it into, whether it’s a PS5, PC, or Nintendo Switch 2.

The headset’s ear cup controls include a dedicated Bluetooth button that, beyond pairing with another gadget, lets you pause or resume Bluetooth content (it can also answer or hang up calls). You can also control Bluetooth audio volume using the integrated volume rocker, but only when a device isn’t connected via PlayStation Link. In simultaneous audio mode, the rocker adjusts audio solely for the device connected via the Link dongle.

If you have Sony’s PlayStation Portal, the Pulse can connect directly to the handheld without the Link dongle, just as Sony’s Pulse Elite and Explore can. It’s so convenient, and I hope Sony pushes for more devices that integrate the Link wireless technology in the future.

The Pulse isn’t the only headset that Sony has in the works. Its Pulse Edge wireless headset doesn’t have an exact launch date or price, but the company touts that it packs in twice as many magnets in its drivers (eight versus four), active noise cancellation, and a detachable boom mic that will likely provide better-sounding voice audio than the built-in mic in the standard Pulse. It will also include game / chat audio controls, a 3.5mm jack, and a carrying case.

While the $99 Pulse is comparatively light on features, Sony’s focus on providing standout audio quality and comfort makes it worth seeking out if you have a PS5 and are on a strict budget for your next wireless headset.

Arsenal receive mixed verdict after Liverpool penalty review

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

We’ve an important update on the Arsenal Liverpool penalty decision. Arsenal received a mixed reaction from the Premier League’s independent panel after their controversial win at Anfield.

The panel were unanimous in their decision that Liverpool should have been awarded a penalty after Jurrien Timber tugged Alexander Isak’s shirt. However, they were divided on whether it should have been reviewed by VAR.

The panel also cleared the officials on the incident involving Noni Madueke. This means that Arsenal were justified in their win due to the wrong decision being made on the penalty.

The incident happened during the 1-0 win for Arsenal at Anfield on November 1. Isak was trying to run onto a cross from Bradley Barcola after Kai Havertz had given the Gunners the lead.

As Timber pulled him back, he was unable to cleanly head the ball. The referee Andy Madley allowed play to continue. The decision was then made by VAR Stuart Attwell that there was not enough evidence to suggest a review.

The five-man Key Match Incidents panel has now ruled that this was the wrong decision. Four of the panel voted that Liverpool should have been awarded the spot kick after Timber’s handball.

However, there was a tight vote on whether VAR should have intervened. Three panellists felt that Attwell should have shown Madley to the pitchside monitor, whilst two felt it did not reach the threshold.

One panellist felt the length of the hold was not long enough to affect the movement of Isak. The other two members who felt that VAR should not have intervened also said that it is subjective as to how much the hold would affect the striker.

This is an important point to note. Whilst they agreed that the penalty was deserved, there was less agreement on whether it was VAR’s responsibility to overturn the decision.

There was also good news for Arsenal in the panel’s decision regarding Madueke’s late altercation with Alexis MacAllister.

The Liverpool midfielder fell to the floor following a headbutt from the Arsenal winger. The incident was reviewed by the VAR, but Madueke was spared a red card. Mac Allister received a yellow.

All five panel members agreed that Madueke should not have been sent off. They felt that there had not been any contact, and Mac Allister’s reaction was certainly excessive.

It certainly gives us an alternative standpoint into the situation. Whilst Arsenal benefited from the incorrect decision regarding the penalty, the officials were backed up with the disciplinary incident.

This won’t have any impact on the result of the Anfield game, as Havertz’s goal was the winning goal and gave Arsenal their first league victory at the stadium in 14 years.

However, it does show how risky Timber was to make the challenge and how close the panel were with whether they should have intervened with VAR.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/caresource-launches-1m-grant-program-support-ai-powered-solutions" hreflang="en">CareSource launches $1M grant program to support AI-powered solutions</a>

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →
CareSource launches $1M grant program to support AI-powered solutions" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Nonprofit insurer CareSource is launching a $1 million program that aims to back responsible AI solutions in healthcare.

The initiative, launched through the CareSource Foundation, is seeking proposals for ways to advance and identify deployments for AI that can drive better outcomes and address barriers for individuals enrolled in Medicaid or who otherwise struggle to access care.

The program will provide up to $1 million over the course of two years to ensure that the tool can deliver on meaningful and scalable benefits for the communities it serves.

Erhardt Preitauer, CEO of CareSource, said in an email to Fierce Healthcare that AI offers the potential to improve not just patient care but the experience for providers too as they navigate complex conditions.

"I'm a big believer that when technology, healthcare and the people in our communities rally around a shared purpose, magic can happen," said Preitauer. "Leaving no stone unturned is the only way we can make healthcare work better for people, and AI gives us the power to explore every possibility."

The program will include two phases. First, interested applicants can submit shorter proposals, with the goal of generating interest. Applications that are selected to move on will be tasked with submitting a more fleshed-out proposal, including details on implementation, AI governance and sustainability.

CareSource is aiming to identify solutions that are ready for implementation and has the potential for scale. Potential focus areas include care coordination, member engagement, behavioral health and rural healthcare. Solutions should use AI to support care delivery, but it should not replace human judgement or oversight, the insurer said.

To be eligible, a qualified nonprofit organization must be leading the effort, according to the announcement. Technology companies, academic researchers, healthcare organizations and community-based groups can also play a role as partners.

Applicants moving to the second phase of the program will also be required to showcase data privacy and security, solutions to address and prevent bias and ongoing performance tracking.

How to Be Grateful for Your Morning Cup of Coffee

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

The most trusted resource for mindfulness and meditation.

Writer AJ Jacobs explores what happens once you decide to thank everyone responsible for your morning coffee.

We’re used to saying a quick “thank you,” to the barista who serves us coffee, or the kind stranger who holds the door open as we leave the cafe. But how many people would you have to thank if you wanted to express gratitude to absolutely everyone responsible for your morning cup of coffee?

That’s a concept writer AJ Jacobs explores in his TED Talk and new book, Thanks a Thousand, based on his journey to thank everyone involved in getting that daily cup of joe into his hands.

While Jacobs initially believed his choice to focus on a cup of coffee would be a simple task, he soon learned that there were hundreds of people involved in producing the hot drink most of us take for granted every day.

“I would thank the trucker who drove the coffee beans to the coffee shop, but he couldn’t have done his job without the road. So I would thank the people who paved the road, and then I would thank the people who made the asphalt for the pavement,” Jacobs says. “And I came to realize that my coffee, like so much else in the world, requires the combined work of a shocking number of people from all walks of life.”

Why bother practicing gratitude in the first place? Jacobs, like many of us, found he often overlooked the positive aspects of his day and focused on the bad. By expressing gratitude when things went right, he was able to break out of his negative thoughts.

He explains: “It allowed me to focus on the hundreds of things that go right every day, as opposed to the three or four that go wrong.”

Here are five lessons he learned when he set out to thank everyone responsible for his morning pick me up:

Even if you start and end your gratitude journey by thanking your local barista (or local grocer, taxi driver, bartender, etc.) there’s a right way to do so. Namely: Look up from your phone and make eye contact.

“It reminds you, you’re dealing with a human being who has family and aspirations and embarrassing high school memories,” Jacobs says.

Taking a moment to connect with the person serving us can help us appreciate the people we interact with daily and show them that they are not “human vending machines.”

“That little moment of connection is so important to both people’s humanity and happiness,” Jacobs says.

As part of his mission to thank everyone, Jacobs reached out to Ed Kaufmann, who tests, tastes and selects coffee for a living. Jacobs was wowed by Kaufmann’s ability to notice all the small elements of flavor in coffee (notes of honeysuckle, for example) that most of us miss.

In our rush to finish our drink while getting ready for work, riding the subway, or taking notes in an office meeting, we often fail to slow down and truly notice what we’re tasting.

“Inspired by Ed, I decided to really let the coffee sit on my tongue for five seconds,” Jacobs says. “We’re all busy, but I could spare five seconds, and really think about the texture and the acidity and the sweetness.”

Doing so taught Jacobs the importance of savoring—the art of slowing down rather than consuming on autopilot.

“This idea of savoring is so important to gratitude,” he says. “Psychologists talk about how gratitude is about taking a moment and holding on to it as long as possible, and slowing down time so that life doesn’t go by in one big blur, as it often does.”

When you consider all that goes into a cup of coffee—the beans, the sugar, the milk—it’s easy to overlook the cup itself. Yet Jacobs says one of the best conversations he had throughout his project was with a man who creates coffee cup lids.

“He says a bad lid can ruin your coffee,” Jacobs recounts. “That it can block the aroma, which is so important to the experience. It made me realize there are hundreds of masterpieces all around us that we totally take for granted.”

By taking the time to notice the little things we can “tap into that sense of wonder and enrich our lives,” Jacobs says.

Once he began his gratitude journey, Jacobs realized his project of writing thank you notes each day was creating a larger shift in his life: that when he saw the impact that “thank you” had on others, it began to affect him as well.

If you act as if you’re grateful, you eventually become grateful for real.

But that process often started with a lot of grumpiness and not a small amount of toil.

“I would wake up every morning in my default mood, which is grumpiness, but I would force myself to write a thank-you note, and then another, and then another,” he explains.

Despite not feeling grateful at first, Jacobs forced himself to go through the actions of gratitude and eventually noticed a transformation in his “default mood.”

“What I found was that if you act as if you’re grateful, you eventually become grateful for real,” says Jacobs. “The power of our actions to change our mind is astounding. So often we think that thought changes behavior, but behavior very often changes our thought.”

For every one person Jacobs thanked, he learned there were many others he owed his gratitude to, people he would never be able to meet. It made him realize that sometimes, the best way to express gratitude for someone’s help is to help others in return.

“Some people worry that gratitude has a downside,” Jacobs says. “That we’ll be so grateful, that we’ll be complacent…Well, it turns out, the opposite is true. The research shows that the more grateful you are, the more likely you are to help others.”

For example, thanking the people whose job it is to provide clean water (essential to making coffee), caused him become involved with an organization called Dispensers for Safe Water whose mission is to provide drinking water to those in need.

“When you’re in a bad state, you’re often more focused on your own needs. But gratitude makes you want to pay it forward,” Jacobs concludes.

Nicole Bayes-Fleming holds an MSc in Gender, Media and Culture from the London School of Economics, where she researched the relationship between social media, knowledge production, and feminist social justice movements.

She has a bachelor’s in journalism, and has formerly worked as a digital editor and freelance reporter with bylines in Mindful Magazine, the Globe and Mail, FLARE, Ottawa Citizen, Ottawa Magazine, and HalifaxToday.ca.
