Arsenal receive positive Piero Hincapie injury update ahead of return

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Piero Hincapie has provided some good news for an Arsenal team that have struggled at the start of the season by confirming that he is now fully fit.

The defender has had two separate injury issues since joining Mikel Arteta’s squad but feels he is now over the setbacks and ready to play regularly.

Hincapie is playing for Ecuador in their November international fixtures. His inclusion in the squad was also a positive, as he was unable to play in their last camp due to injury.

Hincapie initially missed Ecuador’s September squad because of a groin problem. He had been named among the substitutes for Arsenal’s final match before that break, against Brighton.

After recovering, the 24-year-old returned to action in the 2-1 Premier League win over Leeds United on October 10. However, he suffered another setback during the warm-down after the match.

The ankle injury was particularly unfortunate, with Hincapie having to leave the pitch with assistance from goalkeeper Illan Meslier. He subsequently missed Arsenal’s matches against Lille and Nottingham Forest.

Mikel Arteta had at first suggested that Hincapie could be out for a matter of weeks, but the defender has since rejoined individual training.

He has now given a much more positive update on his injury whilst on international duty. He is now “100% fit” and does not have any niggles.

This is great news for Arteta. He has had to be cautious with his defensive choices throughout the campaign.

Hincapie will have a chance to get some much-needed game time while he is included in the squad to play Ecuador.

He has been included in the squad by Marcelo Gallardo to face Costa Rica (13th November) and Bolivia (17th November) in friendly games. This will be the first games Ecuador play at home under their new head coach.

Hincapie has also expressed his optimism for his initial discussions with Gallardo. The player is excited to work under the former River Plate manager and help the national team.

He should prove to be a valuable asset in the squad, considering he can play in both roles. He is a natural centre back but can also play as a left back.

Hincapie has shown that he is happy to play anywhere in the team his manager requires. This can be an important factor for Arsenal as the fixture list gets tougher.

He has only featured three times in all competitions this season and has yet to play in the Premier League. Consistent playing time will be key if he is to become a regular in Arteta’s plans.

However, his latest update shows that he is no longer struggling with the physical issues. If he can stay fit over the international break, he will provide another key defensive option for Arsenal in the coming weeks.

How Two WWII Fighter Pilots Hacked Through the New Guinea Jungle for the Best Duck Hunt of Their Lives

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 11, 2026 1:10 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

This story, “Ducks Can Improve Any War,” appeared in the December 1944 issue of Outdoor Life.

A duck hunter is always a duck hunter. No matter how long he’s been away from the sport, he still longs for that song of the wind on the lake reeds. He craves the acrid smell of burning powder and the comfortable feel of his favorite gun. He never forgets the beauty of the cold gray mists of dawn, the hazy silhouettes of decoys, the tense, exciting wait, and finally, the sight of fast gray wings above him.

You duck hunters know exactly what I mean, but I doubt if any of you ever felt the old longing as much as I did that day in New Guinea in February, 1943. Yes, New Guinea — hot, steaming, sultry. I had been in the Pacific area for eighteen months, flying a fighter. Four of those months were combat flying. I was getting in plenty of hunting — a serious kind of hunting that I enjoyed — when late one afternoon while sitting on alert, a flock of ducks, real live ducks, passed right over our pilot’s hut and lit in a near-by swamp.

When those ducks flew over, two old-time duck hunters jumped to their feet and watched ’em till they’d disappeared. Then they turned and looked at each other. They didn’t say anything; they didn’t have to. One of those duck hunters was Lieut. Fred Sibley from Maine. I was the other one. And right then and there the war we were in had improved 1,000 percent.

When we opened the boxes of No. 6s we found that half the shells were bloated by the damp atmosphere, and wouldn’t fit into the guns. So we had to take our knives and patiently whittle each shell down until it would slip into the chamber.

We started right in, lining things up for our next day off. We dug up a couple of old double-barrel guns from the squadron supply and, after making the rounds through several camps, found some honest-to-goodness 12 gauge ammunition a pilot had bought in Australia. We finally succeeded in heckling the officer into turning some of it over to us — by promising him a big fat duck for dinner.

Now, this was really an effective offer, since his squadron had been practically living on bully beef for six months. It didn’t hurt our consciences to take advantage of the poor man, as we knew we could feed a lot of hungry soldiers if we could get him to come across.

Anyway, we got back to camp with two boxes of No. 6’s. Later, when we opened them we found that half the shells were bloated by the damp atmosphere, and wouldn’t fit into the guns. So we had to take our knives and patiently whittle each shell down until it would slip into the chamber. Then we gave every shell a coat of grease to protect it from the humidity until the big day.

We had found previously from wandering around in the jungle that it was absolutely imperative for a man to sprinkle himself with some kind of sulphur as a protection against the chiggers and ticks. These tiny parasites were dangerous not only because their bites caused infections which took weeks to heal in that climate, but because they were known to carry a tropical disease. We also knew it was necessary to use mosquito lotion as millions of these pests swarmed everywhere.

Our problem, then, was to concoct something that wouldn’t wash off our bodies when we were wading through swamps and marsh land after those ducks. We hit on the bright idea of mixing the sulphur powder with vaseline, and it worked like a charm. The mosquito lotion was no problem as we could carry plenty of it along in a small bottle. For extra protection we each obtained a mosquito head net and a pair of leggings. Two canteens of water completed our preparations in the way of equipment for the unique duck hunt.

We still had a couple of days to go before our day off, so we decided to do some aërial scouting. We knew we’d be doing some flying in those remaining days, and that while on our landing patterns we would have an opportunity to study the swamp and determine the best possible way of getting into it from our camp.

To appreciate the importance of this, one must imagine a typical Florida swamp surrounded by the most dense undergrowth in which, every five or six feet, giant trees rear their heads. Undergrowth and trees are connected by a maze of hanging moss and tough vines, the only trails being pig runs and an occasional hidden native trail that no white man can follow. The only way a white man can get through such stuff is to cut and fight his way through. So our problem was to determine the spot where we could cut our way through with the least effort. We studied the jungle carefully during the next two days’ flying, and found that a few miles from our camp a grassy hill cut through the jungle to within about a quarter of a mile of the swamp. We could get to this hill easily from the camp, and figured that with the aid of a compass we should be able to bore through the jungle to the swamp itself in two or three hours. Our plans were now complete.

The big day came at last. It dawned misty and cool — for New Guinea — as near a perfect duck-hunting day as that part of the world could have provided. And it found Fred and me hacking our way into the jungle surrounding the swamp. We looked more like a couple of jungle soldiers than we did duck hunters. We had our .45’s on our belts for use on snakes or crocodiles, and also because neither of us was sure there might not be a stray Japanese pilot hiding in the brush. We knew several had recently bailed out or crash-landed in the vicinity.

We looked more like a couple of jungle soldiers than we did duck hunters. We had our .45’s on our belts for use on snakes or crocodiles, and also because neither of us was sure there might not be a stray Japanese pilot hiding in the brush.

Our heads were covered by our mosquito nets, and our canteen and ammunition belts rattled as we whacked at the undergrowth with a big jungle knife, taking turns at hacking and resting.

All through the jungle we encountered many varieties of beautiful birds. Most of them were of the parrot or pigeon families, and were of every color of the rainbow. Some were as small as wrens, others much larger than domestic pigeons. Once we caught a glimpse of an Australian jungle turkey, a magnificent bird almost as large as the American wild turkey, but were unable to get a shot at it in the thick undergrowth. We also saw a couple of dark brown jungle chickens of the grouse variety. Later, in another part of New Guinea, I was fortunate enough to kill several of these birds, and they were very delicious, the meat being light and tender. But this story is about duck hunting — and what duck hunting!

In exactly one hour and thirty minutes after the first swing of our jungle knife we broke into the swamp — and, believe me, it was beautiful to behold.

“Look at them, Dave, look at them!” Fred was wild with excitement.

Ducks were everywhere — circling the swamps by the hundreds, quacking, whistling, and splashing down into the open spot among the tall reeds.

We stood there just looking for a long time. Then Fred said, “Dave, you know what we’ve got to do?”

Ducks were everywhere — circling the swamps by the hundreds, quacking, whistling, and splashing down into the open spot among the tall reeds.

“Yes, I’m afraid I do,” I replied. “But it’s sure going to be hard to remember all the hungry people at camp when those singles start flying over. Boy! I wish we had plenty of shells.”

“Yeah,” sighed Fred, “or not such a large family to feed.”

I had an idea. “I’ll tell you what let’s do,” I said. “Let’s each of us get fifteen ducks with as few shells as possible, and then use the rest of the shells for singles. That will give us at least thirty ducks to feed the sixty guys back at camp.”

“O.K., Dave, we’ve got to do it, I guess,” agreed Fred. “It’s going to seem real unsportsmanlike to take pot-shots, but bully beef is sure getting tiresome. Let’s go!”

Neither of us relished shooting those beautiful birds on the water, but this was one time we had to bring home the bacon.

We decided that since the length of the swamp lay north and south we would separate, Fred going south and I going north, this way keeping the ducks moving back and forth between us. So, out we started. The water in the swamp proved to be from ankle to almost waist deep. The bottom, however, was solid, so walking was not difficult. Most of the swamp was covered with thick dead reeds about six feet high that broke easily as we waded through them. In the midst of these reeds were a great many small open spots where some kind of short green water grass grew which the ducks fed upon. These openings were also the feeding grounds of many different kinds of wading birds which swarmed up with the ducks as we approached. Then there were several flocks of large white herons that seemed almost psychic, as they would take wing long before we reached their resting place, circle, climb high into the sky, and disappear into the distance. They alone seemed to leave the swamp. All other birds, including the ducks, would fly to some other clearing in the swamp and settle there.

Related: We Had a Week Off from Active Combat, So I Went Trout Fishing. I Got Pinned Down by a Sniper

Hundreds of large coots were present too. They were about twice the size of any I had seen before, and looked like American birds, except they had brilliant red bills.

Before I had made my way very far into the swamp I heard Fred fire twice and, looking in his direction, saw hundreds of ducks getting up. A dozen black mallards passed right over my head. How I wanted to shoot! But didn’t, remembering my promise to Fred to get my first fifteen with as few shells as possible.

Most of the ducks lit about 100 yards north of me, so I began creeping in that direction, making as little noise as possible. As I crept closer through very thick reeds I came to a small opening no more than ten yards in diameter, and, lo and behold, there sat about twenty honest-to-goodness greenwing teal. They saw me and shot up like a covey of quail. I caught them just as they rose, and fired once, knocking down three. I had a tough time recovering them in the reeds, but finally found them all. At the sound of the shot the mallards got up about seventy-five yards away and circled for some ten minutes, settling back down in the same place about the time I had recovered my last teal. With the teal in my belt I crept toward the mallards, crawling through the ankle-deep water on my knees and one hand, holding my gun in the other, as the reeds were not tall in this area.

How I wanted to shoot! But didn’t, remembering my promise to Fred to get my first fifteen with as few shells as possible.

When I reached the edge of the clearing where the mallards were I saw they were all about fifty yards away on the opposite side. About halfway across the clearing was a small clump of tall reeds that I thought I might be able to keep between the mallards and myself until I could get them a little more in range.

I started crawling toward those reeds, making every effort to keep the ducks from seeing me but, alas, ducks seem to be as wary in one part of the world as another. Just as I got started they spotted me, and off they went in every direction. The largest bunch of them circled over the edge of the swamp and then seemed to disappear into the trees. This I believed to be an illusion — ducks don’t light on trees, especially mallards.

By this time I had decided that it was too hot for crawling and that I would let Fred do the work for awhile. So I took my station in the clump of reeds in the center of the clearing and waited for him to send some ducks my way. I took off my head net, wiped the perspiration from my face, applied some mosquito lotion, and put the net into my pocket, deciding I could shoot better without it.

After about ten minutes’ waiting I heard Fred open up again, firing three times, and, looking in his direction, I saw them coming. There they were, hundreds of them! And from then on, things went too fast to tell about. All the ducks seemed to want to light in the clearing I commanded — and I did command every inch of it!

There they were, hundreds of them. And from then on, things went too fast to tell about. All the ducks seemed to want to light in the clearing I commanded — and I did command every inch of it.

First, a big flock of mallards tried to light within ten yards of me, and I opened up with both barrels just as they were settling, getting four of them. A bunch of pintails came in right after them; then teal, and more mallards. I was firing as fast as I could reload. I could hear Fred doing the same thing and knew he was in their favorite clearing at the other end of the swamp. I had never seen so many ducks at one time, and they were flying back and forth between the two of us. The boys at camp were all going to get a real meal.

After about fifteen minutes of the fastest shooting I have ever done I took inventory. I found I had seventeen ducks — and four shells left! And I actually had four black mallards, a couple of beautiful little ducks that I couldn’t identify, three pintails, and several greenwing, bluewing, and cinnamon teal. The teal were larger than any I had seen in the states, but were exactly like our teal in every other respect.

Since I had more than my quota of fifteen birds I felt I could use the remaining four precious shells for the tough shots that I have always loved so much. So I took my station and waited.

A high flying mallard came in fast and weaving. It was one of those shots you make once in a lifetime and never forget. I led him plenty and just hoped. He came down from way up there like a rock, and I felt a thrill inside I hadn’t come close to feeling for two years.

A minute later two teal came in fast and low. I caught one with the first shot, but missed the second. But the first bird came down so clean I didn’t feel badly about the miss. With my remaining shell I stopped another mallard, not as high as the first, but it was a sweet shot.

I gathered up my birds and started back to meet Fred. We met just in front of our trail that led back into the jungle, and as we waded through the last few yards of swamp Fred said, “Look at ’em, Dave. We’ve got thirty-five of the most beautiful ducks I ever saw!”

A bunch of pintails came in right after them; then teal, and more mallards. I was firing as fast as I could reload. I could hear Fred doing the same thing and knew he was in their favorite clearing at the other end of the swamp.

We reached the edge of the jungle and sat down to rest. As we sat there we beheld a most astonishing sight — about ten black mallards flew right out of the tree we were sitting under and winged their way back into the swamp. Believe it or not! Mallards do light in trees, in New Guinea anyway.

We started home and Fred said, “It’s a funny war, isn’t it? Black mallards lighting in trees; our belts loaded with ducks, and a bunch of hungry boys back at camp to appreciate them more than any ducks were ever appreciated before. I think, Dave, today has been the most wonderful day of my life.”

Related: I Hunted Ducks from a U.S. Naval Destroyer During the Korean War

I was glad Fred said that, because it was his last duck hunt. Fred went down fighting — doing his bit to guarantee a free and fearless future full of “wonderful days” for all of us.

How a 20-Year-Old Texas Climber Lost His Life on the Matterhorn

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Kieran Liner, a 20-year-old climber based in Austin, TX, died in an accident on the Matterhorn in October. (Photo: Courtesy Precenia Newman duplicate)

A 20-year-old climber named Kieran Liner from Austin, Texas, died on October 27 after falling from the Matterhorn’s Hörnli Ridge. He was climbing with his friend Eli Hawkins.

Valais Cantonal Police said the two climbers were ascending shortly after 6 a.m. when rocks fell near them at about 11,500 feet. Hawkins lost sight of Liner and immediately called for help. An Air Zermatt helicopter responded. Rescuers found Liner below the route and pronounced him dead at the scene.

Prosecutors opened an investigation to determine the precise circumstances of the fall. The initial police statement reported rockfall nearby, but didn’t specify exactly how Liner fell or died.

Hawkins, who says he met Liner in Austin around five years ago, called him his “best bud” and his climbing partner. “It is what it is,” Hawkins told Climbing. “He died doing what he loved.”

Originally from Pensacola, Florida, Liner had lived in Austin since 2020. According to Hawkins, Liner liked Modest Mouse, IPAs, and American Spirit Blues. But what he loved most, Hawkins wrote, was climbing in the mountains.

In an obituary shared last week, Liner’s sister Precenia Newman remembered her younger brother as a skateboarder, golfer, and alpinist—someone who brought the same enthusiasm to everything he pursued. “He approached everything in life with 150% of himself,” Newman wrote.

Newman described a close circle of friends who had become family through shared adventures and everyday life. Among them was Hawkins, whose friendship and life as Liner’s roommate she singled out as especially meaningful. She also remembered the love surrounding Liner from his parents, Justine Hamel and Nick Liner, as well as his siblings, grandparents, aunts, uncles, nieces, and nephews. Newman has organized a fundraiser to bring him home so the family can celebrate his life in Pensacola and Austin.

While Hawkins grew up climbing as a kid with his father and uncle, Liner got into the sport later in life, but it quickly became his passion. Hawkins said the two of them had strong mentors, including Hawkins’s dad Stephen, Ben Darce, and his uncle Bryan Hawkins who owns a climbing gym in Everett, Washington. “He was fearless,” Hawkins said of Liner. “I was fearless—still am. I’m going to keep climbing for him. We just climbed hard and partied hard.”

This past July, Liner had started an Instagram account called @mountkieran dedicated primarily to climbing and mountaineering content. His first reel captured climbing Mt. Rainier. Most of his content leaned humorous, with jokes about “Conrad Anger” and poking fun at himself as an “Austin influencer” while board climbing.

But occasionally, Liner shared more serious content, like footage of ascending a steep snow pitch with a quote from Reinhold Messner: “To go on an adventure, you need difficulties, you need danger. If death were not a possibility, coming out would be nothing.”

Generally considered the most popular and easiest route by which to ascend the Matterhorn, the Hörnli Ridge rises roughly 4,000 vertical feet from the Hörnli Hut to the 14,692-foot summit. Rated AD (“fairly difficult”) on the alpine scale, the route combines sustained exposed scrambling with technical rock climbing rated around 5.4. In general, parties take four to five hours for the ascent, plus another five to six hours for the descent, which involves downclimbing and rappelling much of the same terrain.

Fixed ropes assist climbers on certain sections, but parties also bring their own ropes. Snow and ice can be encountered near the summit, requiring crampons and an ice axe. The technical grade captures only part of the challenge of navigating and descending that exposed terrain in mountaineering boots.

To learn more about the Hörnli Ridge where Liner’s accident took place, I reached out to my friend Steph Abegg, who claimed the fastest known time by a woman on the route this past summer.

In 1985, Abegg’s dad soloed the Matterhorn in a T-shirt. Twenty years later, when Abegg and her sister were in Zermatt and decided to climb it, that was the image they held in their minds. Their dad was an experienced climber, and he’d enjoyed ideal conditions on his ascent.

Accordingly, the Abegg sisters expected a casual adventure. Instead, they encountered verglas—a thin glaze of ice on the rock—and a whiteout. They turned around at the Solvay Hut, an emergency shelter at 13,133 feet. At this point, they were roughly 2,440 vertical feet above the Hörnli Hut, where the climb begins, and still about 1,560 feet below the summit.

“It could be said I underestimated the Matterhorn myself in 2005,” Abegg told me. “Had we continued, it’s entirely possible we could have gotten into trouble.”

This past summer, Abegg returned, climbing the Hörnli Ridge with a partner before completing a solo, unsupported round trip from Zermatt in 10 hours, 8 minutes, and 19 seconds—the fastest women’s time then recorded on the FKT website, supported or unsupported. A month later, Kalie McCrystal lowered the record to 6 hours, 47 minutes, and 33 seconds.

The route’s moderate technical grade, fixed ropes, and popularity can make the Hörnli Ridge seem approachable. But finding the line and staying on solid rock demands attention. Stray from the standard route and the rock can become looser. Altitude, changing weather, and fall consequences add to the difficulty.

During Abegg’s partnered ascent this past summer, a washing-machine-sized boulder was accidentally dislodged. It tumbled down a gully, triggering what she described as a significant rockslide, mostly off route. Higher up, loose rock littered the climbing line. Abegg described rocks ranging in size from marbles to baseballs, or even volleyballs, that climbers could knock onto people below. The terrain, she said, had historically remained under snow year-round.

“Had to move very carefully,” she wrote. Guides stopped taking clients up the route during her trip.

Aside from the omnipresent risk of rockfall in any alpine environment, a 2025 study found that climate change has caused an increase in rockfall on the Matterhorn as permafrost thaws. Just a couple months ago, German online magazine Lacrux published an article about the increasing hazards of rockfall on the Matterhorn, as well as on Mont Blanc.

“Having the innate mountain sense to know when to call it is a valuable quality on an objective like this,” Abegg said.

Andy Hansen, an IFMGA-certified (International Federation of Mountain Guides) guide based in Longmont, Colorado, has guided clients on the Matterhorn twice. Speaking from his own experience, he strongly recommends hiring a guide for a first attempt.

He said recreational climbers in Western Europe are generally more accustomed to hiring guides than their counterparts in the United States. One advantage of hiring a guide on the Hörnli Ridge is how closely they follow variable conditions on a route, according to Hansen.

“The local guides have an intimacy with the mountain that gives them the advantage of having the route finding dialed, understanding the conditions of the mountain from day to day, and knowing where and when to move quickly to manage the rockfall hazard,” Hansen told me.

Hansen observed an unusual amount of rockfall in the Alps this past spring and summer, calling the Hörnli Ridge noticeably more unstable. He pointed to local guides’ familiarity with the ridge and their day-to-day knowledge of its conditions. A weather forecast can tell any climber how warm it’s likely to get, but knowing the ridge helps a guide judge which sections are most prone to rockfall, where to move quickly to limit exposure, and whether to climb that day at all.

However, whether a guide would have changed the outcome for Liner is an entirely different question. “It’s virtually impossible to say with any degree of certainty that had this party been with a mountain guide, the outcome would have been any different,” Hansen said. In his view, it was the kind of misfortune that could befall a guided party, too.

Hawkins told Climbing that they were aware of the risk of rockfall and had accepted it as a possible and uncontrollable hazard that you can’t “really do anything about.” He added that he never considered hiring a guide because he has sufficient experience and wants to take on alpine objectives independently. “If you do hire a guide, you didn’t climb the mountain,” he said of his philosophy.

While a guide is not required to climb the Matterhorn, it does materially improve your chances of reaching the summit. According to Global Summit Guide, an independent, research-based mountaineering resource based in Utah, the summit rate varies considerably depending on whether climbers are traveling with a guide.

A Global Summit Guide report updated this past July states that while the overall Matterhorn success rate is 55%, guided climbers see a 72% success rate, while unguided climbers hover around a 38% success rate. Meanwhile, first-timers like Liner and Hawkins only have an 18% shot at reaching the top. Furthermore, during the popular climbing season of May through October, October has the lowest success rate of 22%.

Global Summit Guide also reinforces the Matterhorn’s reputation as Europe’s most deadly mountain, with an average of eight to 12 fatalities annually. They note that about 1 in 140 climbers perish in their attempt to summit. More than 500 people (and counting) have died on the Matterhorn to date.

Dialogue and debate over the broader trends indirectly related to Liner’s death have already  emerged in the climbing community.

After Liner died, a Reddit thread in r/Mountaineering sparked debate about the climbing-related content he and Hawkins had posted on their Instagram accounts. “Obviously, there are videos of me and him partying in Europe, but when we’re actually climbing, I take precautions and I’m not an idiot when I climb,” Hawkins told Climbing. “I take safety as a number one precaution.” He added that Liner would “probably be laughing” at what Hawkins sees as misinformed comments online.

Then, in an Instagram reel posted on November 2, Washington-based gear manufacturer High Mountain Gear turned the discussion toward a different topic: a video Norwegian climber Magnus Midtbø made about his 2025 Matterhorn solo. High Mountain Gear took particular issue with Midtbø looking up how to use crampons shortly before his climb. In the comments of High Mountain Gear’s reel, some agreed that a climber with Midtbø’s reach ought to think about the example he sets. Others asked why he was being brought into a conversation about someone else’s accident.

Hawkins explained that he and Liner had set the Matterhorn as a big goal independent of any online content. “It’s just a climb we wanted to do for a long time,” Hawkins said of the Matterhorn. “It had nothing to do with any Instagram videos.” He noted that he wasn’t very familiar with Midtbø’s Matterhorn video.

Beyond this recent incident, when Midtbø’s video came out, Swiss public broadcaster SRF reported that Midtbø talked openly about his limited preparation. He struggled with the altitude and got off route. Other climbers had to call him back to the right line. Once he got down, he admitted he had underestimated the risk and urged viewers against following his example. Air Zermatt’s rescue chief Anjan Truffer told SRF that he worried viewers might still interpret Midtbø’s climb as a success, without appreciating the gap between his climbing ability and their own.

In Midtbø’s defense, however, he could have cut the moments when he was tired, lost, or scared. But he chose to leave them in. Some viewers in the YouTube comments took the climb as a cautionary tale. Others wanted a follow-up with a guide explaining where things nearly went wrong. Ultimately, given the irrelevance of Midtbø’s video to Hawkins’s and Liner’s Matterhorn objective, as well as the full disclosures that Midtbø provided in his video, the notion that YouTube content is to blame for this or any other fatality seems besides the point.

As a guide, Hansen sees responsibility on both sides of the screen. He said creators like Midtbø have some accountability for what they put out, even when they include a disclaimer. But viewers must assess whether their own skills match the demands of any climb described in online content.

Abegg’s father came home with a story about soloing the Matterhorn in a T-shirt. Abegg and her sister came home with a story about turning around at the Solvay Hut. Midtbø came home, too. But in his video, you can see how close he came to a different outcome when he lost the route on exposed terrain.

Kieran Liner didn’t come home. He was 20 years old. The mountain doesn’t care what drew you there, whose story you heard, or how long you spent preparing. Even the most experienced climber can be in the wrong place at the wrong time, especially when it comes to escalating hazards like rockfall.

Liner’s family is planning to celebrate his life in Pensacola and Austin. In her obituary, Newman invited everyone who knew him, shared his adventures, or found inspiration in his life to join them. “Whether you knew him for a lifetime or crossed paths with him only briefly,” she wrote, “you are part of the story he leaves behind.”

Climbing Magazine reached out to Kyle Willis of High Mountain Gear and Magnus Midtbø for comment, but have not received a response.

Our 30 Unwritten Rules of Triathlon

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Tim Heming outlines the comical 30 unwritten rules of triathlon that every athlete should know. (Photo: Challenge Family)

Like a lot of sports, triathlon is full of rules. For example, you’re not allowed to cut the course, have a motor on your bike, curse at officials, punch a rival, or, most craven of all, have fewer than three safety pins on your race number in France.

However, and particularly helpful for beginners, there are plenty more unwritten rules that we need to be aware of. Some of them, we’ve helpfully outlined below:

1. Procrastination is Expensive — Swear you’ll sign up at the early bird rate, procrastinate, and then find out your chosen Ironman sold out within 24 hours anyway.

2. Forever Alone — The training paradox. The more you do triathlons, the more all your friends are triathletes, and the more you will train alone.

3. You Are Not Kristian — The Norwegian Method is to train more hours in a day than you do not train. For more training methods, see YouTube.

4. The Devil’s in the Details — Dedicate 20 hours a week to training for three months in the lead up to your A race. Spend zero minutes working on transition.

5. Shelve Twitter Hate — Write a mantra on your water bottle and start an Instagram account for reels, stories, and collabs. Don’t be an angry person on Twitter. That’s not cool.

6. Match at All Costs — Never sacrifice style for performance. Color-coded kit and equipment are essential, unless you’re going for garish retro, which is an acceptable look for 2026 only.

7. Weather or Not? — Check the weather forecast and water temperature before checking the weather forecast and water temperature, and then solicit opinions on what the weather and water temperature are likely to be on race day.

8. Wetsuit Your Worries Away — Convince yourself you can’t swim without a wetsuit/won’t be able to get your wetsuit off if it’s a wetsuit swim.

9. Experience Equals Complacency — The athlete guide must be studied with a focus that inversely correlates to how many races the athlete has done.

10. Selfie First, Reality Later — Take a selfie with the giant M-Dot “Ironman Is In Town” sign. Post this to social media as soon as you land, feigning a smile because although you’ve arrived, your bike hasn’t yet followed.

11. Hell’s Bells — Set three alarms minimum and then wonder why the first of them is going off as you force-feed yourself rice because you couldn’t sleep anyway.

12. Nothing Matters as Much as Everything — Take time to stare at your race tattoo, realize the transfer has only half stuck, and have a mild panic by convincing yourself you’ll be disqualified before the start.

13. Apply Liberally — Use sunscreen, making sure you miss a key patch of skin. It will show more of your suffering.

14. Pay it Forward — In the name of karma, offer to: a) Zip up someone’s wetsuit; b) Lend them a bike pump (even if it’s not yours); c) Thank everyone you see profusely and wish them all a great day.

15. Ride the Intake Earthquake — Increase your volume of carbohydrates per hour exponentially with the same dedication you had when you raced 15 years ago and were insistent on using only your 5% body fat as a fuel source.

16. Be a Bathroom Banterer — Talk with bravado about your trained ability to both pee in the swim and on the bike while you stand for an hour in a revolving porta potty line.

17. What’s Good for the Goose is Good for the Gander — Oops! You punched someone in the eye in the swim completely by accident. Also, the breaststroker you got stuck behind at the first buoy deliberately kicked you in the face.

18. Meditate on Aquatic Paradoxes — During the swim, reflect on: a) Whoever learned to swim as a kid is basically a cheater. Or b) “Why do triathletes swim so freaking slowly?”

19. Style Reigns — Replace existing drink bottles on the bike with those from the on-course sponsor. Call bottles bidons if you’ve ever watched a clip of the Tour de France.

20. The Refs Need You (Really) — Become militant about drafting and shout obscenities at a pack who: a). Aren’t in the race anyway; b) Are out for a gentle social weekend ride; c) Are still moving faster than you.

21. Ride the Highs and Lows — When you are riding downhill, you think you will win. When you are riding uphill, you think you will come last.

22. Roll the Dice — Breathe a sigh of relief when you complete the bike leg without puncturing because you haven’t practiced changing tires for five years.

23. Don’t be Patronizing Pat — If you pass someone on the run, resist the urge to tap them encouragingly on the back. One athlete’s chirpy come-on is another athlete’s patronizing pat. And if you must do it, always avoid the backside.

24. Leave Self-Awareness Back at the Hotel — Do something in the finish chute that in any other walk of life you would find cringey. This includes sweaty mess marriage proposals to a long-suffering partner who has a cooked neck from staring at a race tracker for the past 14 hours.

25. Be Congratulating Carl — Always congratulate the first person you see at the finish. However hollow-eyed your expression or soiled your tri suit. It’s your duty to have that mutual moment with a complete stranger.

26. The Rules of Race Photography are Unbending — Acknowledge that official race photographs only ever capture: 1. Grotesque faces. 2: When you “briefly” sat up out of aero. 3. Ugly heel strikes

27. You’re the Best — Use multiple filters of the race results, including but not limited to age-groups, disciplines (including transitions), and nationalities to claim a top-10 position at some aspect of your race. If you rode a road bike, you can also disqualify everyone who rode a TT/tri bike.

28. Brutality Reigns — Include the word “brutal” at least three times during any social media race report.

29. There Are Steps — Once you have finished your first sprint triathlon, take the following actions (in order): a) Sign up for an Ironman, b) Sign up for a Hyrox, c) Advertise your coaching services.

30. Triathlon = Procrastination (Always) — Finally, swear you’ll sign up at the early bird rate, procrastinate, and then find out your chosen Ironman sold out within 24 hours anyway.

Planets Don't Just Disappear, But They Can Wobble Out of Sight if Their Neighbour is Close Enough

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

NASA's TESS mission was a success by almost any measure. So far, as of mid-October 2026, it's identified more than 8,100 candidate exoplanets, and just over 1,000 of them have been confirmed. The mission is still ongoing, and astronomers are also finding more exoplanets in its archival data with new machine learning techniques.

TESS was primarily aimed at finding smaller super-Earths and sub-Neptunes, but it also found many hot-Jupiters during its mission. One of them, about 1,000 light years away, is a hot Jupiter named TOI-2535b and it exhibited some strange behaviour. TESS observed multiple transits by TOI-2535b over seven years, then the planet seemed to just disappear.

Planets can't just disappear, unless the Death Star is nearby, and astronomers have wondered what's behind the hot Jupiter's disappearing act. New research in The Astrophysical Journal Letters has a natural explanation for the vanished star. It's titled "A TESS Disappearing Act: The Vanishing Transits of TOI-2535 b," and the corresponding author is Samuel Yee, a postdoctoral fellow at UCLA.

"Planetary transits require a fortuitous alignment between a planetâs orbit and our line of sight, but dynamical interactions can render this geometric coincidence only temporary," the authors write in their research letter. "We report the discovery of the hot Jupiter TOI-2535b, the first confirmed planet orbiting a single star whose transits have been directly observed to disappear."

Detecting planets via the transit method only works if everything lines up, as the authors explain. But everything doesn't necessarily stay lined up. Dynamical interactions abound in solar systems, as we know from our own Solar System's history. Giant planets have migrated, ending old, stable orbital patterns and heralding new ones. Interactions can scatter smaller objects, and even send comets careening into the inner Solar System.

But a massive shift isn't required to upset the finely-tuned line of sight that allows us to see distant exoplanets as they transit their stars.

"Because a planet is only observed to transit when its orbit normal is within a few degrees of the sky plane, even small changes in a planetâs orbital inclination can result in measurable changes in its transit shape," the authors explain. "For sufficiently large inclination changes, a planet may only have the correct transit geometry for a fraction of its dynamical evolution."

Here's the kicker: "If the variation is fast enough and we are lucky to catch the planet in the right phase of its inclination evolution, we may see its transits appear or disappear," the researchers write.

This figure presents some of the research's findings. The first panel shows TESS transits from sectors 10, 63, and 90. Sectors are rectangular strips of the sky that TESS observes. The second panel shows the movement of the transit chord across the stellar disk. The dark circle shows TOI-2535b's size relative to its star. Image Credit: Samuel W. Yee et al 2026 ApJL 1011 L60

That's what happened with TOI-2535b, according to this work, and it's probably because of another exoplanet interacting with the hot Jupiter.

By watching it over several years, astronomers observed TOI-2535b's orbit in detail. It didn't just suddenly disappear. Both the timing and the shape of the transit slowly changed.

This can happen with circumbinary exoplanets, those that orbit a binary star. "Circumbinary planets frequently exhibit rapid changes in their transit impact parameters," the authors write. "As a result, even a small misalignment between the two orbits leads to intervals when the planet may transit both, one, or neither of its host stars."

But TOI-2535b orbits a single star, making its disappearance extremely unusual. "Around single stars, inclination variations of planets are typically less dramatic and appear to be relatively rare," the researchers explain. But that's what seems to be happening here due to the influence of another exoplanet.

âWe think of the universe as resembling clockwork, where everything is regular, but thatâs not true. If there are multiple planets in a system, they can affect each otherâs orbits. We know of a few systems that are like this,â said Yee. âThis is the most extreme example weâve seen because the two planets are interacting so strongly. The planetâs orbit is tilting so quickly that its transit disappeared while we were watching.â

This work rests partly on what are called Transit Duration Variations (TDV). These are small yet measurable and periodic changes in the amount of time it takes for an exoplanet to complete a transit in front of its star. TOI-2535b's TDVs are unusual, according to the authors, and so are the changes in its orbital inclination.

"The TOI-2535 system is an unusual and exceptional system in many ways. Observationally, it has the largest TDVs and inclination change ever observed for a planet orbiting a single star, with a precession period of decades, shorter than other known systems," the authors explain. The system also has some of the largest Transit Timing Variations (TTVs) ever detected, and they're definitely the largest ever detected for a hot Jupiter.

The researchers say that another, neighbouring exoplanet of Saturn-mass or greater is tugging on the exoplanet's orbit, making it "disappear." This is surprising, since hot Jupiter's aren't expected to have any close neighbours. In the rare cases where they do have neighbours, the neighbours are quite a bit less massive.

"This makes TOI-2535b one of only a handful of hot Jupiters known to have a close-by planetary companion. Furthermore, the companion to TOI-2535b is likely a second short-period giant planet. This differs from all but one of the currently known hot Jupiters with a nearby companion, in which the companions are sub-Neptunes or super-Earths," the authors write.

A pair of massive gas giants orbiting one system in resonance with each other isn't something planet-formation models predict. The authors rule out high-eccentricity migration as a cause. "Such systems could not have formed through high-eccentricity migration, which has been posited to be responsible for most other hot Jupiters," the authors write. But there are two other migration possibilities.

In one, the planet is pulled into an eccentric orbit by a powerful interaction of some sort, possibly a near collision with another object, and then the star's gravity pulls it in, where it takes up residence as a hot Jupiter.

The second scenario sees the planet pulled into the inner solar system with other planets inside the protoplanetary disk. But that counterindicates the observed orbital tilts. "However, the protoplanetary disk would also quickly damp the inclinations of the two planets, requiring some later excitation to explain the nonzero inclination required by the TDVs," the authors write.

An illustration of a protoplanetary disk. It's possible that the planet was pulled into the inner system with other planets inside the protoplanetary disk. But disks tend to flatten orbital inclinations. So something else would've had to happen to explain the exoplanet inclinations. Image Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech/T. Pyle (SSC) - February, 2005

There are other possibilities, but they're outliers that "only operate in specific regions of the space of possible planetary orbital parameters," and only an accurate understanding of the system's overall architecture could confirm any of them. The researchers don't have that yet.

These are the types of solar system architectures that intrigue astronomers because they hint at new processes. Somehow, components of the system have interacted in ways that have produced a very unusual situation: a pair of massive planets close to their star. Not only that, but we can watch their interactions play out over only a few years, which is very rare.

"Our dynamical modeling reveals that the perturber is likely of Saturn-mass or greater and in a mean-motion resonance with TOI-2535b, making this an unusual system that contains two short-period giant planets," the authors write. "The TDVs measured for TOI-2535b represent one of the most extreme examples of planetâplanet interactions driving orbital evolution on human timescales."

If there were only a single close-in planet, a more ready explanation would offer itself. But because there's a pair, the researchers have to reach further for an explanation.

âBecause TOI-2535 b has a close neighbor, it probably migrated by slowly spiraling in instead of getting flung in,â Yee said. âWe may be seeing a formation channel that doesnât happen very often but is very interesting.â

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

I Got Shingles Training for a Marathon. I’m Not the Only One.

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Shingles typically appears as a band of blistering, painful rash on one side of the body. (Photo: Getty Images)

I had just finished the last hard training run of my marathon build, 19 miles with 15 at race pace. I nailed it, but couldn’t help thinking it felt harder than it should have.

“It’s just nerves,” I told myself. “You’ll have two weeks to taper, and you’ll be ready to run that BQ (Boston Marathon Qualifying time) on race day.”

The next day, I felt exhausted, and my ribs were sore and tender to the touch. I started to wonder if I’d pulled a muscle in my rib cage while breathing heavily during my training run. I, of course, turned to Google. “Ribs sore from running.” “Ribs hurt after running.” Most of the posts advised that I’d feel better in a day.

But I didn’t feel better the next day. Instead, I noticed a small pink mark on my stomach, and now my ribs felt both sore and tingly. My Google searches grew more alarming: I could have shingles.

I scheduled an appointment for that same day, and my doctor confirmed it. She took one look at me and said, “I’m so sorry, you have shingles.”

Shingles is a painful viral infection that looks like a terrible rash and usually involves nerve pain that can last for months. The virus comes from dormant chickenpox in your body that can reappear when you’re stressed.

My doctor asked about stressors in my life. “I haven’t really been that stressed,” I promised, saying my family life and my career were extremely smooth. “But I have been training for a marathon,” I said.

“That could do it,” she told me. “Your body is clearly telling you that you were pushing too hard.”

I’m 43, younger than the typical shingles patient, but I’m part of an unfortunate trend. Shingles cases are on the rise and the biggest increase is among people in their 30s and 40s. One study found overall shingles cases nearly doubled between 2005 and 2021. Another found cases in Americans under 50 quadrupled from the late 1940s to 2000, and are still going up, according to data from the CDC.

A big reason for the increase among millennials is that we fall into a gap: 50 and up, and you can get the shingles vaccine; 30 and under, and you’ve had the chickenpox vaccine, which will make getting shingles way less likely.

That age range is also most common among marathoners, so my big question was whether running, especially hard training, can give you shingles. Unfortunately, that specific question hasn’t been extensively studied, so there’s no clear-cut answer. But doctors are drawing correlations between heavy training and whether it decreases the body’s immunity overall.

There’s research that shows immunity dips after heavy training, says David Nieman, DrPH, FACSM, director of the Human Performance Laboratory at Appalachian State University. He pointed to a study involving mice and the herpes virus.

“When you infected the mice and then forced them to run to exhaustion, there was a higher mortality rate in those mice than mice that just did moderate activity and rested,” he says. “Moderate activity actually helped a little bit, but by far the really bad response was in the mice that ran to exhaustion.”

In another study, researchers gave horses the flu and then ran them really hard. Those horses developed a longer-lasting, more intense bout of the flu compared to the horses who were infected but didn’t run hard, he said.

But Nieman and other researchers agree that moderate exercise can actually boost your immunity. James Turner, a professor at the Sport, Exercise and Rehabilitation Sciences school at the University of Birmingham, says it’s far more common that inadequate sleep, diet and psychological stress suppress immunity than exercise, even really hard training.

“Research on both prolonged exercise and marathon running shows that antibody responses are not suppressed and might actually be higher,” he says.

Meanwhile, Dr. William Schaffner, MD, a professor of infectious diseases at Vanderbilt Health who’s an expert in shingles, says it’s too soon to say definitely that lots of stress leads to the virus. He points out there’s no evidence that soldiers in combat or police and firefighters have an increased risk of shingles.

“When people get a shingles episode, there is a tendency to look back in their lives to see if there might be some sort of inciting event or stressful event and there’s a tendency to link the two,” he says. “But the question is, is that just coincidence because there’s a lot of stress in life, or was it a causal relationship? I think we’ll still have to say that hasn’t been definitively settled.”

Instead, for Schaffner, the likeliest explanation for the increase in shingles is because the chickenpox vaccine is decreasing our exposure to chickenpox. We used to all get chickenpox as children, and so the virus was more common in the wild. That gave our immune systems a boost. Now that there’s a vaccine, that isn’t happening, which means we may be more likely to get shingles, he said.

Whether my running caused my shingles or not, as soon as I had my diagnosis, my next questions were about my marathon. My doctor said my symptoms would likely peak in four days to a week, and so it still seemed possible I would be ready for race day. I rushed out to get a seven-day course of Valtrex—if you start an antiviral treatment quickly enough, it can stop the spread of the shingles rash.

But as the week passed and the rash grew along my rib cage to my back, it only continued to feel worse. I was achy everywhere, and my ribs felt like they were constantly being stabbed with tiny needles. A week later, I called it and canceled my marathon plans, harboring a hope that within a few weeks I would be better and could rebound to run my BQ.

What I did next probably hurt my cause rather than helped it. As soon as I felt a bit better, I would go out and do a race-pace run, which would give me a painful setback that lasted for several days. This likely was making my bout with the virus longer, Nieman says.

He reminded me of the mice who were running to exhaustion and not getting well. The mice really needed to keep it low-key to get an immune boost, not drain.

“If they did 30 minutes of something like a brisk walk equivalent, that would help a little bit,” he says.

My conversation with him sounded like a negotiation.

“I’m mostly feeling better, but I still sometimes have sensations in my ribs, so do I still need to take it easy?” I asked.

“I have a lot of empathy. I know you don’t want to hear it,” he said gently. Nieman has run 58 marathons himself, so he knows the feeling. “You don’t want anybody telling you not to run, but this is where you’ve got to be really, really careful and just bide your time and slowly get back into it once you feel better.”

His advice to runners: listen to your body and don’t push yourself. That’s not an easy metric for marathoners who are used to pushing beyond what feels comfortable, but Nieman says exhaustion can exacerbate the nerve pain and make it all last much longer.

Other things to keep in mind: it’s possible that sunburns can impede your immune system, so cover up once you’re back out and running, says Jennifer Moffat, a SUNY microbiology and immunology professor and expert on chickenpox and shingles.

And, Moffat says, while you still have a rash, you’re contagious and can spread the virus to others through contact. If you’re at the gym or anywhere you might touch other people, you should have the rash fully covered.

She also advises keeping an eye on the other stresses around marathons, like not eating enough, not sleeping enough, and the mental stress around races.

“The anticipation, logistics, travel hassles, changes in environment and food, rivalries with other competitors, jet lag, time away from family, the cost can become overwhelming,” she says. “Getting to the starting line of a marathon is much more than showing up with your shoes tied.”

In fact, she says, it’s common for people to come down with other viral infections right before a race (the dreaded taper cold).

As for me, two months after my rash first appeared, I finally truly threw in the towel. I couldn’t keep up any kind of marathon training without making myself feel worse, so I switched to low-effort maintenance running and decided my BQ would have to come next year.

ARK Invest Trims Block and Bullish Positions as Bitcoin ETF Outflows Continue

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Disclosed ARK Invest trading activity points to sales of shares in crypto-linked companies Block and Bullish alongside changes elsewhere in its portfolios. A fund manager selling shares is not the same as investors redeeming money from a spot Bitcoin ETF, and the trades should not be treated as a categorical forecast for crypto prices.

Crypto markets generate several types of selling headlines, but they do not all describe the same thing. One involves investors moving money into or out of exchange-traded products. Another involves a fund manager changing which companies it owns inside a portfolio. ARK Invest’s latest trade disclosures belong to the second category.

ARK’s trade-notification service provides information about purchases and sales by its exchange-traded funds. Reporting of the November 9 trades indicates reductions in shares of Block and Bullish, both businesses tied to the wider digital-asset economy.

Trade data published for the session should be checked against the original ARK notification for exact fund allocations and amounts. Public summaries can combine activity across several ETFs, while dollar-value estimates change with the share price used for calculation.

A growth-oriented fund might sell shares to rebalance position weights, manage liquidity, fund purchases of other holdings or respond to company-specific developments. A share sale alone cannot reveal which of those motives dominated.

For that reason, it would be a mistake to describe ARK’s selling as proof that the manager has abandoned Bitcoin. The sale may reflect a view on the risk and valuation of individual equities rather than the future value of the underlying cryptocurrency.

Block’s exposure to digital assets is also different from Bullish’s. Their revenue drivers, regulation and business models are not interchangeable merely because both are routinely grouped under the label ‘crypto stock.’ Investors comparing them need to look beyond the common theme.

NewsBTC has recently tracked sizable outflows from US spot Bitcoin funds and earlier daily Bitcoin ETF withdrawals. Those figures measure net investor flows into or out of specific products, rather than an ETF manager switching between equity holdings.

Even within ETF reporting, transactions can be misunderstood. Selling shares in a listed company held by an actively managed ETF does not necessarily imply the ETF itself suffered a redemption. Likewise, a Bitcoin ETF net outflow does not automatically show why an individual investor chose to redeem.

Those distinctions become especially important in a market where the same financial firm may own Bitcoin directly, operate a fund holding mining or exchange equities, and manage entirely different technology-sector positions.

Shares of trading venues, payment companies and miners are often affected by crypto prices but can also move on earnings results, borrowing costs and capital spending. A mining company pursuing AI infrastructure may be valued on its power contracts as much as its Bitcoin output. A brokerage might add accounts while earning less from crypto transactions.

That mixture means crypto-equity portfolio changes sometimes convey a different message from spot-token trading activity. They are better read as part of ongoing active management than as standalone signals to buy or sell a coin.

The strongest verifiable facts are the securities traded, the direction of the trade and the number of shares reported for each fund. If later reporting establishes exact price-weighted values, those should be dated and described as estimates where appropriate.

ARK’s trading notices offer a window into portfolio decisions, not a complete explanation of motive. Their value for readers comes from knowing precisely what changed and avoiding the temptation to translate every share sale into a broad claim about the Bitcoin market.

Oura Ring users may get in-app access to real doctors soon – what we know

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Oura's acquisition of Counsel Health could signal a new dawn for digital care.

Oura has entered into an agreement to acquire AI primary care company Counsel Health for $625 million. The acquisition of Counsel Health, which was already a partner in the Oura app, will give users access to Counsel’s in-house physicians and 24/7 medical AI.

The Oura Ring has evolved from its beginnings as a sleep tracker to what its CEO, Tom Hale, calls “a doctor in your pocket.” The company has its sights set on making the device a continuous medical companion, and Counsel’s acquisition is one way it’s furthering that mission.

Oura employs its 24/7 health monitoring to recognize potential deviations from a user’s biometric baseline, and the partnership will build on those capabilities by adding more clinical guidance through Counsel Health.

The partnership will also connect members with Counsel Health’s medical AI and in-house physicians to address timely health problems, support, or “prescriptions for episodic health conditions,” the press release said. The acquisition offers specialty and chronic care for chronic conditions, like reproductive, aging, and cardiometabolic-related conditions.

“Oura tells members what’s happening in their bodies, and Counsel helps them act on it. This combination expands our addressable market and deepens the value we deliver to every member,” David Shuman, executive chairman of Oura, said in a press release.

This is the third acquisition Oura has made this year, following its acquisition of Galen AI, a conversational and personal health platform that connects smart ring data to medical records, and Doublepoint AI, an AI‑driven, biometric and gesture recognition company that could add voice or gesture controls to Oura’s next smart ring.

Also: Before you buy a smart ring, consider what you’re giving up

The Counsel Health deal is expected to close by the end of the year. It’s unknown whether access to Counsel Health physicians or the 24/7 medical AI will be included in an Oura app subscription.

Tech companies have a growing appetite for health tech startups. Last year, Samsung announced a similar acquisition of Xealth, a care platform that connects more than 500 hospitals with 70 digital health centers. The acquisition, according to Samsung VP of digital health Praveen Raja, furthers Samsung’s mission to connect the healthcare landscape, from symptom detection to care provider connection.

One of the Oura Ring’s superpowers is its ability to detect developing illnesses or deviations from my biometric baseline (a spiked resting heart rate, a higher body temperature) before I feel those symptoms. I’ve written about how my Oura Ring detected an illness earlier this year, one day before I began to feel sick.

Also: I should’ve listened to my Oura Ring when it warned me about my health

When Oura has flagged these concerns to me, I take them seriously, even as my care provider — to whom I show this data — may shrug them off. The Counsel Health acquisition enables medical AI and in-house physicians to contextualize your biometrics when you are sick or managing a chronic condition.

Will this acquisition help ease the demand for urgent care clinics? As a regular visitor to and hater of my Brooklyn CityMD, I hope so. It is notoriously difficult to get a same-day, in-person appointment with a primary care provider in New York because of high demand and a stretched-thin healthcare system, which often leaves urgent care as the only in-person option for patients.

The press release says that users could ask physicians for prescriptions. I’m thinking that if I get another bad cold this winter, I could go to my Oura app for help and symptom management instead of CityMD. Making a physician readily available to Oura users could, in theory, reduce dependence on primary or urgent care providers.
