Leonard Clark: Explorer, Spy, Liar

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Born in 1907, Leonard Francis Clark was an explorer, war hero, and writer who made daring expeditions all over the world before dying mysteriously in Venezuela in 1957.

Today, he isn’t well known, but has this lack of scrutiny been a blessing in disguise for this forgotten explorer’s legacy? His books, filled with adventure and excitement, read like fiction. The more one digs into the forgotten reports and old newspaper articles, the stranger his story, and the more complicated the man.

A Tibetan expedition under Leonard Clark in 1949. Photo: The Marching Wind

The main sources on Leonard Clark are all, well, Leonard Clark. This isn’t necessarily damning, but it is something he shares with most good con artists or cult leaders. The following is a short biography based only on official records, such as draft cards and census data. The first rule of reading historical accounts is that every historical figure is a liar.

Leonard Francis Clark was born in British Columbia, Canada, in January 1907 to two Americans, Maud and Francis Clark. In 1909, they moved back to Maud’s birth state of California, and Francis promptly died.

There are conflicting statements online about which university he attended, but census data shows him living only a few kilometers from the University of California (now the University of California, Berkeley) in 1930, so I’d put my money on that one. He began traveling right out of college, spending three years in Borneo on a hunting trip with his brother, Laurence. By the late 1930s, he was on a National Geographic-funded expedition to the South China Sea.

His article, cryptically titled Among the Big Knot Lois of Hainan, was published in the 1938 September edition. In it, he recounts how he and a small expeditionary force landed in the midst of a deadly cholera epidemic. Relying on hired porters to carry their supplies, Clark and Nicol Smith, another future travel writer, set off into the interior. Soon, malaria and infection laid low most of the team, including, per Clark’s article, Smith.

Again, according to Clark, Smith urged him to take the remaining five porters, the minimum of equipment, and keep going.

Much of the article is dedicated to ethnographic observations, with the typical exoticism and patronizing tone of the era. At one point, Clark noted that the Li people of Hainan reminded him of indigenous Amazonian people and “the men were tall, many six feet.”

A recent study showed that the average height for men in Hainan today is 168.7cm, around 5’6″, and the same study found that average heights had increased over the past century. This is a small nitpick, but he’ll repeat this unlikely claim again in the Amazon. It is worth noting that the “six-foot-tall Indian warrior” was a stock character in contemporary pulp adventure fiction.

There is an interesting contrast between Clark’s account and some of the newspaper reports, especially one from a Virginia paper, which relied on Smith for the story. This article implies Smith did much more than lay around his tent with malaria. Clark’s article claims he was the first white man to enter 47 different villages, while the newspaper says that Smith was the first Westerner to penetrate inland.

There is a third source: the 37-minute silent film compiled from the expedition’s footage and photographs. In it, we briefly see a white man lying under a mosquito net as if ill. But whether this is Smith, Clark — who admits himself to experiencing malaria symptoms on the expedition — or the third westerner on the expedition, one Jack Munroe, the film doesn’t say.

His previous experience in the region led him to return a few years later, this time on behalf of the U.S. Office of Strategic Services (OSS), a precursor to the CIA. His work during the war centered around the China-Burma-India corridor, particularly the Yellow River valley. He was involved in organizing guerrilla activities, building intelligence networks, and navigating the Japanese surrender of Taiwan.

In a later press statement, Clark explained how he had worked with General Dai Li, and that “the Reds had it in for him,” due to the infamously repressive actions Dai Li took against Chinese Communists. Dai Li, whom historian Barbara Tuchman called “China’s combination of Himmler and J. Edgar Hoover,” was killed in a 1946 plane crash. Wide speculation places the blame for this crash on either the Chinese Communist Party or the OSS. This would make things awkward for Clark a few years later.

He continued his government work in the area after the Second World War, sliding right into the developing Cold War. In 1949, Clark led a large, well-funded expedition into Tibet.

It would have gotten him in the history books if Leonard Clark identified a peak higher than Everest. Unfortunately for him, he didn’t. But he certainly proposed that he had, when he claimed that he’d surveyed Amne Machin and found it to be 9,040m high.

Amne Machin is not taller than Everest. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

Clark published his account of the expedition years later, titled The Marching Wind. In it, he wrote with his typical style: daring escapes, stereotypically aggressive natives, and breezy adventure writing. What it did not contain was the data to support his claims.

As soon as he released his first report and measurements, geographers, geologists, and mountaineers pored over them. Bradford Washburn, a cartographer, photographer, and explorer, published a thorough examination of the surveying techniques and questionable results from Clark’s expedition.

A contemporary review in the Alpine Journal, commenting on his photographic evidence, wrote that “anything more unconvincing could hardly be imagined — yet, ostensibly…the mountain was the object of the expedition.” More on that “ostensibly” later. Suffice it to say, Clark’s estimate that Amne Machin could be almost 1,000m taller than Everest was not adopted by geographers at large.

The review concluded that “it can hardly be wondered if mountaineers, whom Mr. Clark rather despises, cannot accept his results.”

Clark blithely remarked that he had “some rather bulky complaints by mountain and other specialists,” but admitted subpar instruments made his results “indecisive.”

A surveying party including Clark, far right, taking observations of Amne Machin. Photo: The Marching Wind

If he doesn’t seem too broken up, it may be because Amne Machin’s height wasn’t the main purpose of his trip. Clark was supported logistically and went on behalf of Ma Bufang, the fiercely anti-communist warlord ruling Qinghai, on the border of Tibet. Anticipating that he could not long hold out against the People’s Liberation Army, Clark proposed that his expedition would map a path of retreat. This would help Ma Bufang’s army cross into Tibet and continue their anti-Communist campaign from the mountains.

Clark envisioned Ma Bufang’s Qinghai, through Tibet, becoming “the bulwark against Red invasion of India.” Clark went on the expedition and did his mapping, but the PLA took Qinghai only months later, and Ma Bufang fled to Hong Kong.

In his introduction to his book, Clark claims that the United States was unaware of his “very slight aid” to Ma Bufang and local anti-Communist guerrilla activities, and he involved himself with the Chinese Nationalist army “as a private American citizen.” If I were to say that I was skeptical of this claim, I would not be the first to do so. But that falls under speculation, so I will say only that I sent a Freedom of Information Act request to the CIA regarding Clark’s work in Tibet. However, the records were not forthcoming.

After returning from the mountains, Clark went to a wedding anniversary celebration in Canton, surrounded by his fellow Allied intelligence members and their set. Things got a little out of hand, and Clark soon found himself in a Cantonese jail with a bullet wound and two manslaughter charges.

Chinese Nationalist soldiers on Clark’s Tibetan expedition. Photo: The Marching Wind

The other two men involved were Harold Harris, an Englishman who died immediately, and Willard Freeman from Massachusetts, who died later from his injuries. Clark also sustained a gunshot wound, but had recovered sufficiently by late November to be moved from hospital to prison.

The three had been drinking together in Freeman’s home when, at about 4 am, servants reported hearing gunshots. Mrs. Maya Freeman entered the room at 10 am to find an odd and grisly scene. Harris, fully clothed, lay dead on Freeman’s bed with three gunshot wounds. Freeman, completely nude, was also lying on the bed, alive with two shots to the abdomen. Clark, in his underwear, was on a second bed, with a single gunshot to the chest. Furniture was overturned, indicating a fight, and all three were covered in scratches. The only weapon found was Clark’s .32 caliber pistol.

Later that month, the Canton District Court denied Clark’s bail request. The court, still trying to determine what exactly happened, focused on the widowed Maya Freeman. Interviewing her for hours, they attempted to make her confess to an affair with Clark. Both of them repeatedly denied it. Clark further denied that he had fired any of the six shots, though he didn’t name the shooter.

Clark’s brother contacted former OSS head General William Donovan, who rallied the State Department. Clark reportedly was concerned that if he wasn’t released before the city fell to the Communists, he would be tortured for information regarding Dai Li and OSS activities. The State Department investigated and, thanks to its intervention, Clark was released.

Clark’s legal troubles were splashed across newspapers worldwide. Photo: The Journal (Caldwell, Ohio) November 24, 1949

What exactly happened in Freeman’s house that night? Was the dispute between the Allied Intelligence men personal, or professional? If the world were ever to find out, it wouldn’t be from Clark. Once freed, Clark returned to California and moved on to his next adventure. It was an ambitious one: Leonard Clark was going to discover the lost city of El Dorado.

He had an old parchment map to the lost city, he claimed, which showed it lay in the upper reaches of Peru. Never mind that it had been pretty well established for over a century that El Dorado was a myth, following the efforts of explorers Alexander von Humboldt and Robert Schomburgk.

Clark’s resulting account, The Rivers Ran East, contains all the genre tropes you would expect from a fictional work in the same setting. Native Amazonians are ferocious cannibals who capture Clark and keep him in a poison snake pit, crocodiles can and must be lassoed (there are no crocodiles in Peru, only caimans, a type of alligator), and there is a beautiful lady explorer, Inez Pokorny, who is secretly part of a London-based international El Dorado-seeking organization. If this organization existed, I have found no trace of it outside of this book.

If I were to list every blatant impossibility in his account, we would be here for days, so I’ll just continue on one particular thread: Leonard Clark seems to have a very loose grasp of which animals are in Peru and how big they are. At one point, he is blinded by a venom-spitting snake. There are three venom-spitting snake species, and none of them are in South America.

His 500-pound (227kg) boars must be peccaries, pig-like animals native to the Americas. The Amazon native white-lipped peccary tops out around 40kg, while the unconfirmed giant peccary still has only been estimated at 50kg. Likewise, his 12m-long pythons must be anacondas, which have a maximum recorded length of eight meters.

Clark claims to have found El Dorado. What he found was a group of Chicham people panning for gold, who traded some of it to Clark and Pokorny. According to his book, Clark traveled under the pretense of investigating botanical medicines, while actually searching for El Dorado.

In fact, as the El Dorado adventures on the page grew more and more ridiculous, I began to wonder if it were not the other way around. Here I have fully entered the realm of speculation. American corporations were, and still are, extremely interested in extracting resources from South America, including pharmaceuticals. In fact, Clark also reported success in this area, claiming to have identified over 60 native medicinals with commercial possibility — as well as Amazonian oil.

In praise of Clark’s work, Baron Wolfenegg, Director of Colonization for the Orient in Peru, accidentally said the quiet part out loud, stating he’d “learned much from this book that will help my work of colonizing this vast jungle.”

On Peru’s Perené River. Photo: The Rivers Ran East

It is practically gauche for explorers to die in their beds, and Clark, who seemed to mold his life to the shape of adventure stories, was no exception. Clark was in Venezuela in 1957, looking, he claimed, for diamond mines. He entered the Amazon basin, never to emerge again.

One history of the OSS offhandedly mentions that Clark died with “two native arrows in his back.” I suspect the explorer’s penchant for sensationalism survived his death. Other contemporary reports describe Clark as having drowned when his canoe overturned.

As I pointed out earlier, most of the information on Clark’s expeditions came from Clark himself. Since he never returned, startlingly little information is available about his death at age 49.

The area around the Orinoco River in Venezuela is, in fact, rich in diamond deposits. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

The line between fact and fiction is notoriously vague in much of the adventure travel genre. Is Herman Melville telling the full truth about his time spent on the island of Nuku Hiva? Did anyone actually complete the “Long Walk” out of a Soviet detention camp, which Slavomir Rawicz falsely claimed as his own tale? How much artistic license and embellishment is acceptable before exploration accounts become outright hoaxes?

I didn’t write this article with the goal of proving Leonard Clark a charlatan. It can hardly be denied that he really was going to these places, at the very least. But there are enough discrepancies and inaccuracies in his accounts to conclude that they should be taken with a grain of salt, and his treasure-hunting pseudo-archaeological tendencies make his anthropological findings, well, questionable.

As a contemporary New Yorker book critic put it, Clark had “the same gift, or weakness, for grandiose verbiage that got Marco Polo in trouble with his fellow Venetians.”

The real story, and one which may never be fully disentangled, lies in why, exactly, Clark was doing his exploring. Somewhere between the romantic adventurer and the cynically colonialist spy using exploration as a guise for the extractive, violent enforcement of Western capitalist hegemony, lies the truth.

“Every explorer has two faces,” Clark wrote in the opening to The Rivers Ran East; “the secret one and the one he shows to the world.”

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

Paws and Effect: The Reasons Behind a Cat’s Sudden Aggression

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

We all know that feline behavior can be somewhat unpredictable. Just when you think youâve got it all figured out, a cat is already three steps ahead of you. But thereâs a big difference between normal feline quirks and uncharacteristic aggression.

A cat that has always been gentle and suddenly starts lashing out may be reacting to something in its environment, changing hormones, pain, or a health problem. Aggression has many causes, from fear and frustration to competition with other cats. Understanding what has changed can help identify the cause and determine the best way to respond.

Unneutered male cats may fight over access to females, particularly when several cats are competing for mates in the same area. When a female cat is in heat, she may attract multiple males, making fights more likely.

Female cats can also behave differently during heat cycles. Many become unusually affectionate and demanding of attention, while others may become irritable or aggressive. Hormonal changes can also affect how they interact with other cats, particularly other females.

Spaying/neutering is an important aspect of taking care of a cat. Not only does it help to minimize aggression and friction between cats, but it also prevents unwanted litters and helps reduce the risk of certain diseases.

Pain can make even a normally friendly cat lash out. A cat may react aggressively if someone touches a sore area or approaches when it is feeling unwell.

Urinary tract disease can make a cat pretty miserable, and males in particular are vulnerable to urinary blockages, which are extremely painful and can become life-threatening. A cat that is straining to urinate or unable to pass urine needs emergency veterinary attention.

Infections, injuries, arthritis, and cancer are often painful but difficult to recognize from the outside. If aggression appears suddenly or seems linked to discomfort, a veterinary examination is an important first step.

A frightened cat may respond aggressively when startled, even if it is usually relaxed and friendly. A sudden noise, an unfamiliar person, or another threatening experience can trigger a defensive reaction.

Ongoing stress can also contribute to aggression. Cats that feel unsafe in their surroundings may remain on edge and react defensively to people or animals around them.

Every cat should have access to a quiet, comfortable place where it can retreat without being disturbed. Giving a frightened cat space can help it feel more secure.

Not every aggressive-looking interaction is driven by anger or fear. Play helps cats practise their hunting skills, and some become too rough when playing with people or other animals.

However, rough play should not be encouraged because biting and scratching can cause injuries. Rewarding gentle play and redirecting a cat toward appropriate toys can help it learn more acceptable ways to use its energy.

Cats can be protective of their territory, so the arrival of another animal may trigger aggression. The newcomer could be a new cat or dog in the household, or an outdoor cat that has started visiting the yard.

Sometimes, owners may not even realize another cat is nearby. Seeing or smelling an unfamiliar animal can leave a cat agitated, causing it to react to other pets or people.

Aggressive behavior plus inappropriate urination and scent-marking are not uncommon when a cat feels its territory is under threat.

A cat that becomes frustrated by something it cannot reach may take that frustration out on another target. This is known as redirected aggression.

For example, a cat might become agitated after seeing another cat outside, then lash out at a person or pet nearby. The target may have done nothing wrong and simply been in the wrong place at the wrong time.

Giving an agitated cat time and space to calm down is safer than trying to handle it immediately.

Cats living together usually have established social relationships, and introducing a new pet tends to upset the status quo. A certain amount of fighting and posturing is to be expected as the cats adjust to the change, but escalating aggression or bullying of one cat should be addressed.

Introducing a new animal gradually can help reduce conflict. Cats should have time to become familiar with each other without being forced into close contact before they are ready.

Some cats seem to enjoy attention but suddenly bite or scratch once petting begins. A cat may become overstimulated by touch, or it may simply want to be near a person without being handled.

Watch for signs of discomfort, such as flattened ears or a flicking tail. If a cat seems agitated, stop petting and give it space. Treats can reward calm interactions when the cat is comfortable.

Aggression can be difficult for a household to manage, but many causes can be addressed with patience and the right approach. Rewarding calm behavior, avoiding known triggers, and providing a safe place to retreat can all help.

The first step is to work out why the cat is behaving differently. If the aggression is sudden, it may be linked to pain or illness, so you should always consult a veterinarian before treating it as a behavioral issue. Once medical causes are excluded, look for changes â both indoors and outside the home â that might be affecting your cat.

Simple steps like providing extra hiding places, calming aids, and adapting handling techniques can go a long way to reducing feline aggression.

Are Dogs the Secret to a Happier, Healthier Childhood?

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

We just sent a welcome email your way with a quick overview of what to expect (including those vital recall alerts).

Dogster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn a commission at no cost to you. Read about privacy.

Dogs have been a fixture of family life for thousands of years, and many people who grew up with one say they canât picture their childhood without them. Beyond the obvious benefits of companionship and fun, growing up with a dog has been shown to have positive effects on physical and emotional well-being; advantages that show up in how children handle friendships, feelings, and everyday challenges.

Of course, this doesnât mean that having a dog is right for everyone. Bringing a dog into your home is a big decision that affects budgets and daily routines for years to come, and the best outcomes tend to come from families who go in with open eyes.

For anyone considering life with a dog, here are a few ways they can improve a childâs life, and things to consider before adopting one.

Growing up around animals can lower the chance of developing allergies later on. Dogs, and all the things that come with living with them, expose children to a range of allergens, which helps build a more robust immune system. The key seems to be early exposure, which means the sooner a dog becomes part of a childâs world, the better.

Children who are close to their dogs tend to be more securely attached to their parents, and theyâre more likely to form strong friendships, too. Strong relationships in one part of a childâs life seem to go hand in hand with strong relationships in others. Kids who share more physical contact with their dogs, from cuddles to roughhousing, tend to get along better with their moms, dads, and friends.

Kids who grow up with a family dog tend to have lower rates of anxiety. The benefits reach well beyond childhood, too. Dogs can ease feelings of depression and anxiety in adults who went through childhood trauma, and they can reduce signs of post-traumatic stress, which is part of the reason theyâre so often brought into therapy.

A dog is a living, breathing creature with needs, and children who help care for one learn responsibility and dependability. A five-year-old canât take on every chore, but age-appropriate tasks are a great place to start. Young children spend much of their lives at the mercy of parents and teachers, so having a dog to look after can give them a real sense of empowerment and build self-esteem.

Caring for an animal from an early age also builds empathy, which supports emotional intelligence and helps a child grow into a more compassionate person. The lessons go on from there. Dogs model loyalty; training and bonding teach patience, and learning to read a dogâs body language and respect their boundaries teaches respect. When a family pet eventually passes away, children also learn about death and grieving.

Children with pets tend to have better self-esteem than children without them. The effect appears to be strongest as kids approach adolescence; an age when the emotional support of a companion animal may matter most.

Families with dogs tend to be more physically active than their peers, getting about 11 extra minutes of exercise a day. That may not sound like much, but it adds up to roughly 5.5 hours a month in a society that is growing more sedentary all the time.

For some children, the effect is far larger. Girls in particular gained around 52 minutes of light physical activity a day after a dog joined the family. The flip side was striking too, since girls who lost their pet saw their daily activity drop by 62 minutes.

Children tend to handle stressful moments better with a dog nearby. They feel happier and more at ease, and being able to touch the dog calms them even further. Simply having a furry companion close by seems to help kids feel steadier inside.

For all the upsides, adopting a dog isnât a decision to take lightly, and there are a few things every family needs to consider before racing out to get a four-legged friend.

Budget: Dogs depend on their owners for food, veterinary care, and emergencies, and a household budget that canât cover those costs is a reason to rethink the plan.

Age: Although many childhood benefits increase the earlier they start living with a dog, sometimes waiting until children are older is a good idea. St. Bernards make fantastic family pets, for example, but they can weigh as much as 180 pounds and can hurt a child accidentally. Small breeds have their own risks, since they can be fragile and prone to injury until kids learn boundaries and how to respect animals.

Lifestyle: A family that works long hours and spends every evening shuttling children to activities needs a plan for what the dog will be doing. Not every schedule has room for a pet, and that is perfectly fine, but a dog shouldnât be adopted without free time to spend with it.

Motivation: Children often beg for a dog, promising to walk, feed, and pick up after them.

Then, the novelty fades, and the adults end up with the bulk of the care. Although itâs a good idea to encourage children to take on some of the responsibility of looking after a dog, you should assume that youâll be the one doing most of the work â hope for the best, plan for the worst!

Growing up alongside a dog can bring real benefits, from fewer allergies and lower anxiety to stronger relationships and more time spent moving. But to enjoy the benefits of living with a dog, families need to have the time, money, and genuine desire to take care of one.

Growing up with a dog might not guarantee health and happiness, but itâs a pretty good place to start!

Pixel users are getting a major Android Auto update – and it rivals Siri AI

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

More phones are getting Gemini Intelligence, but only Pixel users can access it in a new way.

Gemini Intelligence is spreading beyond the Google Pixel 11 lineup to the Pixel 10, the upcoming Motorola Signature 27, and several of Samsung’s Galaxy foldables and flagship phones.

Though Google often offers its most upgraded features to other Android phone manufacturers, there are some features that are Pixel-exclusive or come to Pixel users first. This time, Gemini Intelligence is coming to Android Auto, but only for eligible Pixel users.

Also: I’m an Android Auto ride or die – but Apple CarPlay beats it on these 3 features

Google’s blog post states that Pixel users with Gemini Intelligence can expect it to gather information from their apps and surface it to their car’s infotainment screen. Google’s example is that if a friend texts you asking where you’re meeting for Pilates, Gemini Intelligence can find the correct confirmation email and offer to send it with one tap.

To access Gemini Intelligence in Android Auto, the Gemini app and subsequent setup are required; it’s available in select countries to users aged 18 and older, and feature availability will vary.

With more context about your messages, emails, and calendar events, a more capable assistant can complete cross-app tasks while driving, such as “remind me to buy mom a birthday card before her birthday” or “which route to my dentist appointment will be the fastest?” I often use Siri AI in CarPlay for tasks like these, and Gemini operates the same way. However, these tasks require user input first; what makes an AI assistant in in-car software great is when it suggests tasks before you ask for them.

Siri suggests to me when I should leave my house for an upcoming event in my calendar, such as a dinner reservation, flight, or doctor’s appointment, based on traffic conditions. It looks like Gemini Intelligence in Android Auto will offer the same assistance. I’ve reached out to Google to confirm, and will update this article if I get a response.

Also: 4 wireless Android Auto adapters we recommend (and they’re cheap)

I find Siri’s upgraded capabilities most useful when I have a busy day and heavy Atlanta traffic is thrown in the mix. Sometimes people need information about where to meet up, but I don’t remember the exact name of the location or which street it’s on. It’s difficult to search through my old messages or emails while staying alert to crazed drivers on the interstate, so a smarter AI assistant comes in handy.

Though Gemini Intelligence in Android Auto is reserved for Pixel users, the upgrade follows a series of improvements Google has rolled out to its in-car software. Non-Pixel Android phones and Android phones without Gemini Intelligence can use Personal Intelligence, which connects Gemini across your Google Maps, Photos, or Gmail data.

Personal Intelligence users can ask Gemini to navigate to a restaurant with a specific cuisine or vibe, or search their photos to find a car wash they photographed last week. Gemini Intelligence is more proactive and expansive, drawing on more sensitive data from your phone, such as messages, while Personal Intelligence relies solely on data from your Google services.

Arca Opens to Everyone, Paying People for the AI Sessions They’d Otherwise Throw Away

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

After invite-only early access, the desktop app from the team behind Brevis is now open to anyone on Mac and Windows.

HONG KONG, November 11, 2026: Arca, a new desktop app from the team behind Brevis, opened to the public today. Arca pays people for the AI sessions they’d otherwise throw away. It finds the session history their AI tools already keep on their computer, lets them choose which sessions to submit, strips sensitive details on the device before anything is uploaded, and pays for the sessions that are approved. Starting today, anyone can sign up with an email address, with no invite and no waitlist.

Much of today’s knowledge work runs through AI tools. People steer a model through a task, correcting it, throwing out what fails and keeping what holds up. Every one of those sessions leaves a detailed record of real work: the back-and-forth, the dead ends, the fix that finally worked. These records pile up by the thousands on people’s computers, and once the window closes, they’re worth nothing to the person who did the work. Arca gives that work a value.

Arca works with the AI tools people already use, including Claude Code, Codex, Cursor and Claude Desktop. After installing the app, users see the sessions Arca has found on their machine, past ones included. They pick a session to upload, and Arca anonymises it on their computer before it leaves, then returns a quote range. Users either confirm, which sends the session to review, or withdraw, in which case the upload is deleted. Approved sessions are paid weekly.

Arca only reads sessions that are already on the user’s computer, and it lists everything it finds. Nothing uploads in the background, and nothing leaves the device until the user picks a session and confirms. Before a session is uploaded, Arca strips names, file paths, account details and keys, and further anonymising runs on Arca’s side.

“Every AI session is a record of real thinking and real work, and until now the person who did that work got nothing for it,” said Michael, co-founder and CEO of Brevis. “Arca lets people see what their sessions are worth and decide for themselves what to share. We opened it in small batches first to get the experience right. Today it’s open to everyone.”

Arca is built for anyone who does real work with AI, from code to contract reviews, research and long working conversations. Sessions with Claude Opus and Fable, and with the latest GPT models in Codex, qualify today, with more to come. Very short sessions don’t qualify, and every submission is reviewed before it pays. Users can connect GitHub to strengthen their profile, which can raise the value of their sessions, but it isn’t required.

Arca carries the principle behind Brevis’s verifiable computing work into a new domain: things you can verify are worth more than things you have to trust.

Arca first opened on October 13 in invite-only early access, admitting users in small batches. With today’s launch, the invite requirement is gone. Users sign up with an email address or a Google account and can start right away. Arca is free to use and available now for Mac and Windows at arca.market.

Through November 30, the Arca Launch Challenge adds $10,000 in prizes on top of regular payouts for approved sessions.

Arca pays people for the AI sessions they’d otherwise throw away. The desktop app finds the sessions people’s AI tools already keep, lets them choose what to share, anonymises sessions on the device before upload, and pays for approved sessions. Arca is built by the team behind Brevis. Learn more at arca.market.

Brevis builds verifiable computing infrastructure. Its zero-knowledge proving technology, including the open-source Pico zkVM, lets applications prove the results of complex computations. Learn more at brevis.network.

My Favorite Spot in Denali National Park Is a Literal Pile of Trash

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Old cans abound at one historic dump in Denali National Park. (Photo: Ryleigh Norgrove)

One of my favorite spots in Denali National Park’s vast wilderness isn’t some grand vista or rocky overlook. It’s much trashier than that.

Wander enough of the park’s frontcountry woods and you’ll come across all sorts of things: pioneer graves, little slate caves, mossy goshawk perches, a fantastic blueberry patch. Keep walking, and you might find a drainage as wide as a semi, full of garbage.

There are beer cans and glass bottles, coffee cans and broken gravy boats. It looks like a house disgorged its contents on a hillside and the tundra is slowly gobbling them up

The first time I encountered it, I wondered why no good Samaritan had come along with a bag or several by now, to haul this away to some other, less federally protected pit. We’re supposed to pack it out, after all.

After asking around at the local dive, I learned something else: This trash was considered historic. It felt like an oxymoron to me: How could a bunch of nasty empty beer cans tell us anything about the past other than a bunch of old-timers here liked to get drunk together, or that they were serving alcohol during prohibition?

One answer is that people don’t tend to write down what they’ve had for lunch. Or what kind of socks they wore through, or what they dropped and shattered. Discarded objects don’t mean much once they’ve hit the bin. And that’s exactly why impromptu landfills like this are one of the most significant sources of data archaeologists have.

“A lot of the stories of these individuals in the past never got written down,” says Dr. Justin Cramb, an assistant professor of archaeology at the University of Alaska Fairbanks, told me. “So the material traces are what we have.”

Archaeologists actually have a word for this historic trash: middens. They’re nasty and they’re everywhere. Depending on their age, they could contain shells, animal bones, or the last of someone’s canned dinner. They’re treasure troves of information, teaching us how people lived. Enough of them, alongside the other things people discarded, might tell you something about making a home in a place like Denali where getting groceries was considerably more complicated than driving to the store.

It’s something Cramb knows well. Throughout his work, he looks for details of daily lives: what resources people had, how they built their camps, and where they might have gathered building materials. Garbage is a part of that evidence.

“At the time, the world was kind of considered to be too vast to worry about those kind of things,” Cramb said. “And often in cases, somebody’s living at a place like Denali, they’re not going to haul their trash out, right? There was no mechanism for that to happen.”

Still, surely not every empty beer can deserves to spend eternity in a national park. But what’s the birthday on which it graduates from trash to treasured artifact?

The answer I got from the National Park Service on this was less tidy than I expected: “Age alone does not determine whether an object warrants preservation.”

It comes down to preserving cultural resources as much as natural ones. There are a few laws involved: Under the National Historic Preservation Act, historic areas generally need to be at least 50 years old and meet certain criteria for historical significance and integrity. The Archaeological Resources Protection Act also covers archaeological resources more than 100 years old that have cultural significance. Neither amounts to a blanket instruction to save every old can, but they do offer about 50 years of leeway on either end to determine if something is worth saving.

Cramb put it this way: “The house I grew up in is over 50 years old, right? But it’s not a historic property.”

Apparently, getting old and falling apart isn’t enough. The issue lies in what an object can teach us. Was it associated with a significant person or event? Could it fill in a piece of history we don’t know much about? And is enough of that evidence still there to make sense of it?

Around nearby Riley Creek, there’s quite a bit of history to make sense of. Before this was a place to order a latte or catch a bus, it was a place people lived. On Thanksgiving Day, 1921, Maurice Morino opened his Mount McKinley Park Hotel to feed and entertain miners, trappers and travelers. Just down the road, there were cabins, a trading post, and a silver fox ranch. Down below the old railroad bridge, you could visit “the Hole” for gambling, bootlegging, and other activities you won’t find on the ranger-program schedule.

Later on, in 1932, the park boundary absorbed that community. Today, cabin remains, debris, and trash dumps are among the traces of McKinley Station. Morino’s grave is still here, too. I haven’t been able to trace who exactly left behind my little trash pile, but it’s tucked into a landscape with considerably more human history than the trees let on.

Of course, I’d left an imprint getting there, too. I’d followed one of those little paths through the woods and added my footsteps to the record. Even my wandering had inherited a shape.

There’s something difficult in that. I want to know these woods well enough to return to the blueberries when they’re ripe, to recognize the perch before I look up for the bird. That kind of knowing takes effort. But each one presses a little more of a path into the ground.

“I always say that the goal is really to leave as little trace as possible,” says Erin Collier, Director of Education for Leave No Trace. “That doesn’t sound as good, you know, on the T-shirt and on the logo, but that’s really what the goal is.”

“If we were truly leaving no trace,” she says, “we wouldn’t be out there.”

We leave evidence of ourselves just by visiting. Roads, campgrounds, the little paths we wear into the woods. The trick is in paying attention to what we’re adding, and what we might accidentally take away.

Sometimes that means resisting the urge to clean up. The Park Service advises leaving potentially historic objects exactly where they are and asking staff if you’re unsure. Where something lies, among the other things people left behind, is part of its story.

But that doesn’t give the rest of us permission to start contributing.

“Today we have the infrastructure to deal with things like taking our trash out of the park,” Cramb told me. “We can do better. So we should.”

I know these woods, and knowing them means accepting that other people have known them, too. Their lives are still here, in cabin foundations and coffee cans, slowly disappearing into the hillside. My own footprints will leave something behind. I’d like to be thoughtful about what that is.

This story grew out of Ryleigh’s Certified Interpretive Guide (CIG) coursework, taught by Suzanne Miller, Interpretive Programs Manager for the Doyon/Aramark Joint Venture at Denali National Park and Preserve.

Exodus Reports $8.3M Quarterly Loss as Wallet Platform Expands Payments Business

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Exodus reported third-quarter revenue of around $32.5 million, up 7%, while swinging to an $8.3 million net loss from a profit a year earlier. The wallet company’s expansion into payments and card infrastructure adds a new commercial opportunity but also increases the importance of integration costs and execution.

Exodus built its brand around software that helps people hold and exchange digital assets without relying on a traditional centralized exchange account. Its latest results show that running a wallet business at scale now involves more than downloads and token swaps.

In its November 9 third-quarter report, Exodus said revenue reached about $32.5 million, an increase of roughly 7% year over year. The company nevertheless recorded a net loss of approximately $8.3 million, compared with a net profit of about $17 million a year earlier.

The difference between higher revenue and weaker net income points to costs and accounting items that require as much attention as transaction volume.

Exodus reported around 1.4 million monthly active users and approximately 1.4 million quarterly funded users, reflecting the scale of its consumer product. Those are company-defined measures and may overlap; they should not be added together to imply a larger distinct customer base.

The company also cited roughly $1.1 billion in quarterly swap volume. Transaction volume measures value moved through a service; it is not the same as revenue or profit. The share retained by a platform depends on fees, execution arrangements and the costs of providing the service.

This is a useful distinction across the wallet industry. A product can support heavy activity without retaining a large percentage of the value exchanged. Increased usage can even create more customer-service, compliance and technical costs.

Exodus has been extending its role in the infrastructure around digital payments. Its recent company updates describe a combination involving Baanx and Monavate and a growing emphasis on card and payment services. It has also referenced around 1.6 million active cards and substantial payment volume in the broader business context.

Payment services offer a way to make digital assets useful for routine spending rather than only investment and exchange. But building or combining regulated payment operations brings operational work: issuing relationships, settlement partners, compliance programs, fraud controls and support obligations.

NewsBTC previously covered Visa’s work on stablecoin treasury and settlement infrastructure. Exodus operates in a different part of the market, yet both stories show businesses looking for ways to make digital value move through established financial channels.

Likewise, the growing attention paid to tokenized securities infrastructure reflects a wider focus on how blockchain assets connect with ordinary financial workflows. Neither development should be mistaken for proof that Exodus’s specific payment plans will succeed.

Management cited increased general and administrative spending among the pressures on the quarter. As a company moves from a comparatively focused software product toward a broader financial-services platform, staffing, oversight and integration costs can rise before new lines generate consistent returns.

That does not make the strategy inherently flawed. It means the outcome will depend on whether additional services create more durable recurring earnings than they cost to build and operate.

The useful measures in future results will include transaction and card activity, net revenue retained from those services, the cost of operating the expanded business and whether losses narrow. Users and investors should also pay attention to security, custody arrangements and regulatory disclosures as more payment services are introduced.

Exodus is showing modest revenue growth alongside a more expensive operating model. The next several quarters will help determine whether its payments ambitions create a stronger business or simply add complexity to an already competitive wallet market.

Claude can collaborate in your team meetings soon – like an intern

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Claude is responding 'Yes' to that calendar invite - with some limitations. Plus: Anthropic launches two more new features that are available now.

Anthropic often refers to its Claude agent as your coworker. Soon, the company said, it will be able to join your meetings like one, and start working on the tasks you and your team are discussing live.

Also: I locked down my OpenClaw agent with Claude Code – then dared it to escape

“Claude Meetings will let you add Claude to a call to take notes and update the documents your team is working on during the conversation,” Anthropic said. The feature, which will be available for Team and Enterprise plans, theoretically goes beyond something like Gemini’s Google Meet transcription and note-taking, which identifies action items but doesn’t proactively contribute to them.

In material shared with ZDNET prior to the launch, the company said Claude “won’t join a call with outside guests unless an admin allows it, and it leaves if one joins anyway.” The company also said Claude Meetings will be on by default for Team plans, but Enterprise admins will be able to choose who gets it. The feature won’t work for organizations with “zero data retention (ZDR), HIPAA-ready configuration or customer-managed encryption keys (CMEK).”

The company did not clarify when Meetings would roll out, but announced several other updates for companies that are available now.

To further keep as much of your work ecosystem within the Claude universe, Anthropic released a new Whiteboards feature in beta.

Also: Claude’s Record-a-Skill cut my research to minutes

“Now, you can talk through a messy plan out loud with Claude, and Claude can turn it into a board with a timeline with owners and dates, so you and your team can collaborate in real time,” Anthropic said. Claude will group crowdsourced notes into themes and action items. Maybe double-check the results of that spew before locking anything in.

After brain-dumping like you might on a physical whiteboard, Anthropic said, Claude can turn that output into a deck or document, or export it to other tools for next steps.

A new Sheets feature is exactly as it sounds: Claude can now create shareable spreadsheets from a prompt and edit inside cells with trackable changes. But it’s not fully replacing the old guard of spreadsheets just yet.

“When you need the full functionality of Excel, you can export your file to .xlsx or CSV, or use Claude for Excel,” Anthropic said. “You can also continue working in partner tools including Airtable, Asana, monday.com, and Smartsheet.”

Also: I tried replacing Claude Code with a free, local AI stack

Claude Dashboards, which only launched in beta last month, is now called Data. The tool can query company data from large external services such as Amazon Redshift and ClickHouse and comes with tracked changes and faster loading thanks to caching. According to Anthropic, it’s intended to let companies standardize metrics to avoid having conflicting data, surface data stories, and create a live source of truth everyone can access.

“With live sharing, your colleagues can see up-to-date numbers when they open a shared dashboard, even if they don’t have access to the connectors that keep that data current. Viewers see the results of the queries you’ve approved, and can’t run others with your access,” the company said.

Anthropic said that for Team plans, all three features are on by default. For Enterprise plans, only Claude Data will be on by default starting Nov. 19. Whiteboards and Sheets are off by default, though you can adjust those settings in Artifacts.

Anthropic has maintained a lead over OpenAI by focusing on enterprise clients first, while OpenAI has prioritized several consumer products. Releases like this are unsurprising, as they serve Anthropic’s primary base. Both Anthropic and OpenAI are increasingly branching into workplace and productivity software for large-scale companies. That said, Anthropic’s recent release of a personal finance tool rivals OpenAI’s existing equivalent geared toward consumers.

Also: Claude’s new Personal Finance tool aims to help analyze your spending

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

In September, at its Dev Day, OpenAI released ChatGPT Space, which houses document and slide creation tools reminiscent of Google Workspace. Before that, Anthropic had similarly consolidated its document and slide creation features into a single hub.

If there’s a slight differentiator here versus Google Workspace, it’s interface seamlessness. Anthropic has designed Claude itself as the starting point for a range of workplace deliverables, rather than maintaining distinct applications that can quickly feel siloed.

This Year’s NYC Marathon Proved Different Strategies Can Lead to Victory

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

I’m sure I could think of a couple of thousand ways, many of which include espresso, to spend a Sunday morning in New York City than running 26.2 miles through all five boroughs. But on Nov. 1, nearly 60,000 people decided to run the New York City Marathon—again—like they have every year since 1970. Some were chasing a championship. Some were chasing a personal best. Others probably just wanted to finish before their feet decided to go on strike. And there are hosts of runners dressed in all kinds of costumes just out for a jog. And the crowd keeps everyone well cheered on. No matter who it is.

The men’s winner was Andreas Almgren of Sweden, who covered the course in two hours, six minutes, one second. And I’m going to put an asterisk next to it because this was his first marathon. Not his first race, obviously. Almgren arrived as the reigning world half-marathon champion and European 10,000-meter champion. He’d spent years developing the engine. He just hadn’t taken it out for this particular 26.2-mile test drive.

The lead pack stayed together deep into the race, and Almgren waited until the final mile to make his decisive move. Italy’s Yemaneberhan Crippa finished second, thirteen seconds behind him, and Kamohelo Mofolo of Lesotho was third. Defending champion Benson Kipruto wound up fourth. That’s quite a debut, particularly on a course that includes bridges, hills, crazy potholes and enough turns to get you lost.

Before anybody decides the lesson is to skip marathon preparation, understand what a debut means here. Almgren wasn’t some guy who woke up last Thursday and discovered running shoes. He had world-class credentials at shorter distances. Moving up required applying an already extraordinary aerobic foundation to a longer event. A marathon is not merely two half-marathons stapled together. Fueling, pacing, muscular endurance, and how you respond after mile twenty all become rather important when your body starts negotiating terms of surrender.

The women’s race was even more entertaining near the finish. Kenya’s Sheila Chepkirui reclaimed the title she’d won in 2024, finishing in 2:24:36. Defending champion Hellen Obiri was in the fight late, but American Jess McClain came charging from behind and passed her in the closing meters. Chepkirui won, McClain took second in 2:24:42, and Obiri was third one second later. McClain became the first American woman on the New York podium since 2018.

And if you think the only interesting athletes are the ones with the smallest numbers on the clock, the wheelchair divisions cranks them out! Switzerland’s Marcel Hug won his eighth New York title in 1:28:14, edging American Daniel Romanchuk by one second. Catherine Debrunner, also Swiss, won the women’s wheelchair race in 1:40:02, nearly eight and a half minutes ahead of Tatyana McFadden. Same city, same event, completely different demands on the athlete. The upper-body power, equipment handling, and tactical judgment involved deserve more attention than they usually get.

Then there’s Mildred Baker, an 80-year-old Bronx resident who took up running in her late seventies and completed her first marathon in 7:03:49. Seven freakin’ hours! No, Almgren is not the only champion. That’s the point. The marathon can be a world championship-level knife fight at the front and a personal expedition at the back. And a trade in between. You can appreciate both extremes without pretending they’re the same athletic achievement.

Secondly, I must admit that l like the way this race exposes a certain kind of gym stupidity: the assumption that only one type of physical performance counts. A Swedish speed merchant conquered a distance he’d never raced. An American woman stole second place in the final strides. An 80-year-old finished something most people wouldn’t even start. You’re actually, in one Sunday event, looking at the entire definition of fitness.

Stanford AI May Have Discovered a Natural Ozempic Alternative for Weight Loss

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

With a monthly course of the synthetic weight loss drug, Ozempic costing upward of $1,000 per month without insurance, news that researchers at Stanford harnessed AI to locate a natural compound that could lead to new drugs is welcome news. But what exactly what is this molecule, and how did AI help to identify it?

The discovery of the organic compound was first unveiled as the results of a March 2025 study, which looked at peptide hormones and the effects on energy regulation. During the study, experts programmed AI to map peptide fragments and found that a 12-amino-acid molecule, labeled “BRP” acted in a distinct, but similar way to semaglutide, potentially offering an even more targeted approach to dropping fat.

“The receptors targeted by semaglutide are found in the brain but also in the gut, pancreas and other tissues,” explained Katrin Svensson, PhD, who is an assistant professor of pathology at Stanford, and was the lead author of the breakthrough study. “That’s why Ozempic has widespread effects, including slowing the movement of food through the digestive tract and lowering blood sugar levels. In contrast, BRP appears to act specifically in the hypothalamus, which controls appetite and metabolism.”

AI drastically accelerated the identification of BRP thanks to it’s superior processing power, because traditional methods of protein isolation face difficulties with distinguishing peptide hormones (which are relatively rare) from the more prevalent  natural byproducts of protein degradation and processing. The researchers had designed a new algorithm that inspected 20,000 protein-coding genes, narrowing the field down to 373 prohormones containing 2,683 peptides. The team then focused on peptide sequences that were likely to be bioactive in the brain, just like GLP-1 drugs, and screened 100 such peptides.

When the researchers put BRP to the test on lean mice and miniature pigs (which more closely mirror human eating and digestion than mice), they discovered that an intramuscular injection of BRP, prior to feeding, reduced food intake over the next hour by up to 50% in both animal models, meaning that with less energy consumed, fat levels would decrease with repeat treatments. Further studies of physiological and brain activity illustrated that BRP activates metabolic and neuronal pathways in these animals that are different from those activated by GLP-1 or semaglutide.

Following the study’s promising results, Svensson soon co-founded a company to launch clinical trials of the molecule. But before it can be released to humans, further work is now underway to investigating how to help BRP’s effects last longer, to allow a more convenient dosing schedule, should this peptide prove to be effective in regulating human body weight.

BRP’s results on humans is yet to be published, but while there is no firm date for its release confirmed as of now, the synergy between human scientists and AI could prove to be a gamechanger. While Ozempic is a synthetic medication, the discovery of more natural molecules like BRP could provide a more natural, and potentially cheaper approach.

“The lack of effective drugs to treat obesity in humans has been a problem for decades,” said Svensson. “Nothing we’ve tested before has compared to semaglutide’s ability to decrease appetite and body weight. We are very eager to learn if (BRP) is safe and effective in humans.”

Test your applications against AWS locally with AWS Emulator

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Today we’re announcing AWS Emulator, a local emulator for Amazon Web Services (AWS) that you run on your machine. The AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), the AWS SDKs, and your coding agents speak to it over a local HTTPS endpoint using the same AWS APIs they would use in the cloud, from your laptop. AWS Emulator has no additional service fee. You pay only for AWS resources when you deploy on AWS. Its source is on GitHub. You install it from an RPM or Debian package on Linux, and on macOS and Windows you run it in Docker.

How it works You install the emulator, start it, and point your tools at the local endpoint. When AWS Emulator starts, it generates its own credentials, a config file, and a certificate, and writes them to ~/.aws/emulator/9297/. You set environment variables so the AWS SDK talks to your laptop instead of AWS, and your application code stays unchanged.

From there, your existing tests, CLI scripts, and coding agents keep working the way they already do. A unit test that creates an Amazon DynamoDB table, writes an item, and reads it back is the same test, and it runs on your machine without a deployment. A coding agent can create resources, check the result, and iterate again without waiting on a deployment in a Region.

The emulator is implemented in Rust so that it stays light on CPU and memory on a laptop. It supports the APIs of AWS services that have an API model in the AWS API models repository on GitHub, and because it’s built from those models, the same ones the AWS SDKs use, your requests and the errors you get back have the same shape as on AWS.

If you are a developer, especially one working with coding agents such as Kiro or Claude Code, you can use that loop to test AWS interactions of real complexity locally.

Let’s try it out To get started, I installed the package on Amazon Linux 2023. The package adds the aws-emulator command to /usr/bin/, so it’s on the path.

On macOS and Windows, you build a Docker image from the short Dockerfile in the documentation and run the emulator in a container. We don’t publish a ready-made image.

I started the emulator with aws-emulator. It serves HTTPS on 127.0.0.1:9297, so only my own computer can reach it, and it writes its log as JSON lines that show the address, the config file, and the certificate file.

In another terminal, I set the environment variables that point the AWS CLI at the emulator, and unset any variables that could supply real AWS credentials.

The emulator generates new keys each time it starts. Its access key IDs start with EMULATOR, so you can’t mistake them for real AWS keys, and it rejects requests signed with any other keys. To confirm that my shell was talking to the emulator, I checked the health endpoint and the caller identity. The emulator uses a single account, 000000000000.

Then I created a bucket, copied in a small object, and listed it.

With the AWS CLI pointed at my laptop, I had the same commands I already use and a local endpoint that answered them.

Next I wanted to see it the way I actually work now, so with the emulator still running I opened Kiro and asked it to create an Amazon DynamoDB table, write an item, and read it back, all against AWS Emulator rather than my AWS account.

To use the same setup with an existing test suite, I would start the emulator, set the same environment variables in the script that runs the tests, and run them as usual.

Using the emulator in process Running the emulator as a local process gives you an HTTPS endpoint that works with the AWS CLI and the AWS SDKs through environment variables. Some SDKs need an extra setting to trust the emulator certificate, for example a trust store for the AWS SDK for Java. For tests, there is a second option. AWS Emulator ships an embedded mode that you instantiate directly inside your test process and wire into the SDK through its HTTP client, rather than talking to a separate server over the network. Each embedded instance keeps its own state, so tests do not share buckets, tables, or queues, and you can run many of them in parallel. Embedded mode is available for the AWS SDK for Rust. You add the aws-emulator-embedded crate from GitHub to the [dev-dependencies] section of your Cargo.toml file. The following example creates an Amazon DynamoDB table, writes an item, and reads it back, entirely in process.

When it runs as a local process, the emulator requires every request to be signed with Signature Version 4 (SigV4), the same way AWS does, and it exposes a /__admin/health endpoint on the same port so a script or a container can wait until it is ready before running tests. By default it also sends anonymous usage and error data, and you can turn it off.

How we measure fidelity An emulator is only useful if a test that passes against it would also pass against AWS, so we measure that directly with fidelity tests. A fidelity test is a short program that uses an AWS SDK to run a workflow, such as storing an object and then reading and deleting it, and asserts how AWS behaves. We verify each fidelity test against real AWS. A fidelity test passes only if the emulator matches AWS on the response to every call, including the status code, the body, and the exact error code and message, and on the resources and data that the workflow creates.

With each release, we publish how many fidelity tests pass for each service, as k of n. For example, 40 of 50 means that 40 of the service’s 50 fidelity tests pass. The set of fidelity tests is public in the aws-emulator-evaluation-suite repository on GitHub, including the ones that don’t pass yet, so you can see what we verified and run the fidelity tests yourself. Read a score as a snapshot of a product that’s improving. When we add fidelity tests, a score can go down even while the emulator gets better.

If the emulator doesn’t implement an operation yet, it returns HTTP 501 (Not Implemented), so your tests fail right away. If the emulator acts differently from AWS, you can tell us by opening an issue in the AWS Emulator repository on GitHub.

How the emulator is built AWS Emulator is built and maintained by agents from the AWS API models using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime Instances, AWS CodePipeline, and AWS CodeCommit, so it stays current with AWS as those models change.

One group of agents looks for gaps, both by comparing emulator responses against real AWS and by statically comparing the emulator’s code against the published Smithy models, and files an issue for each gap it finds. Another group picks up an issue, writes tests, implements the behavior, and reviews the change for correctness, performance, and security. When the change is ready, it is pushed to a source repository, and that push triggers an AWS CodePipeline run that runs the fidelity tests before the change is published to the public GitHub repository.

How we keep it secure Because the code is written by agents, an independent security agent reviews changes adversarially, both while the fix is being made and again in the release pipeline as a final check before anything ships.

Security of your own use remains a shared responsibility. We build the emulator to run safely on your machine: it listens only on your own computer by default and rejects requests that aren’t signed with its own keys. You decide where it runs, what data you put in it, and how you test before production. Don’t use the emulator to test whether your application is secure. It doesn’t evaluate AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies and resource-based policies the way AWS does, so a request that AWS denies might succeed in the emulator. Keep real credentials out of the environment of your test jobs, and test your security settings and your whole application on AWS before you go to production.

Now available AWS Emulator is generally available today. It has no additional service fee. You pay only for AWS resources when you deploy on AWS. You can install it from RPM or Debian packages on Linux, run it in Docker on macOS and Windows, or build from source, and it works with the AWS SDKs and the AWS CLI once you set environment variables. At launch, it has some limits. It doesn’t evaluate IAM policies the way AWS does, and each emulator acts as a single account, with no cross-account or cross-Region behavior. How closely it matches AWS varies by service and feature. To see how well the emulator covers the services that you use, check the fidelity scores in the AWS Emulator documentation.

If you want to check regional availability, visit the AWS Capabilities by Region page in AWS Builder Center. If you want to call APIs, search documentation, find regional availability, and check troubleshooting about this new feature, try using the AWS MCP Server and plugins with your preferred AI tool.

To get started, see the AWS Emulator documentation.

Daniel Abib is a senior specialist solutions architect at AWS, focused on generative AI and Amazon Bedrock — and passionate about serverless. He helps startups and enterprises build AI-powered applications and modernize with cloud-native architectures. A three-time Ironman finisher and four-time re:Invent speaker, he brings the same endurance mindset to building cloud solutions.

The Dynamo-Electric Principle: 160 Years of Powering Industry

📅 2026-11-11  |  Read Source →

How a breakthrough in electromechanical energy conversion helped shape the motors and generators that drive modern industry

Innomotics is a global leader in electric motors and large drive systems, operating from its headquarters in Nuremberg, Germany.

The dynamo-electric principle: one idea, 160 years, millions of motors.

This article is brought to you by Innomotics.

In the annals of industrial history, few innovations have demonstrated such remarkable staying power as the dynamo-electric principle. Discovered by Werner von Siemens in 1866, this groundbreaking principle remains fundamental to modern electrical engineering more than a century and a half later – and continues to shape the technology portfolio of Innomotics, a globally leading provider of electric motors and large drive systems. What began as a breakthrough in electromechanical energy conversion has evolved into a cornerstone of global industry – powering everything from brewery operations in Qingdao to high-voltage drive systems in petrochemical plants.

The year 1866 marked a decisive turning point, not only in the broader arc of industrial transformation but specifically in the realm of electric motion. At a time when Europe was entering a new phase of industrial transformation, Werner von Siemens pioneered the dynamo-electric principle – a breakthrough that enabled electric machines to generate their own magnetic field through self-excitation. This innovation transformed electric machines from laboratory curiosities into practical power sources, enabling the widespread electrification of industry and infrastructure that defined the late 19th century.

This electric motor from Innomotics was installed in the early 1900s at the historic Tsingtao Brewery in China and supported production processes for more than 90 years, operating continuously under industrial conditions.Innomotics

The principle itself is elegantly simple: an iron core generates a stable magnetic field; a rotating coil is placed within this field; movement in the magnetic field induces an electric current; part of this current strengthens the magnetic field; and electrical energy is continuously converted into motion. This self-reinforcing cycle made efficient and reliable electric motion possible – and it remains the foundation of modern motors today.

More than 160 years later, the same fundamental idea continues to power industrial applications around the world. Millions of motors operate reliably under demanding conditions, keeping production running across generations. The dynamo-electric principle made it possible for electric machines to convert electrical energy into motion (and motion back into electrical energy), creating the technological foundation for modern electric motors and drive systems.

Active Vibration Control System (AVCS) enhances stability and reliability by actively reducing vibrations.Innomotics

Perhaps no example illustrates this longevity better than the electric motor from Innomotics at the historic Tsingtao Brewery in China. Installed in the early 1900s during the early electrification of industrial brewing, this motor supported production processes for more than 90 years, operating continuously under industrial conditions. It became a powerful example of long-term reliability in real-world applications – a testament to how engineering ensures reliable motion across decades.

Continuity, however, does not mean stagnation. Sixteen decades after the original breakthrough, innovations continue to emerge that build upon this proven foundation. Two recent developments – the Active Vibration Control System (AVCS) and oil-lubricated rolling bearings (OLRB) – carry this spirit of innovation and ingenuity forward, a commitment that has been recognized with the German Innovation Award.

Innomotics’ patented Oil-Lubricated Rolling Bearing (OLRB) technology enhances reliability and reduces maintenance requirements.Innomotics

AVCS helps optimize high-voltage motor performance by actively decoupling motors from their foundations to reduce vibration issues. OLRB helps prevent rolling bearing failures from the start with continuous lubrication, supporting higher availability, less downtime, and long-term cost savings. These innovations demonstrate how the basic principle of 1866 can be enhanced through modern engineering to meet contemporary demands for reliability and efficiency.

For three decades, with over 25,000 units driving performance globally, Innomotics’ Perfect Harmony GH180 medium-voltage drive platform has supported reliable operation across industries. Its modular architecture enables efficient power control, high availability, and long-term performance. With thousands of installations worldwide, Perfect Harmony GH180 continues to build on a proven technological foundation, minimizing downtime and ensuring stable production.

Keeping critical motors running requires the right expertise and innovation spirit throughout the entire lifecycle. With Innomotics Customer Services, operators and plant managers gain the insights they need to maintain reliability and optimize maintenance strategies. Winding Lifetime Analysis (WLA) combines operating history, machine measurements, and physics-based modeling to assess the condition of the winding insulation and estimate its remaining useful lifetime. This enables informed maintenance decisions, reduces uncertainty, and helps minimize the risk of unplanned downtime.

The Perfect Harmony GH180 delivers efficient, reliable and flexible medium-voltage drive performance.Innomotics

What began as a breakthrough in 1866 still brings its value to modern industry. The dynamo-electric principle made efficient and reliable electric motion possible – and it remains the foundation of modern motors today.

As industries worldwide grapple with challenges ranging from energy efficiency to operational resilience, this 160-year-old principle continues to prove its relevance.

For plant managers and engineers seeking long-term performance in demanding applications, the message is clear: the foundation laid in the 19th century remains as solid as ever. What has changed are the tools and services built upon it – from vibration control systems to predictive analytics – all designed to extract maximum value from a principle that has, quite literally, powered the modern world for a century and a half.

Innomotics, carrying forward a legacy that spans the full arc of this history, continues to move industry forward with innovations that create real progress. The dynamo-electric principle may be 160 years old, but as the company’s portfolio demonstrates, it is far from obsolete. It is, in every sense of the word, still running.

Discover how Innomotics is driving the future of industry: https://www.innomotics.com/hub/en/company/about-innomotics/dynamo-electric-principle

Innomotics is a global leader in electric motors and large drive systems, operating from its headquarters in Nuremberg, Germany.
